That coupling qualifies the hypoxia-leg framing of Section 10.1: hypoxia can raise the iron contribution even as it lowers the oxygen-dependent sonodynamic yield, so a blanket "hypoxia protects against RSL3" is too strong. It is now wired into the spatial model (Section 10.1): enabling it raises RSL3 ferroptosis kill several-fold, including in the deep hypoxic core. That deep-core rise is a model artifact, because the default Fenton ROS term is oxygen-independent (a real anoxic core would also lose the H2O2 substrate that drives Fenton). A follow-up off-by-default option (ferroptosis-core 0.44.0, issue #383) now couples the Fenton substrate to local oxygen, and enabling it removes the deep-core rescue, confirming it was a substrate artifact (Section 10.1); the honest takeaway remains the direction, hypoxia can raise the iron contribution, not the deep-core magnitude. Like the lipid axes these knobs are uncalibrated and unused for any frozen result in this book.

[^porter2005]: Porter NA et al. Mechanisms of free radical oxidation of unsaturated lipids. Chemical Reviews (2005).
[^dodson2019]: Dodson M et al. NRF2 plays a critical role in mitigating lipid peroxidation and ferroptosis. Free Radical Biology and Medicine (2019).
[^bersuker2019]: Bersuker K et al. The CoQ oxidoreductase FSP1 acts parallel to GPX4 to inhibit ferroptosis. Nature (2019).
[^pmid32939090]: Zou Y et al. Plasticity of ether lipids promotes ferroptosis susceptibility and evasion. Nature (2020). PMID: 32939090.
[^pmid33731874]: Cui W et al. Peroxisome-driven ether-linked phospholipids biosynthesis is essential for ferroptosis. Cell Death and Differentiation (2021). PMID: 33731874.
[^pmid37267948]: Liang D et al. Ferroptosis surveillance independent of GPX4 and differentially regulated by sex hormones. Cell (2023). PMID: 37267948.
[^pmid24695223]: Mancias JD et al. Quantitative proteomics identifies NCOA4 as the cargo receptor mediating ferritinophagy. Nature (2014). PMID: 24695223.
[^pmid27245739]: Hou W et al. Autophagy promotes ferroptosis by degradation of ferritin. Autophagy (2016). PMID: 27245739.
[^pmid10963860]: Kehrer JP. The Haber-Weiss reaction and mechanisms of toxicity. Toxicology (2000). PMID: 10963860.
[^pmid28985560]: Stockwell BR et al. Ferroptosis: A Regulated Cell Death Nexus Linking Metabolism, Redox Biology, and Disease. Cell (2017). PMID: 28985560.

**Calibration constraint**: All phenotypes must show <2% death under Control (no treatment), reflecting the biological reality that even fragile persister cells are viable at baseline.

**Results** (Figure 7):

| Phenotype | Control | RSL3 | SDT/PDT |
|-----------|---------|------|---------|
| Glycolytic | 0.00% | 0.00% | 87.2% |
| OXPHOS | 0.04% | 1.1% | 99.9% |
| Persister (FSP1↓) | 1.2% | 42.5% | 100.0% |
| Persister + NRF2 | 0.00% | 0.05% | 99.5% |

**Key findings from the simulation**:

1. **RSL3 selectively kills persisters (42.5%, a model output at the Figure 7 million-cell point) while sparing glycolytic cells (0%)**. The *direction* — FSP1-downregulated persisters sensitized to GPX4 inhibition while glycolytic cells resist — is consistent with Higuchi et al. (PMID:41481741), who show that FSP1 and histone deacetylases *suppress* persister-cell ferroptosis (they are protective brakes, so inhibiting them — or the persister's own FSP1 downregulation — sensitizes the cells); the model reproduces that *mechanism* (with FSP1 down, GPX4 is the sole lipid-repair pathway, so its pharmacologic inhibition is lethal), not a specific published kill fraction. The 42.5% is the model's own number, not a measured value.

2. **SDT/PDT kills all cell types at high rates (87-100%)** because the exogenous ROS burst overwhelms GSH defense regardless of phenotype. This is an important finding: **physical ROS modalities are not inherently selective for persisters**. Their advantage over RSL3 is not selectivity but spatial confinement (focused ultrasound/light limits damage to the tumor volume). Any ICD advantage remains a biological hypothesis, not a result established by this model.

3. **The bistable switch is the critical mechanism.** Under SDT, GSH is catastrophically depleted (5.0 → 0.03 mM), which unlocks autocatalytic lipid peroxidation propagation. Cells that maintain GSH above the threshold recover; those that don't collapse irreversibly. This is qualitatively different from a linear dose-response.

4. **NRF2 compensation fully rescues from RSL3** (0.05% death, matching NRF2's known role in GPX4 upregulation) **but NOT from SDT** (99.5% death). The ROS burst from SDT overwhelms even NRF2-elevated GSH. This suggests that the NRF2 compensation failure mode we identified as the paper's primary risk may be less problematic for SDT than for pharmacologic ferroptosis inducers — a novel prediction from the simulation.

5. **Sensitivity analysis**: The qualitative result tested in the sensitivity suite — Persister > Glycolytic under SDT — holds in 22/22 parameter perturbation conditions (±50% on each of 11 rate constants), indicating robustness to parameter uncertainty for that specific comparison. A global sensitivity analysis using PRCC (Partial Rank Correlation Coefficients) with 2,000 Latin Hypercube Samples across the same 11 parameters formally ranked parameter importance. For Persister × RSL3, the LP cascade parameters dominate: lp_propagation (PRCC = +0.92), gpx4_rate (−0.87), and lp_rate (+0.80) — the death outcome is a balance between LP accumulation and enzymatic repair, with five parameters showing |PRCC| > 0.4. For Persister × SDT, a single parameter dominates: sdt_ros (+0.63, exogenous ROS dose); all other parameters have |PRCC| < 0.16. This asymmetry quantifies the manuscript's central mechanism: SDT overwhelms cellular defenses with exogenous ROS, making the death rate insensitive to the biochemical parameters that determine RSL3's outcome. A Pearson-vs-Spearman divergence check flagged 2 of 66 parameter×condition pairs (sdt_ros in two SDT conditions, reflecting a ceiling effect at high ROS rather than true non-monotonicity), below the >2 threshold for Sobol escalation. Stage 2 (Sobol indices) is therefore not warranted for this parameter set (see analysis/prcc-results.json for the full PRCC matrix across 6 conditions). As a model-qualification check beyond that escalation criterion, we nonetheless computed variance-based Sobol indices for the Persister × RSL3 kill rate (issue #331; analysis/sobol-sensitivity-report.md, scripts/sobol_sensitivity.py), screening the same biochemical rate constants over the same ranges as the PRCC (the PRCC's eleven parameters minus the SDT-only sdt_ros). They confirm the PRCC ranking: the total-effect indices rank lp_propagation (ST = 0.50), gpx4_rate (0.29), and lp_rate (0.18) as the top three, the same three the PRCC ranks for Persister × RSL3 and in the same order, together about 89% of the kill-rate variance as first-order effects. They also confirm the "Stage 2 not warranted" call from the other direction, since first-order effects explain essentially all the variance (ΣS1 ≈ 1.0), so parameter interactions are negligible and the univariate PRCC ranking was adequate. The same screen yields a practical-identifiability verdict: six of the ten screened rate constants (fenton_rate, gsh_scav_efficiency, fsp1_rate, nrf2_gsh_rate, death_threshold, gpx4_degradation_by_ros) move the kill rate negligibly (ST < 0.05), so they are not constrainable from the kill-rate observable and are flagged accordingly in simulations/calibration/parameter_provenance.md rather than presented as if data-fitted. Beyond the single-cell switch, a per-headline Morris elementary-effects screen (issue #331; analysis/headline-sensitivity-report.md, scripts/headline_sensitivity.py) extends this to a spatial/combination headline by perturbing the same rate constants through the simulation binaries themselves. For the RSL3 plus FSP1i Bliss synergy, the same LP-cascade and GSH/GPX4 constants (lp_propagation, gsh_scav_efficiency, gpx4_rate) lead the ranking, but unlike the single-cell kill rate the synergy is strongly non-additive: the Morris interaction/nonlinearity indicator (sigma) exceeds the importance index (mu-star) for every active parameter, a structure the univariate PRCC cannot see (Morris sigma does not separate interaction from single-parameter nonlinearity, but either way the effect is non-additive). Two parameters are structurally inert for this headline (sdt_ros, because no SDT acts in the pair, and rsl3_gpx4_inhib, because the combination model applies RSL3 as a fixed drug effect rather than through that parameter), so they are not constrainable from the Bliss observable. The hypoxia kill-collapse headline (the SDT-minus-RSL3 hypoxic-zone kill gap, from the spatial sim-tme model) is now screened the same way: the SDT exogenous-ROS dose (sdt_ros) is the single largest driver, the model-side confirmation that SDT's hypoxic efficacy is primarily dose-driven, which is why it survives hypoxia where RSL3 collapses. The screen is more nuanced than a pure dose story, though. Because the SDT hypoxic kill sits high (around 0.87) but is not saturated, the LP-cascade and GSH-defense constants (lp_rate, gsh_scav_efficiency, lp_propagation) also move the gap substantially, so SDT's hypoxic efficacy is dose-driven but defense-modulated, not defense-independent, consistent with the framing that the SDT advantage is real but not absolute. This screen holds SDT oxygen-independent (the optimistic upper bound of Section 10.1); the contested oxygen-dependent SDT mode is a separate knob, not one of the screened rate constants. The immune-amplification headline is screened the same way, completing all three headlines. At the canonical condition the raw immune-kill ratio matches the headline (SDT far above RSL3, roughly 104 to 1 at the default gradient-120 immune-on, the same asymmetry shown in the immune figure), because SDT's dense ferroptotic death builds a large DAMP field that amplifies immune killing far more than RSL3's sparse death. The raw immune-kill count is, however, confounded for a sensitivity screen by pool depletion, since SDT ferroptotically clears most of the tumor first, so a parameter that raises SDT's ferroptosis shrinks the residual pool the immune layer acts on, which can lower the immune-kill count even as the per-cell amplification rises. A faithful sensitivity observable therefore controls for that pool, namely the de-confounded immune kill rate (SDT's immune kills divided by the non-ferroptotic cells), which is bounded between zero and one because the immune kills are a subset of that pool. Screening that observable (from the same sim-tme runs as the hypoxia gap) puts the LP-cascade constants (lp_propagation, lp_rate) at the top, with the SDT dose close behind, so the per-cell immune amplification rides on the same ferroptosis death switch the single-cell and hypoxia screens rank: the DAMP that primes immune killing is the ferroptotic-death density, set by how readily cells tip into ferroptosis more than by the SDT dose alone. This is the informative contrast with the hypoxia gap, where the SDT dose led. SDT's hypoxic survival is dose-driven, but its per-cell immune amplification is death-density-driven, so the immune amplification is not a structurally independent axis but a downstream readout of the same biochemistry. The immune-coupling parameters themselves (DAMP diffusion, dendritic-cell activation, immune kill rate) are not in the biochemical rate-constant set screened here, so this says which biochemical constants move the amplification, not how the immune coupling is tuned. Finally, to read the single-cell death rates as intervals rather than point estimates, we propagated the same parameter uncertainty forward (issue #332; analysis/uncertainty-intervals-report.md, scripts/uncertainty_intervals.py): placing a uniform prior over each rate constant across its PRCC range and Monte-Carlo sampling the joint prior gives the prior-predictive distribution of the death rate for each phenotype and treatment. The result reinforces the order-of-magnitude reading. The pharmacologic RSL3 death rates carry very wide intervals because the bistable tipping point sits inside the plausible range (Persister × RSL3 spans roughly 2% to 100% around its 42.5% point estimate), so the RSL3 numbers are the least parameter-constrained, whereas the Glycolytic × RSL3 floor (near 0% across the whole prior) and the Persister × SDT ceiling (above 98% across the prior) are robust; Glycolytic × SDT and OXPHOS × SDT, by contrast, are wider (2.5th percentiles of roughly 4% and 68% respectively), because the lower end of the sdt_ros prior does not fully overwhelm those defenses. The NRF2-rescued persister, which the manuscript flags as the primary RSL3-resistance risk, stays resistant at the point estimate (0%) but its interval reaches roughly 38%, so even that resistance is not parameter-robust. The point estimates here are computed at the Figure 7 sample size (a million cells) so they reproduce the frozen Figure 7 values, while the intervals come from the smaller per-draw ensemble where the parameter spread dominates. This is a prior-predictive (forward) uncertainty, not a data-conditioned Bayesian posterior: turning the uniform priors into a posterior requires external drug-response data (the GDSC/DepMap calibration of issue #330). Propagating the same priors through the spatial/combination headline outputs has now begun with the cheapest such binary, the RSL3 + FSP1i Bliss synergy from sim-combo-mech (issue #332; analysis/headline-uncertainty-report.md, scripts/headline_uncertainty.py): under the same uniform priors the synergy spans a 95% prior-predictive interval of roughly 1.0× to 5.2× (median ~1.35×) around its 1.99× point estimate, so the supra-additive direction is robust at the lower 95% bound (a small tail of the prior reaches ~0.95×, i.e. just sub-additive) while the magnitude is not, consistent with Section 10.3's directional-not-quantitative reading. Propagating the priors through the two costlier sim-tme headlines has since been done at a smaller 100-draw ensemble (analysis/headline-uncertainty-tme-report.md, the same scripts/headline_uncertainty.py harness; sim-tme costs roughly four minutes per run, so the tails are read cautiously): the hypoxia kill-collapse gap (the SDT-minus-RSL3 hypoxic-zone kill) stays positive across its entire 95% prior-predictive interval, roughly 0.16 to 1.00 (median 0.96, around its 0.87 point estimate), so the collapse-asymmetry direction (SDT holds, RSL3 collapses) is parameter-robust even though its magnitude is wide; and SDT's pool-de-confounded immune kill rate spans roughly 0.009 to 0.171 (median 0.042), robustly low-but-positive. The penetration headline has likewise been propagated (analysis/headline-uncertainty-penetration-report.md): perturbing the same biochem priors at each tissue's fixed (uncalibrated) transport scenario, the vessel-wall RSL3 kill carries very wide intervals at every tissue (well-vascularized median 23%, 95% interval roughly 0 to 93%; poorly-vascularized median 8%, 0 to 83%; CNS/BBB median 4%, 0 to 77%), because the bistable RSL3 switch dominates as it does for the single-cell rates, but the across-tissue ORDERING (well-vascularized > poorly-vascularized > CNS/BBB) is preserved within every draw. The ordering is robust as a within-draw paired comparison rather than from the marginal intervals (which overlap almost completely because the biochem prior, not the tissue, dominates the spread): each draw applies the same biochem rate constants to all three tissues at their decreasing fixed vessel-wall concentrations, a shared monotone dose-response, and the well-vascularized >= poorly-vascularized >= CNS/BBB ordering held in 300 of 300 draws. So the penetration-gradient direction is parameter-robust while the absolute per-tissue magnitudes are not. With that, all four headline families have been read as prior-predictive intervals over the documented rate-constant ranges. The data-conditioned posterior has since been computed for the in-vitro single-cell switch: a joint multi-inducer ABC (issue #500, `analysis/calibration/joint-posterior.md`) conditions the shared switch on two inducer mechanisms at once (the GPX4 inhibitor ML162 and the System Xc- inhibitor erastin, both from CTRPv2) and reports credible intervals rather than point estimates (lp_propagation 95% CrI roughly 0.44 to 0.98, lp_rate roughly 0.37 to 0.97) that generalize to a held-out GPX4 inhibitor (ML210, 5 of 7 points inside the 95% predictive band). Crucially, that in-vitro posterior lies entirely above the in-vivo PRCC priors that drive the spatial and headline intervals, so it conditions only the in-vitro switch magnitudes. One qualification belongs with that sentence, because it is easy to read as stronger than it is (issue #PRIOR-PROVENANCE): those "in-vivo PRCC priors" are not independently measured in-vivo values but ±50% perturbations of the default parameters themselves (`scripts/run_prcc.py` sets the ranges that way, and `lp_rate`'s [0.03, 0.12] is exactly ±50% of its 0.06 default). So the disjunction states that the in-vitro fit falls outside the defaults' own neighbourhood, which restates the falsification rather than supplying an independent reason to discount it. The numerical disjunction is real; what it does not establish is that the defaults are correct in a regime no measurement has tested. The honest position is that the one leg anchored to independent data rejects the defaults by eleven-fold RMSE, no independent in-vivo measurement of these rate constants exists here, and the spatial and headline numbers therefore rest on parameters that are unfalsified because untested in their own regime rather than because a test has cleared them (`analysis/calibration/in-vivo-prior-provenance.md`); the in-vivo and spatial headline numbers cannot be conditioned on in-vitro data and remain prior-predictive, which is why conditioning those still requires an in-vivo ferroptosis dataset that maps onto these dimensionless headlines — one that does not yet exist publicly (the in-vivo ferroptosis readouts that do exist, e.g. the IKE pharmacokinetic course used for tumor-PK calibration and in-vivo SCD1/MUFA data, measure different observables that do not condition the headline outputs). These per-headline verdicts are now consolidated into a single machine-checked accounting (`analysis/identifiability-report.md`, issue #503, guarded by `tests/test_identifiability_report.py`): with eleven free rate constants, six of them non-identifiable from the kill rate, and zero of the headline outputs conditioned on data in the regime that produces them, no headline magnitude is point-estimable. The single-cell kill rate and the immune ratio are directional-only; the Bliss synergy, the hypoxia asymmetry, and the penetration gap are direction-robust but magnitude-uncalibrated. So every reported magnitude should be read as order-of-magnitude or directional, exactly as this book labels it.

[FIGURE 7: Monte Carlo simulation results. Ferroptosis death rate by cell phenotype and treatment (n=1M cells/condition), as a single panel of bars with **no error bars**. The caption previously described error bars carrying prior-predictive 95% intervals and a second panel with a sensitivity tornado plot; the shipped figure has neither. Read the intervals from `analysis/uncertainty-intervals-report.md` instead, where they are computed and committed — and read them, because they are wide: the Persister-under-RSL3 bar shown here at 42.5% spans roughly 1.6% to 99.7% across the prior. The sensitivity result the second panel would have shown is stated in the text above as 22/22 conditions holding.]

**Correction to Figure 7's error bars.** An earlier version of this figure reported a "95% CI" computed from the n=1,000,000 simulated cells. At that sample size the binomial interval is roughly ±0.1%, so it measured only how long the simulation was allowed to run, and reporting it alongside a point estimate implied a precision the model does not have. The parameter uncertainty is three orders of magnitude larger: for Persister × RSL3 the prior-predictive interval spans 1.6% to 99.7% around the 42.5% point estimate, and even the NRF2-rescued persister, reported at 0.0%, reaches 37.8%. The figure now carries the prior-predictive interval, which is the one a reader should use. Neither interval captures the stochastic run-to-run variation, which is reported separately in `analysis/seed-replication-report.md`; a magnitude needs both before it can be quoted.

### 5.3 Where the Numbers Come From

The simulation uses approximately 30 parameters. Some are drawn from published experimental measurements: GSH concentrations in cancer cells, GPX4 catalytic rates, the Michaelis-Menten constants for antioxidant scavenging, and the physical attenuation coefficients for light and ultrasound in tissue. Others are estimated from indirect evidence: FSP1 repair rates, NRF2 upregulation kinetics, the rate at which exogenous ROS generates lipid radicals, and the stochastic variation range for each parameter.

Each cell in the simulation receives its own parameter set, drawn independently per parameter from a **normal** distribution about the population mean, with a coefficient of variation between 10% and 40% depending on the parameter and phenotype (`gen_cell` in `ferroptosis-core/src/cell.rs`); each draw is floored at a physiological minimum. This reflects the biological reality that no two cells are identical — even genetically identical cells in culture show measurable variation in protein expression and metabolic state. An earlier version of this paragraph described the draw as "±20% uniform", which is wrong in all three respects that matter: the family is Gaussian rather than uniform, the 95% spans run from about ±20% to ±78% rather than ±20%, and — the consequential one — the support is UNBOUNDED rather than bounded. Under a bounded draw a cell outside the box is impossible, so a death rate reported as zero would be exactly zero; under the Gaussian draw every threshold retains a positive-probability tail, and a rate reported as zero is an upper bound set by the sample size. By running 1,000,000 cells per condition the simulation produces stable population-level outcomes, though as Section 5.2 records, the interval that matters for these numbers is parametric rather than sampling.

**Resolving the zeros.** Because that support is unbounded, a rate this chapter reports as 0% is a statement about the sample size rather than about the biology, and it can be pushed down simply by simulating more cells. We did so, sweeping n from 1,000,000 up to 100,000,000,000 for the three conditions sitting at or below the resolution limit (`scripts/rare_event_sweep.py`, Figure 29, `analysis/rare-event-findings.md`). The result separates cleanly into two kinds. For **Glycolytic under Control** and **Glycolytic under RSL3**, no death occurred at any scale tried: a hundred billion cells each, and the 95% upper bound falls to 3.69e-11 and 3.69e-11 respectively, from 3.69e-06 at the million-cell point. Read against burden, fewer than about 4 cells in a hundred-gram population would be expected to die under either condition. But the bound is exactly 3.689/n at every scale — the upper end of the exact two-sided 95% Poisson interval at zero events, -ln(0.025)/n — and being pinned to the sample size that way makes it a property of the sample and not of the model. (It is not the rule of three, which is the familiar one-sided approximation 3/n, 23% lower; both fall one decade per decade of n, which is why the shape argument holds for either.) these two conditions remain **resolution-limited**, and what has been established is a limit, not a value. The selectivity constraint the thesis rests on — that RSL3 spares glycolytic cells — now holds to one part in 27.1 billion rather than one part in 271,000, and it is still a bound. **PersisterNrf2 under Control** behaves differently and is the informative case. At the million-cell sample it recorded exactly one death, a point estimate of 1.0e-06 carrying an exact Poisson interval spanning a factor of 220. At a hundred billion it records 72,722 deaths, a rate of 7.272e-07 with an interval spanning a factor of 1.01 ([7.22e-07, 7.33e-07]). This is a measurement rather than a bound, and it carries a caution worth stating plainly: the single-event estimate was 38% high, and only its interval — not its point estimate — contained the answer. Three limits bound all of this. The parameters are the in-vivo defaults, which carry zero calibration targets, so these are facts about the model's tail and not predictions about a patient. A hundred billion independent cells is not a hundred-gram tumor, because these cells never interact; the defensible phrase is a population the size of that burden, sampled as independent cells. And the samples are nested by construction — cell *i* is seeded from its global index, so each larger run contains every smaller one — which makes the successive estimates positively correlated rather than six independent confirmations.

Eight constraints are checked against the model, and it matters what kind they are. All 8 are **self-consistency** checks in `simulations/calibration/targets.yaml` — 8 of 8 carry `target_type: self-consistency` and none carries `target_type: calibration`. They regression-guard the model against its own previously reported behaviour; they are not measurements the model was fitted to, and passing them is not evidence the model matches reality. The constraints are that all phenotypes show less than 2% death under Control; that RSL3 selectively kills persister cells; that the vulnerability window closes on the expected timescale; that the in-vivo MUFA protection factor holds; that PDT attenuation and SDT penetration behave as the optics and acoustics equations say they should; and three 3D checks on oxygen collapse, immune dominance and stromal shielding. An earlier version of this paragraph called these "five calibration constraints [that] anchor the model to reality". They were relabelled in the calibration documents (#589, whose commit message records that `targets.yaml` "now correctly holds 0 calibration + 8 self-consistency") and the relabel did not reach this chapter. The legs of this work that ARE anchored to independent data are named in Section 10.4 and in `simulations/calibration/CALIBRATION_STATUS.md`; none of them is in this list.

### 5.4 What the Model Cannot Do

This is a qualitative model testing directional predictions, not a quantitative predictor of clinical outcomes. The absolute percentages (87%, 42%, etc.) depend on model-specific rate constants and should not be interpreted as clinical predictions. The model's value is in demonstrating that the proposed biochemical chain produces the expected differential sensitivity under biologically plausible parameters, and in identifying which conditions favor which outcomes.

Specific limitations the reader should keep in mind throughout Chapters 7 and 8:

The model does not include upstream signaling — iron import, System Xc- cystine transport, and ACSL4-mediated lipid remodeling are not represented in the 2D engine these chapters use. (The library has since grown an uncalibrated `contact` layer that models the cell-cell-contact downregulation of two of these, ACSL4-driven PUFA remodeling and TFRC iron import, via E-cadherin/NF2-YAP signaling; it represents those two axes at reduced levels, not the import or remodeling dynamics, and is not used for any result in this book. See "The 3D simulation suite" in Section 10.4.) It does not model adaptive evolution — there is no mutation, no selection over generations, no population dynamics beyond the single 180-step treatment window. Spatial drug distribution is handled separately in Chapter 7's spatial simulation, not in the baseline model. Immune response is handled separately in Chapter 8's immune coupling layer.

All fields (oxygen, pH, drug concentration) are steady-state — they are computed once based on distance from the tumor edge, not dynamically updated as cells consume resources or die. In a real tumor, blood flow fluctuates, metabolites are consumed and replenished, and the microenvironment evolves over hours to days. The steady-state approximation captures the spatial gradient but not the temporal dynamics.

The model is a hypothesis-testing tool, not a digital twin of a tumor. It identifies which biological conditions matter for ferroptosis sensitivity, which parameter combinations produce interesting outcomes, and which assumptions are load-bearing. The experiments proposed in Chapters 7 and 8 are designed to test whether those assumptions hold in real biology.

[FIGURE 29: How far down the death-rate tail a given sample size can see. Several conditions report exactly 0% at one million cells; because the support is unbounded, such a rate is a statement about the sample size rather than about the biology, and it can be pushed down by simulating more cells. The curve is the resolution floor, not a result.]

## Chapter 6: The Multi-Modality Engine

> The engine modelled one mechanism in depth and everything else not at all. This chapter is the record of widening it to nine treatment arms, what the comparison between them is worth, and where the widening bought less than it appears to.

### 6.1 Why This Chapter Exists

Chapter 5 describes an engine that models one mechanism in depth. That is a fair description of what this project has been for most of its life, and a fair criticism of a repository whose front page invites the whole cancer community. This chapter is the answer, and it is a measurement rather than a defence.

The criticism was quantified before it was addressed. `analysis/scope-audit.md` counts what the committed work is about — 20 analyses whose subject is ferroptosis or the physical-ROS modalities against one for any other therapy, and 8 of 20 preregistered predictions concerning ferroptosis or the physical-ROS modalities. `analysis/modality-coverage.md` asks the sharper question: not what the work is about, but what the engine can be ASKED. Of the sixteen mechanisms in this project's taxonomy that carry a discriminative MeSH descriptor, thirteen had no engine representation at all, and they carried 90,019 census articles between them.

That column is now empty. What follows is what filled it, what the filling cost, and what it did not buy.

### 6.2 What Was Added, and Why the Mechanisms Were Not Independent

Nine treatment arms exist where three did (Figure 25). The order they arrived in was derived from the coverage measurement rather than chosen, and the striking thing about building them is how often one made the next one possible.

**Ionizing radiation** came first, and not because it is the largest literature. The engine's oxygen anchor for it had already landed — the Alper–Howard-Flanders enhancement ratio, adopted when the sonodynamic work showed the engine's linear O₂ term was the wrong functional shape — so the hardest part was done. It is modelled as TWO channels rather than one, because its dominant lethal lesion is the DNA double-strand break and not membrane lipid peroxidation. A linear-quadratic survival model runs beside the ferroptosis engine without passing through it, and radiation-induced ferroptosis enters separately through the existing exogenous-ROS path. Collapsing them would have modelled radiation as a photodynamic agent, which would predict that a GPX4-competent cell is radioresistant. It is not.

**Synthetic lethality existed as an arm only because radiation did.** PARP inhibitors block base-excision repair of the single-strand breaks ionizing radiation produces; before the engine had a DNA-damage channel there was nowhere for them to act. It is also the arm whose free parameter published data most tightly constrains, for a reason worth stating: the sensitizer enhancement ratio is dose-dependent under the linear-quadratic model when only the single-hit term moves, so one parameter must satisfy the published 1.2–1.7 band across the surviving fractions it samples (2.24 to 9.38 Gy) at once. Only a fifth of the searched range does.

**Checkpoint blockade** needed one thing the engine had never had — antigen presented without ferroptotic death. Before it, every activation path was gated on dying cells: the well-mixed cascade multiplies the count of them, and the spatial model reads a signal field written as lipid peroxidation at death. Anti-PD-1 enters only through a brake term, so it multiplied a structurally zero quantity at every setting. The engine carried a four-axis checkpoint panel, a suppressor field, T-cell exhaustion and dendritic-cell subsets — machinery any reader would call an immune model — and none of it could be applied.

**Redirected effectors** — CAR-T and bispecific engagers — arrived together, because they differ in how the effector arrives and not in what it does. Both bypass dendritic-cell priming, which is the point of the modality. They separate on persistence: a transferred population expands, a redirecting drug washes out. The checkpoint brake applies to both, and that is a claim rather than a convenience, since it is the documented reason the leukaemia results have not transferred to solid tumours. Figure 28 draws that separation as a waterfall, and the shape is the reason it earns a figure: the same construct collapses 633-fold between the two diseases and NO SINGLE STEP looks catastrophic. Three barriers at plausible values leave six per cent, exhaustion removes most of what is left, and the antigen ceiling — drawn even though it does nothing here, because it is a cap rather than a coefficient — contributes exactly one. A model fitting a single efficacy scalar would reproduce the same endpoint and lose the reason, which is what tells a reader which step to attack.

**Oncolytic viruses** needed no new module. The virus lyses cells and that lysis is immunogenic, so the lysed cells feed the same immunogenic-death chain ferroptotic deaths feed — and that shared chain is the only route in the model to the modality's systemic effect, responses at lesions that were never injected.

**Physical ablation** — thermal and electrical — is the one group that does not route through the cell-state model at all, and the reason is a distinction this project had already argued in prose. Ablation is a THRESHOLD, not a dose-response. Above it everything dies; below it essentially nothing does, and no amount of exposure changes that. The interesting quantity is the position of the margin, which makes recurrence after ablation a geometry problem rather than a resistance problem — the one place in this engine where the hypoxia and penetration work cannot help.

**Delivery** turned out to be a modality of its own. Nanocarriers and antibody-drug conjugates change how a payload arrives and nothing about how it kills, so both are transport profiles rather than treatment arms, and the nanocarrier reproduced the field's own tension without being told to: encapsulation protects the payload and slows its clearance, but a hundred-nanometre carrier diffuses an order of magnitude more slowly, and the net effect on penetration is negative. Better accumulation, worse reach.

### 6.3 What the Panel Shows, and What It Refuses To

`sim-modality-panel` runs every applicable arm against the identical tumour from the identical seed, so a difference between two rows is the arm and nothing else (Figure 26). Nine treatment arms and an untreated baseline, 7 distinct routes to death, and only 3 of the arms through the ferroptosis engine. The baseline used to be counted as a fourth: it runs inside the ferroptosis loop with no treatment applied and inherited that loop's label, so a table about which arms need ferroptotic death was counting one that kills nothing.

**It is not a ranking of therapies, and the artifact says so on every surface a reader can reach.** Each row prints its own calibration tier, and all but radiation's DNA channel are placeholders whose kill fractions are functions of parameters they were given. What the panel shows is structural: which arms need ferroptotic death and which do not, which are dose-responses and which are thresholds, and which are limited by delivery rather than by biology.

The sharpest row is the last of those. `AntibodyDrugConjugate` kills 1.84% against sonodynamic therapy's 87.24%, both driven by the same exogenous-ROS constant through the same engine. What differs is that the payload arrives on a 150 kDa antibody whose binding-site barrier holds its penetration to about seven micrometres. This manuscript has argued since the penetration work that delivery dominates the in-vitro-to-in-vivo gap; that row is the first place in the repository where one exogenous-ROS setting runs through two delivery routes and the transport factor is the only difference between them. **And the comparison is closer to arithmetic than to a discovery, which is worth saying rather than letting the number carry more than it can.** The two arms differ in the transport factor and in nothing else: the ADC arm is the SDT exogenous-ROS constant multiplied by the concentration at each cell's distance from the vessel. So the ratio measures what this model's transport layer does, not what an antibody does in tissue. It also does NOT run RSL3's pharmacology — `biochem::exo_ros` applies the GPX4-inhibition branch only to `Treatment::RSL3` — and an earlier version of this paragraph said the two share the payload's pharmacology exactly, which was false against the code. The honest same-payload comparator sits in the same table and points the other way: the free drug carrying the actual RSL3 payload kills 0.00% at this phenotype, LESS than the antibody-delivered arm, because the glycolytic state is where a GPX4 inhibitor has almost nothing to act on.

::: finding
Run every applicable arm against the same tumour from the same seed and the antibody-delivered arm kills a fraction of what the sonodynamic arm kills, with the identical payload constant driving both. The gap is the transport factor and nothing else, which is what makes it worth reporting: it is the first place in this work where one payload runs through two delivery routes.
:::

::: refusal
That ratio prices this model's transport layer. It does not measure an antibody in tissue, and it is arithmetic more than it is a discovery — the two arms were built to differ in transport, so they do. The free drug carrying the actual payload kills less than the antibody-delivered arm in the same table, which is a property of the cell state the panel runs on, not a recommendation about either.
:::

Two rows are identical, and that is stated rather than hidden. Sonodynamic and photodynamic therapy agree to the last digit because this panel is depth-free and their exogenous-ROS parameters are equal by default. They are not the same modality — the entire physical-modality argument of this work turns on light dying in millimetres while ultrasound reaches centimetres — but the thing separating them is a geometry, not a mechanism, and it lives in the depth comparison rather than here.

### 6.4 Fitting, and the Arm That Would Not Fit

Naming a target is what this project's layer-freeze policy requires before a new axis lands. Fitting to it is what turns a layer from present into calibrated. `analysis/modality-calibration.md` attempts that for every arm with a published target and reports 4 outcomes that are not the same result: a value that reproduces the target from a NARROW window, a value that reproduces it from most of the search range, NO value at all because the target constrains a product of factors neither of which is identifiable from it, and no published target to fit in the first place. An earlier version of this sentence counted four and listed three, having dropped the inadmissible case.

4 of 7 are admissible, and the width of each admissible window is printed beside it. A fit admitting most of the range has been given a target that cannot discriminate, and calling that calibrated is the same error as reporting a p-value without an effect size.

**The most valuable rows are the three that failed** (Figure 31), and the sharpest of them is checkpoint blockade. Checkpoint blockade is inadmissible. With no ferroptotic death the cascade collapses to a product, so the published response band constrains antigen presentation multiplied by kill rate, and neither factor is identifiable from it alone. Even at full antigen presentation the arm reaches 10.3% against a 20% floor, and the presentation fraction that would close the gap is 1.94 — impossible for a quantity bounded by one.

That is the same finding Chapter 9's identifiability analysis makes about the ferroptosis engine's rate constants, arriving independently in the immune layer. It is worth more than the 4 arms that fitted: a flexible form hitting its target is weak evidence, while a form that cannot is reporting something nobody put in by hand.

3 of the 7 targets are clinical, and no amount of fitting makes an objective response and a lattice kill fraction the same quantity. A partial response is a thirty-percent diameter reduction — roughly two-thirds by volume — and a responding patient is not a dead tumour. Each clinical row carries that mapping explicitly, because it is the weakest link and the thing a reader skims past.

### 6.5 What the Microenvironment Does to Them

The panel asks what each arm does to a naive tumour. The question this project actually exists to ask is what the tumour does back, and every arm the campaign added was untested against the resistance axes Chapter 8 establishes. That is the difference between an engine that can express a modality and one that has something to say about it.

`sim-modality-panel --tme-sweep` runs the full factorial — hypoxia, stromal shielding, acidic pH, depth and clonal heterogeneity, across two cell states — through the SAME helpers the ferroptosis work uses, so no arm receives a mechanism-specific adjustment. A modality comparison means nothing if the environment is not identical.

**Which axis bites depends on the cell state, and that is the finding** (Figure 27). The axis that moves each cell state most is not the same one, and the DIRECTION is half the finding: in the glycolytic state it is acidic pH, a loss of 100% for `AntibodyDrugConjugate` — tied exactly with depth, so which of the two is named is arbitrary; in the persister state it is depth, a loss of 99% for `AntibodyDrugConjugate`. This sentence has now been wrong three times, and the third is a different fault from the first two. It was correct when written and went stale in place: the committed sweep artifact stopped matching what the code produces, and the guard comparing this sentence to that artifact passed because the two were stale TOGETHER. Regenerating moved the persister row from a 124% gain to clonal heterogeneity to a 99% loss to depth. What follows is the record of the first two. It first read "hypoxia (77%); clonal heterogeneity (121%)", where the axis was wrong and 121% was the retracted absolute-value artefact. The correction fixed the digits and left the operation: the field behind it was still `max(abs(v))`, so it called a GAIN a *pressure* and broke an exact tie by the order of a hard-coded list. Changing a number is not the same as changing what produced it. An earlier version of this sweep ran one phenotype and reported two axes inert — correctly for that run, and misleadingly, because what was inert was the configuration's ability to see them. At the glycolytic state a delivered drug kills essentially nothing, so ion trapping has nothing to scale and an antioxidant buffer is swamped by an order-of-magnitude ROS insult. In the persister state, which is the state this project's thesis is about, both bite hard. An axis reported inert is a statement about the run and not about the biology.

Under the axes that bite, all nine arms rank AntibodyDrugConjugate (gains 124%), RSL3 (gains 116%), PDT (loses 100%), SDT (loses 77%), AdoptiveCell (loses 56%) and so on — and the ordering was not tuned for. A delivered drug is most exposed, because everything between the vessel and the target can stop it. Light dies fastest with depth, losing 100% where megavoltage photons lose 1% (Figure 27; Figure 30 draws the underlying reach, where PDT falls 92.5% to 0.6% over 9.4 mm against radiation's 45.4% to 43.7%), which is the manuscript's tissue-access argument appearing inside a resistance sweep rather than beside one. An arm whose lethality depends on oxygen comes next; an arm whose dose is merely modified by oxygen moves less, exactly as an enhancement ratio implies. A threshold arm does not move at all, because a destroyed cell does not care about any of it — and reporting that as robustness would be circular.

**One axis can help rather than hurt, and it helps the arm that is failing.** Clonal heterogeneity raises the pharmacologic arm's kill from an acid-suppressed baseline, and the mechanism is not subtle: widening the antioxidant setpoint while holding its mean fixed supplies a low-glutathione tail that dies even when the average cell resists. Variance rescues a marginal drug. An earlier version of this analysis could not have reported that, because it took the absolute value of every change and called the result a loss — which produced the impossible line that an arm had lost 121 per cent of its kill. Collapsing the sign did not merely mislabel a number; it hid a result, since an axis that can help is not the same kind of thing as one that only hurts. Figure 27 is drawn on a signed scale for exactly that reason: a magnitude colour map paints the axis that helps the same shade as the axis that nearly erases an arm, which is the absolute-value error one layer out.

**And the immune amplification is a count effect rather than a quality effect.** Sonodynamic therapy does generate far more immune kills than the pharmacologic inducer, and this measures why: DAMP release per death is 20.0 against 17.7, a ratio of 1.13. The two death modes differ modestly in quality; what differs is how many cells they kill. The first version of that measurement read lipid peroxidation at the moment of death, which returns approximately the death threshold for every arm by construction — death IS the crossing — so the difference vanished entirely. It had not vanished; it was being measured before it happens, and the spatial binaries read the post-grace-period value for exactly this reason. A quantity that comes out equal for every arm, suspiciously close to a threshold the model itself defines, is not a measurement of anything.

### 6.6 Radiation, in the Form It Is Actually Given

Every arm above was added at the same depth: enough mechanism to be selectable, a calibration verdict, and an honest note that the numbers are placeholders. This section does something different with one of them, and the reason is that radiotherapy is the arm where this project's usual excuse does not apply. Elsewhere the model is uncalibrated because the data does not exist. Here it exists, it is randomised, and it has existed for forty years.

**What is known.** Radiation kills by breaking DNA, and the dose-response of that killing is the best-characterised quantitative relationship in oncology: the linear-quadratic model, `S = exp(-alpha*D - beta*D^2)`, whose two terms are one-track and two-track damage. The ratio `alpha/beta` is the dose at which the two contribute equally, and it differs between tissues -- conventionally about 10 Gy for a tumour and about 3 Gy for the late-responding normal tissue that limits what can be given. That difference is not a detail. It is the entire reason radiotherapy is delivered in thirty small fractions rather than one large one: dividing the dose spares the low-ratio tissue more than it spares the tumour.

Around that sit the four Rs, which is how the field has organised the subject since the 1970s. Between fractions a cell **repairs** sublethal damage, which is why fractions six hours apart are not the same as fractions a day apart. Surviving clonogens **repopulate**, and after a lag of roughly four weeks they do so fast enough that each further day of treatment costs real dose. Cells **redistribute** through the cell cycle into more sensitive phases. And the tumour **reoxygenates** -- the hypoxic cells that survive a fraction because oxygen was not there to fix the damage come within reach of a vessel once the cells in front of them have died.

**What the engine had.** One fraction. `radiation.rs` carried the linear-quadratic survival, an `alpha` implied by a published parameterisation, the oxygen enhancement ratio in the saturating form radiobiology measures, and a soft-tissue attenuation coefficient that makes radiation the one physical modality in this book with no depth limit. All of that is the physics of a dose. None of it is radiotherapy, because nobody receives a dose: they receive a course.

::: numbers
The layer added here: a `Schedule` -- fractions, dose per fraction, hours between them, and the calendar the course runs over. Biologically effective dose `BED = nd(1 + d/(alpha/beta))` and its clinical form `EQD2`. The Thames incomplete-repair factor for fractions closer together than repair can keep up with. Withers' accelerated repopulation, which charges nothing until its lag has passed. A reoxygenating hypoxic compartment coupled to the oxygen enhancement ratio the engine already had. And the therapeutic ratio: tumour effect per unit of late-tissue effect, which is the quantity the whole design of a schedule is trying to maximise.
:::

**How it was checked, and why the check is unusual for this repository.** The layer's external test runs backwards. A randomised fractionation trial publishes two schedules and reports whether the outcomes differed; if they did not, the linear-quadratic model implies the `alpha/beta` at which the two are equivalent, through arithmetic that knows nothing about what tissue it is describing. That implied value can then be set against `alpha/beta` estimates the radiobiology literature derives from entirely different data. Two numbers out of a trial protocol predict a third quantity nobody supplied.

::: finding
CHHiP randomised 74 Gy in 37 fractions against 60 Gy in 20 for prostate cancer and found the shorter schedule non-inferior. Those four numbers imply an `alpha/beta` of 2.29 Gy -- inside the low band the literature independently estimates for prostate, and far below the ~10 Gy a generic tumour is assigned. The low prostate ratio is the observation that motivated hypofractionating prostate cancer in the first place, and here it falls out of two schedule descriptions rather than being put in.

The second prediction is arithmetically simpler and no less free to fail. The dose each further treatment day costs once repopulation has begun is `ln2/(alpha*T_p)`. With an `alpha` from a survival parameterisation and a doubling time from a repopulation study -- two constants adopted for other reasons -- it comes to 0.77 Gy per day, inside the published band Withers reports. Neither number was chosen to make it land there.
:::

::: refusal
The breast leg fails, and it is the more informative of the two. START-B compared 40.05 Gy in 15 fractions against 50 Gy in 25 and found the shorter schedule non-inferior; the implied `alpha/beta` is 0.70 Gy, far below any plausible tissue and far below the 2.3 to 4.5 Gy the same trial programme estimates. The reason is visible in the total doses: the shorter arm delivers five fewer Gray and was still not inferior, so no positive `alpha/beta` makes the two EQD2-identical. Under the linear-quadratic model alone that trial's result is not reproducible. That is a statement about what EQD2 leaves out -- the arms also differ by two weeks of elapsed time -- and not about the trial.

Three further limits belong with it. A non-inferiority result says two arms did not differ by more than a margin, not that they were equal, so the implied ratio inherits the width of that margin and is reported as an anchor rather than as an estimate with an interval. The repair half-time is uncalibrated: published values span roughly 0.5 to 4 hours, so the incomplete-repair correction is direction-only and the six-hour interval it makes an argument about is a clinical convention rather than a fitted result. And the reoxygenation half-life is a free parameter that no dataset in this repository constrains, so what panel (c) of Figure 32 shows is a shape, not a size.
:::

**What it means, and what it changes about the rest of this book.** Three things.

The first is that the model now expresses the trade-off it was previously blind to. Run the tumour alone and it prefers one large dose: the quadratic term grows with fraction size, so 60 Gy delivered at once kills far more than 60 Gy in thirty pieces. That conclusion is wrong, and the engine could not say why until the late-responding tissue was in it. With both, the therapeutic ratio falls monotonically as fractions grow -- 1.00 at the conventional 2 Gy, 0.70 at 7.25 Gy, 0.46 for a single 60 Gy dose -- and the preference reverses for the reason the clinic gives.

The second is the fourth R, which lands on an argument this book has been making since Chapter 8. The hypoxic compartment is the reason pharmacologic ferroptosis collapses in the tumour core, and Chapter 8 treats it as a fixed shield. Under fractionation it is not fixed: the same total dose in thirty fractions with reoxygenation kills roughly thirty times more than it does with the hypoxic fraction frozen. The comparison is uncalibrated in magnitude, but its existence qualifies the earlier chapters -- a resistance mechanism measured under a single exposure may be a smaller obstacle to a modality that returns tomorrow.

The third is the honest one. This is the deepest arm in the book after ferroptosis, and it took a schedule struct, thirteen functions and thirty-three tests to get there. `analysis/arm-parity.md` reports what that bought and what it did not: the arm is still a small fraction of the ferroptosis engine, and every other arm is smaller. Radiation was chosen to go first precisely because its literature would carry a real external check; the arms that follow will not all have one, and where they do not, this book will say so rather than substituting a plausible number.

[FIGURE 32: The schedule, and the two things it can be checked against. Panel (a) is the external check, running backwards: two schedules a trial reported as not differing imply the alpha/beta at which they are equivalent, and that value is compared against estimates derived from other data (shaded bands). Prostate lands inside its band at 2.29 Gy; breast crosses at 0.70 Gy, below any plausible tissue, because its shorter arm delivers less total dose and was still not inferior. Panels (b) and (c) are direction-only: the late-responding alpha/beta is a convention and the reoxygenation half-life is a free parameter no dataset here constrains.]

### 6.7 Chemotherapy, and the Cell Cycle the Engine Did Not Have

The previous section took the arm with the best external data. This one takes the arm that had nothing at all, and the gap it fills is the most embarrassing sentence this project can write about itself: until now, an engine built to compare cancer therapies could model a laboratory ferroptosis inducer with no clinical use in several thousand lines of code, and could not model cisplatin.

That is the sharpest form of the criticism this chapter answers. It is not that ferroptosis is over-served. It is that the comparator was missing.

**What is known.** Cytotoxic chemotherapy is the oldest systemic treatment and the one most patients receive, and its quantitative logic rests on three ideas. The first is log kill: a given dose kills a constant FRACTION of a population rather than a constant number, so the arithmetic of cure is about reducing a burden by orders of magnitude rather than by an amount. The second is that the drug classes are distinguished by WHEN they act. Alkylators and platinums damage DNA whatever a cell is doing; antimetabolites act on cells synthesising it; taxanes and vinca alkaloids act on the mitotic spindle. The third is the constraint: the dose-limiting toxicity of most regimens is myelosuppression, which is why a schedule exists at all and why the answer to "how much" is never "all of it".

**What the engine had.** No cell cycle. Not a simplified one -- none. Every treatment in the engine acted on a redox state, and a drug class whose defining property is which phase it catches its target in could not be expressed, because there were no phases.

::: numbers
The layer added here: a population-level distribution over G1, S, G2/M and quiescent G0; a per-class sensitivity for each phase; log kill applied phase by phase and weighted by how much of the population is in each; the distribution left BEHIND after a dose, which is not the one it started with; Gompertzian regrowth between cycles; redistribution back toward the resting profile during the gap; a marrow nadir and recovery, with the shortest interval that clears a chosen threshold; and an efflux factor for the drug that never reaches a lethal concentration inside the cell.

It is a POPULATION-level cycle, deliberately. There is no per-cell cycle position, because adding one would touch every module in the engine and move results that byte-identity gates protect. What that buys is phase specificity, the residue it leaves and redistribution between doses. What it forecloses is a synchronised population and any scheduling argument that depends on one.
:::

**How it was checked, and the answer is: less than any other arm here.** The repository's dose-response route is CTRPv2 through the DepMap download interface. It reached the five ferroptosis compounds this project fetched years ago and it reaches nothing now -- the catalogue endpoint answers with a verification page rather than the documented file, which the calibration-feasibility work had already recorded as a problem of access rather than of method. So the target for a cytotoxic dose-response is public, has been used by this project before, and is out of reach today. The calibration ledger records that as NO TARGET with the reason attached, because saying the literature has no number would be false and saying the arm is fitted would be worse.

What survives is two predictions that need no fitted potency, and both are free to come out the other way.

::: finding
The first is what a phase-specific agent leaves behind. Because it can only reach the cells in its phase, at the same dose and potency it leaves roughly sixteen times as many survivors as a phase-nonspecific agent in a proliferating population. That much is close to a restatement of the sensitivity table. The second half is not: in a population that is mostly out of cycle the gap NARROWS to about six, because the phase-nonspecific agent is struggling too. The phase-specific agent's disadvantage is a property of how much of the tumour is dividing, not a constant of the drug class, and nothing in the model was arranged to produce that.

The second is the dose-density question Norton and Simon raised and CALGB 9741 tested: at the same total dose, does shortening the interval kill more? The model says yes, in a WINDOW. Between roughly 0.002 and 0.1 per day of Gompertz regrowth the shorter schedule is worth at least a tenth, peaking near fivefold. Outside it, nothing.
:::

::: refusal
The window has two ends and only one of them was expected. With no regrowth the advantage disappears -- there is nothing to outrun -- and the model in fact reports the longer schedule very slightly ahead, because the extra week lets survivors redistribute back into sensitive phases. That was not designed in either. But the advantage also vanishes when regrowth is FAST, because then both schedules' tumours return to the Gompertz plateau between cycles and the extra week costs nothing that was not already lost.

**This is not a reproduction of CALGB 9741 and must not be read as one.** The trial found a shorter interval better in early breast cancer; the model produces a window in units of a Gompertz rate constant. Whether breast micrometastatic disease sits inside that window is not something this repository can determine, and asserting it would be exactly the move this book spends its other chapters refusing. The trial is why the question is worth asking. It is not evidence that the answer is right.

Everything else here is a placeholder. The potency, the phase fractions, the redistribution rate, the marrow timings: none is fitted, so no absolute number this arm produces should be carried anywhere. And the panel's chemotherapy row inherits all of it -- it is drawn with the phase-nonspecific class because a table has one row per arm, which means the row's height is a statement about an alkylator and not about chemotherapy.
:::

**What it means.** The immediate consequence is that the comparison this book is for can now be made at all: the panel in Section 6.3 runs chemotherapy against the same tumour, from the same seed, as ferroptosis induction and the seven other arms. It is worth stating plainly that this changes nothing about which treatment is better -- the panel refuses that reading, and an uncalibrated arm cannot support it -- but the absence of the row was itself a claim, and it was a false one.

The second consequence reaches backwards. This engine has modelled drug-tolerant persisters since long before it modelled a drug they tolerate: `persister.rs` exists because the ferroptosis literature is full of cells that survive treatment and become GPX4-dependent, and the treatment they were surviving was chemotherapy. The pressure was missing and only the escape was modelled. The phase-based residue this section adds is a second, independent route to the same shape -- a surviving population enriched for the states the drug could not reach, without a single mutation anywhere in it.

And the third is a limit that will show up again. Cytotoxic chemotherapy has no row in this project's mechanism taxonomy, for the same reason radiotherapy has none: no discriminative MeSH descriptor. So the arm that reaches the most patients is one the census cannot count and the parity table reports as `no row`. The engine can now express it; the instrument that measures the literature still cannot see it.

[FIGURE 33: Chemotherapy's two structural predictions, neither needing a fitted potency. (a) Dose-response by class in a proliferating population, on a log axis: the phase-nonspecific agent falls away while the phase-specific agents flatten, because raising the dose cannot reach a cell that is not in the phase. (b) The residue each phase-specific class leaves relative to a phase-nonspecific agent at the same dose, in a proliferating population and in one that is mostly out of cycle -- the second pair is the result that was not designed in, because the gap NARROWS when fewer cells are dividing. (c) The dose-density window: at the same total dose, the burden ratio between a 21-day and a 14-day schedule, against the Gompertz regrowth rate. The advantage has an interior peak and vanishes at both ends. No dose-response here is fitted -- the CTRPv2 route is access-blocked -- so every absolute value is a placeholder and only the shapes are results.]

### 6.8 Checkpoint Blockade, and What a Ratio Can Constrain

The two previous sections took arms that had a module and made them deeper. This one takes the arm that had none. Checkpoint blockade reached the comparison entirely through machinery it shares with adoptive transfer, oncolytic virus and the ICD chain -- machinery the depth audit credits to no arm, correctly, because several arms reach through it. So the modality with the largest immunotherapy literature in this project's taxonomy owned zero lines of engine.

It also carried the worst calibration verdict in the book, and the reason is worth restating because it is the section's whole subject.

**What is known.** Checkpoint inhibitors release a brake rather than killing a cell. The T cell does the killing; the antibody stops the tumour from switching it off. Two things therefore have to be true at once for a response: the tumour must be visible -- enough neoantigen presented on intact machinery -- and the brake must be releasable. Those are different properties with different biomarkers behind them, and the clinic treats them as different: mutational burden and mismatch-repair status speak to visibility, PD-L1 expression to the brake.

The failure modes are similarly separable, and the field names three. Primary resistance is a tumour that was never visible. Adaptive resistance is a tumour that raises its own brake in response to the interferon its attackers produce -- the reason PD-L1 measured before treatment predicts less well than its biology suggests. Acquired resistance is the loss of machinery: JAK1 or JAK2, so the tumour cannot respond to interferon at all, or B2M, so it cannot present anything to anybody.

**What the engine had, and why its calibration failed.** A single brake scalar inside a shared cascade. With no ferroptotic death the whole thing collapses to a product -- kill equals a coefficient times antigenicity -- so a published response rate constrains that product and neither factor separately. `analysis/modality-calibration.md` reported the arm INADMISSIBLE for exactly this reason, and added a second objection that is arguably worse: an objective response is a thirty per cent reduction in tumour diameter, and a kill fraction is a fraction of cells killed. Those are different quantities, and no fit makes them the same one.

::: numbers
The layer added here: receptor occupancy, so a dose has a saturating relationship to effect rather than an efficacy scalar; PD-L1 as a modifier of the BRAKE and mutational burden as a modifier of ANTIGENICITY, kept apart because keeping them apart is the point; interferon-driven PD-L1 induction, which a tumour that has lost its interferon response cannot perform; a classifier for the three resistance modes; and a dimensionless response index with a ratio function beside it.
:::

**How it was checked, and this is the part worth reading.** The mapping objection is fatal to any comparison of absolute numbers, and it cannot be argued away. But if the observed response rate is any linear function of the model's kill, then a ratio of two response rates from the same trial, the same drug and the same endpoint cancels the unknown constant exactly. The mapping does not become defensible. It becomes irrelevant to the comparison.

KEYNOTE-158 supplies such a pair. In previously treated advanced solid tumours, pembrolizumab produced objective responses in 29% of the tumour-mutational-burden-high group and 6% of the rest -- a ratio of 4.8, or conservatively between 2.6 and 7.8 taking the confidence intervals at their endpoints. Mutational burden moves antigenicity and, to first order, not the brake, so that ratio is a statement about the shape of the antigenicity response and nothing else.

::: finding
The model returns 3.1 at its shipped constants: inside the band, below the point estimate. Scanning the two parameters that set the shape of the antigenicity curve, the measured band admits 31% of the grid.

That is a real constraint where there was none. The absolute response rate excluded no shape at all, because it constrained a product containing a factor nobody can measure. The ratio excludes roughly two thirds of them. The arm moves from INADMISSIBLE to partially constrained on one axis, and the axis it constrains is the one the trial actually stratified.
:::

::: refusal
It identifies nothing. One ratio is one equation and there are two unknowns; the brake is exactly where it was, and the second stratified observable that would constrain it -- response by PD-L1 score within a single trial -- is not in this analysis.

The limit that matters more is smaller and more embarrassing. The trial reports strata, not tumours: to compute a ratio the model needs a representative burden on each side of a ten-mutations-per-megabase threshold, and no trial makes that choice for us. Varying it across defensible pairs moves the model's answer from 1.5 to 7.3 -- a spread comparable to the width of the target band itself. So the comparison constrains a shape in the sense that most shapes fail it, and it would be dishonest to call the surviving region small.

And the first-order assumption behind the whole argument is not testable from this trial. If mutational burden also moves the brake -- through interferon signalling, through the immune infiltrate a mutated tumour attracts -- then it is not a clean antigenicity stratification and the ratio is measuring something more complicated than the model says it is.
:::

**What it means.** Three things, and the third is about the rest of the book.

The immediate one is that this arm now has a target it can fail, which is the difference between a model and an illustration. The structural check beside it is stronger than the numerical one: a tumour that has lost B2M is unresponsive at any mutational burden, the model returns exactly zero for it, and the ratio against such a tumour is undefined rather than infinite. That is a property of how the layer is built, not a fitted result, and it is the kind of thing a calibration exercise usually cannot supply.

The second is methodological and it generalises past this arm. Several of this book's clinical mappings have the same defect -- a model quantity and a clinical endpoint that are not the same thing -- and the ratio construction is a general way to make progress without pretending the defect is gone. Any two strata of one trial that differ in a factor the model represents will do it. The cost is that ratios are noisier than rates and that a stratification which moves two factors at once is worse than useless.

The third is the honest accounting. Checkpoint blockade enters the parity table with a module for the first time, and it remains the smallest of the arms that have one. The calibration ledger now records a constrained shape and an unidentified brake rather than a single word, which is more information than it carried before and still much less than a fitted layer would need.

[FIGURE 34: What a ratio can constrain that an absolute response rate cannot. (a) The model's antigenicity response saturates with mutational burden, with the two KEYNOTE-158 strata marked; a trial can refute the RATIO between them, and cannot refute either height, because the mapping from a model kill to a radiological response is unknown. (b) The region of shape parameters the measured band admits -- 31% of the grid scanned -- with the shipped constants marked. (c) The limit, drawn at the same size as the result: the trial reports strata rather than tumours, and the representative-burden choice moves the model's answer across most of the target band.]

### 6.9 Adoptive Cell Therapy, and a Failure More Dose Cannot Fix

The three arms before this one each gained something the engine could not previously express. This one gains a distinction, and the distinction is the interesting part: it separates two failures that look identical in an outcome table and behave completely differently under the one intervention a clinician can most easily make.

**What is known.** A chimeric antigen receptor gives a patient's own T cells a new specificity, and in B-cell leukaemia it works about as well as anything in oncology: tisagenlecleucel produced remission in 81% of children and young adults with relapsed or refractory B-cell ALL within three months. In solid tumours the same idea has produced almost nothing. The field's account of why is a list of barriers -- the cells must traffic to the tumour, infiltrate it, and stay activated inside it -- and each barrier is a fraction, so their product is small.

But not every failure is a barrier. A CAR needs a minimum density of target molecules on a cell to trigger lysis at all; below that density the cell is not killed slowly, it is not killed. That is a different kind of failure, and it explains a clinical observation the barrier account cannot: relapse with antigen still present but reduced. And the cells that do the work are not the cells that were infused -- they expand, sometimes a thousandfold, driven by the antigen they then consume, which is why peak expansion tracks tumour burden and why the same expansion that produces a response produces the cytokine syndrome that limits the dose.

**What the engine had.** Three barriers, a persistence factor and an antigen ceiling, all multipliers. A product of multipliers has exactly one shape: whatever it takes away, more effectors take back. That is a fair model of a delivery problem and a poor model of this therapy.

::: numbers
The layer added here: an antigen-density threshold that caps what any dose can reach; antigen-driven expansion with a host ceiling and a memory compartment that persists rather than contracting away; a cytokine burden that scales with expansion and tumour burden together, and the largest dose that keeps it under a tolerance; and a dose-escalation function that reports what escalating actually buys.
:::

**How it was checked, and the answer is: not against a number.** This arm has a famous number available and does not use it. A remission is not a kill fraction -- the same objection that made the checkpoint arm's response rate unusable as a target. There, the fix was a ratio between two strata of one trial, where the unknown mapping cancels exactly. It does not transfer here: blood and solid CAR-T are different trials, different diseases and different endpoints, so no constant is shared, and a ratio between them would measure the difference between two protocols as much as between two biologies. The calibration ledger records that the anchor was not used, which is more informative than a fit would have been.

::: finding
What the layer predicts instead is a separation. Take two tumours with the SAME barriers and the same poor outcome. One fails because too few effectors arrive; the other because the effectors that arrive cannot engage what they find. Escalate the dose tenfold and the first improves tenfold while the second improves by less than a per cent.

That is an experiment, not a citation. It says that a failure which looks like insufficient dose and a failure which looks like insufficient dose can be told apart by escalating the dose -- and that in one of the two cases, the escalation everybody reaches for first cannot work in principle.
:::

::: refusal
Nothing here is fitted. The density threshold is a placeholder and a poor one: real thresholds vary by orders of magnitude with the receptor's affinity, its costimulatory domain and the target, and no dataset in this repository constrains any of them. The expansion rates, the host ceiling and the cytokine scale are placeholders too.

The prediction is about experiments that have not been run in this form. No published series known to this project has escalated dose in cohorts matched for antigen density, which is what would test it directly.

And the threshold is sharper in the model than in a patient. Antigen density varies from cell to cell within one tumour, so a real failure is a mixture of the two modes rather than one of them -- which means the clean separation above is the limiting case of a messier reality, and the experiment would see a blend.
:::

**What it means.** The immediate consequence is a change in what the arm can be asked. Before, every question about CAR-T failure had the same answer -- some product of barriers was too small -- and the only lever was more cells. Now the model can say that more cells is the wrong lever, and say when.

The second reaches back to Chapter 8. That chapter treats resistance as a set of microenvironmental pressures that scale a kill down. The density threshold is not that: it is a property of the target rather than of the environment, and it is binary in a way none of the pressures are. A book that models resistance only as attenuation will keep recommending more of whatever it was already giving.

The third is about this campaign. Adoptive cell therapy is now the fifth arm with a section of its own and the smallest engine of the five, and its calibration entry says that no target was used. That is the least satisfying outcome any of these arms has produced, and it is the accurate one.

[FIGURE 35: What escalating the dose buys, and when it buys nothing. Two tumours with identical barriers and identical poor outcomes: one delivery-limited, with antigen density well above the threshold a receptor needs, and one density-limited, with the same infiltration and sparse antigen. A tenfold dose increase multiplies the first by ten and the second by less than one per cent. The engagement curve behind it is a threshold rather than a gradient, which is what makes the two failures different in kind rather than in degree. Nothing here is fitted: the density threshold, the expansion rates and the cytokine scale are all placeholders, and the separation is a prediction about an experiment rather than a reproduction of one.]

### 6.10 Oncolytic Virus, and a Tension That Names Its Own Condition

Every review of oncolytic virotherapy states the same tension in its second paragraph. The virus kills by lysis, and an immune response clears the virus before it can spread, so immunity is the enemy. But the durable benefit is the anti-tumour immunity that the lysis primes, so immunity is also the mechanism. The field states this and moves on.

Stating it settles nothing, because it does not say which side wins. And the question it fails to settle is the consequential one: whether suppressing a patient's immune system to let the virus work is a good idea. That is a decision somebody has to make.

**What is known.** Talimogene laherparepvec is approved for advanced melanoma and produced a durable response rate of 16.3% against 2.1% for the cytokine comparator in its registration trial. It is injected into the lesion, it replicates in cells whose interferon response is defective, it lyses them, and the lysis releases both virus and tumour antigen. Responses have been reported in uninjected lesions, which is the observation that makes the immune arm more than a bonus -- a virus that never reached a lesion cannot have lysed it.

**What the engine had.** A race between replication and clearance, resolved into a threshold ratio, and one immune term. With one immune term the tension cannot be represented at all: whatever the term does, it does in one direction.

::: numbers
The layer added here: a basic reproduction number and, from it, the speed of a spreading infection front -- the Fisher-KPP result, which is a different equation from the bistable front the ferroptosis engine already solves, and the two sit side by side because an infection has no ignition threshold while a peroxidation wave does. An entry-receptor threshold, the same shape as the CAR-T antigen threshold and for the same reason. And the two arms of the bind as separate terms: virus surviving to spread, which falls with immune competence, and anti-tumour priming, which rises with it and is multiplied by the lysis that did the priming.
:::
