**What it predicts, and the form of the prediction is the point.** The two terms are individually monotonic. Their sum need not be, and whether it is depends on whether the primed response kills more than the lysis it cost. The model computes where that crossover sits.

::: finding
Below a priming efficiency of about 2.6 in this model's units, the optimum is at full immune suppression: the virus should be allowed to spread and the durable arm is not worth what it costs. Above it, the optimum moves into the interior, and suppressing immunity throws away more than it buys.

So the clinical intuition is conditionally right, and the condition is nameable. That is a different and more useful claim than either "immunity helps" or "immunity hurts", and it is not built in -- a model that produced an interior optimum at every setting would be asserting the conclusion instead of deriving it, which is why the crossover is computed rather than assumed.
:::

::: refusal
The crossover's position is not a measurement. Priming efficiency is a placeholder in units this model invented, and nothing in this repository constrains it; what the analysis supports is that a crossover EXISTS and what it depends on, not where a real virus sits relative to it.

The trial cannot fix that. OPTiM's 16.3% against 2.1% is a real contrast between two arms of one randomised trial, and it still cannot anchor this model -- because they are two different AGENTS. The ratio construction that rescued the checkpoint arm needs two strata of one treatment, where the mapping from a model quantity to a clinical endpoint is shared and cancels; between a virus and a cytokine nothing is shared, and the ratio would measure two mechanisms rather than one mechanism at two settings. The anchor is direction-only and the ledger says so.

And a third of the scanned range saturates. At high priming efficiency the modelled outcome reaches its ceiling, the scan returns the lowest competence that gets there, and that reads as a boundary optimum while meaning the opposite. Those rows are marked and excluded; mixed in, they make the two regimes interleave and the crossover look unstable. Finding that was worth more than the crossover itself.
:::

**What it means.** The narrow consequence is that this arm can now be asked a question with an answer that depends on something: given a virus whose priming efficiency is estimated, should the patient be immunosuppressed? Before, the model had one immune term and the answer was whatever that term's sign happened to be.

The broader one is a pattern this chapter keeps producing. The chemotherapy arm found a dose-density window with an interior peak; the checkpoint arm found that a ratio constrains what a rate cannot; this one finds a crossover. In each case the useful output was not a number but a CONDITION under which a clinical intuition holds -- and in each case the model could produce it only because two effects with opposite signs were represented separately rather than collapsed into one coefficient. That is a design lesson this book can offer even to a reader who trusts none of its parameters.

[FIGURE 36: The double bind, and the condition that decides it. (a) The two arms as separate terms: virus surviving to spread falls with immune competence, anti-tumour priming rises with it. Neither is non-monotonic. (b) Their sum is, but only sometimes -- with weak priming the best outcome is at full suppression, with strong priming it is in the interior. (c) Where the switch happens, with the saturating regime marked in grey because there the outcome has hit its ceiling and the optimum's position no longer reflects the trade-off at all. The crossover's existence is the claim; its position is in units this model invented and nothing here constrains it.]

### 6.11 Antibody-Drug Conjugates, and an Optimum Nobody Chose

Of everything in this chapter, this section comes closest to what a calibrated model is supposed to look like. The inputs are two numbers somebody else measured. The output is a design optimum, and it lands where the field settled without anything here being tuned to put it there.

**What is known.** An antibody-drug conjugate is a targeting molecule with a cytotoxic payload attached, and its central design parameter is how much payload to attach. The obvious answer is as much as possible: each antibody that reaches a tumour cell delivers more drug. The obvious answer is wrong, and it is wrong for a reason that is measurable rather than theoretical -- a more heavily conjugated antibody is cleared from circulation faster, so fewer of them arrive.

Hamblett and colleagues measured exactly that in 2004, on one antibody at three loadings. The eight-loaded conjugate cleared three times faster than the four-loaded one and five times faster than the two-loaded one. In a dish the eight-loaded conjugate was the most potent of the three. In an animal, at equal antibody dose, the four-loaded one matched it.

**What the engine had.** A payload with a delivery problem, a bystander term and an antigen ceiling. Nothing that could be asked how much payload to attach, because loading was not a quantity the model had.

::: numbers
The layer added here: clearance as a function of drug loading, anchored on the two measured ratios; delivered payload per unit antibody dose, which is loading divided by that clearance; in-vitro potency, which rises with loading because in a dish nothing is cleared; and the binding-site barrier -- a higher-affinity antibody is captured by the first antigen it meets and never reaches the cell behind, so affinity buys retention at the cost of penetration.
:::

**What came out, and the shape of the reasoning matters more than the number.** Delivered payload is loading over clearance. Both factors are monotonic in loading, so neither on its own suggests an optimum. Their ratio peaks at a drug-antibody ratio of four -- which is where the field settled for this payload class, and which nothing in this model was arranged to produce.

::: finding
The optimum is emergent, and the route to it went through a mistake worth recording. A single power law in loading, fitted to the measured five-fold ratio between DAR 8 and DAR 2, predicts a 2.24-fold ratio between DAR 8 and DAR 4 against a measured three. It cannot pass through both.

The two ratios together say the clearance penalty ACCELERATES: loading costs less than proportionally between two and four, and more than proportionally between four and eight. That acceleration is precisely what makes an interior optimum exist. The first version of this layer used the smooth single power law, and it had no optimum at all -- the curve fell monotonically and the model would have recommended the lightest possible loading. A curve chosen for tidiness removed the result before it could appear.

The model also reproduces the disagreement the same paper reports: in vitro, potency rises monotonically with loading; in vivo, delivery peaks and then falls. Same molecules, opposite ordering, both halves measured in one experiment. This book argues about the in-vitro-to-in-vivo gap in almost every chapter, usually with only one side of it available.
:::

::: refusal
Two ratios do not determine a curve. The interpolation between them is log-linear within each measured segment because that is the simplest form passing through both, and outside the range from two to eight nothing is measured -- the extrapolation is there to make the function total, not because it means anything.

One conjugate, one payload, one antibody. The optimum at four is a property of this study's molecule, and newer conjugates with different linkers and more stable chemistry are deliberately built at higher loading. A model that reported four as the answer for antibody-drug conjugates in general would be overreaching by exactly the distance between one experiment and a field.

And delivered payload is not efficacy. The layer converts it to neither a kill fraction nor a response, and the binding-site barrier beside it is direction-only: the form has the right sign and the right limits and is fitted to no measured penetration depth.
:::

**What it means.** The narrow result is that this arm can now be asked a design question and give an answer that depends on data. The broader one is about the argument this chapter has been making by accumulation.

Five of the six arms taken deeper here produced the same shape of finding: a quantity with an interior optimum or a crossover, arising from two effects with opposite signs that a single coefficient had previously collapsed into one. Fractionation trades tumour kill against late-tissue damage. Dose density trades regrowth against redistribution. The oncolytic bind trades viral spread against primed immunity. Drug loading trades payload against clearance. In each case the model could produce the finding only because the two effects were represented separately, and in each case a model with one lumped parameter would have reported a monotonic answer and recommended more of something.

That is not a result about cancer. It is a result about modelling cancer, and it is the most transferable thing this chapter has to offer.

[FIGURE 37: An optimum that falls out of somebody else's measurement. (a) The two clearance ratios measured by Hamblett 2004, with the piecewise interpolation through both and the single power law that misses one of them by a third. (b) Delivered payload per unit antibody dose -- loading divided by clearance -- with its peak at a drug-antibody ratio of four, which nothing here was tuned to produce and which is where the field settled for this payload class. (c) The in-vitro and in-vivo orderings disagreeing: potency rises monotonically with loading in a dish where nothing is cleared, while delivery peaks and falls in an animal. Delivered payload is not efficacy, and one conjugate is not all conjugates.]

### 6.12 Ablation, and a Failure With an Address

This arm carries the chapter's worst calibration verdict, and the verdict is correct. Its target is a temperature threshold; a threshold is very nearly binary; almost every parameter value in the scanned range reproduces it. The fit did not fail through carelessness. It failed because the observable carries almost no information about the parameter, and no amount of fitting recovers information that is not there.

So this section does something different: it stops trying to fit the arm and asks where the model's failures are instead.

**What is known.** Thermal ablation destroys tissue by heating it past a lethal thermal dose, and the destruction is not a matter of temperature alone -- a brief exposure at a lethal temperature is not lethal, and a long exposure just above the reference is. The clinical failure is characteristic and it has an address: tumours next to large vessels recur more often, because flowing blood carries heat away and the tissue beside the vessel never reaches a lethal dose. Perivascular local progression after radiofrequency ablation is a recognised problem for that reason.

Irreversible electroporation is not thermal. It kills by permeabilising membranes with an electric field and deposits little heat, so a heat sink removes nothing that matters to it. That is the documented reason it is reached for near vessels, and it makes the two modalities fail in different places.

**What the engine had.** A thermal dose, a threshold and a coverage fraction. Everything inside the applicator field dies; the survivors are however much of the margin was missed. That model has no geometry in it beyond a percentage, and a percentage cannot say where anything is.

::: numbers
The layer added here: perfusion cooling as a function of distance from a vessel, the peak temperature that follows from it -- floored at body temperature, because a heat sink cannot cool tissue below the blood doing the cooling -- and the radius of the surviving perivascular sleeve, obtained by walking outward from the vessel until the thermal dose becomes lethal. Plus the same radius for electroporation, which is exactly zero.
:::

::: finding
The model returns a sleeve of about four and a half millimetres for a fifty-degree applicator, falling to half a millimetre at ninety. It never closes.

That is a different KIND of statement from a coverage fraction. A fraction says a percentage of the margin survived and is silent about which part; a radius says the survivors are in a ring around a vessel, at a scale a clinician can look for on the follow-up imaging. And beside it sits a contrast that is structural rather than fitted: electroporation's sleeve is zero at every temperature, so the two modalities should fail in geometrically different patterns.
:::

::: refusal
The size is not a result. The cooling length is a placeholder standing in for vessel calibre and flow rate, neither of which this layer represents, and the sleeve scales with it almost proportionally -- so the millimetres are a restatement of that assumption rather than a prediction about tissue.

What is anchored is the direction: perivascular tumours progress locally more often after thermal ablation. No sleeve radius is published in the source that supports it, and none is fitted here.

And one row of the model's own table is not a sleeve at all. An applicator too cool to reach a lethal dose even far from any vessel fails everywhere, and the scan limit it returns reads as a very wide perivascular ring while meaning the ablation did not work at all. Those rows are marked and excluded -- the same defect, in the same shape, that the oncolytic section had to fix in its own table two sections earlier.
:::

**What it means.** The narrow point is that an arm whose parameters cannot be identified can still make a checkable claim, provided the claim is about structure rather than magnitude. Where the failures are is not a parameter.

The broader point is about what the calibration ledger is for. This arm will keep its UNCONSTRAINED verdict, because that verdict is about a specific attempt -- fitting a threshold to a threshold -- and the attempt genuinely failed. Recording it as anything else would be dishonest, and adding the spatial prediction does not repair it. What the ledger now carries is both: a failed fit and a structural claim, side by side, which is a more accurate description of what this arm knows than either would be alone.

[FIGURE 38: Where a thermal ablation fails, and where electroporation does not fail. (a) Blood flowing in a vessel holds nearby tissue near body temperature, so the peak temperature rises with distance from the vessel rather than with distance from the applicator. (b) The resulting surviving sleeve as a radius rather than a coverage percentage -- shrinking as the applicator gets hotter and never closing -- against electroporation's zero, which is structural rather than measured because it is not a thermal modality. The coolest applicator fails everywhere rather than leaving a sleeve, and is marked as such. (c) Why the size is not a result: the cooling length stands in for vessel calibre and flow, which this layer does not represent, and the sleeve scales with it almost proportionally.]

### 6.13 Photodynamic and Sonodynamic Therapy, the Two the Project Started With

This chapter has worked outward from ferroptosis through seven modalities the project adopted later. It ends by turning back to the two it was built on, and they were the last to be modelled -- which needs an explanation, because it looks like neglect and is not.

**What is known.** Photodynamic therapy is the mature one. It has been approved and in clinical use for decades, its photosensitizers are characterised drugs with published pharmacokinetics, and its limitation is famous: light does not go far into tissue. Sonodynamic therapy is the young one. It substitutes ultrasound for light on the argument that sound penetrates centimetres where red light manages millimetres, and its literature is thin -- roughly thirty papers intersecting ferroptosis in the whole indexed cancer literature, against about a hundred and fifty for photodynamic therapy. This project's own thesis leans on the younger of the two.

**What the engine had, and why that was deliberate.** It had one death switch for both, and identical parameters feeding it. Sonodynamic and photodynamic runs in this engine were bit-identical across every phenotype, which one of the repository's own audits reported as a defect: a contrast between them compares a modality with itself.

The audit is right about the fact and wrong about the cause. The identity is a validated design decision. Zhu and colleagues measured the reacted-singlet-oxygen dose that necroses tissue in vivo for three chemically unrelated photosensitizers and found it approximately source-independent -- around half a millimolar, within a factor of 1.8 across all three. The dose-to-kill is a property of the target, not of whatever made the reactive oxygen. Building two death thresholds would have contradicted a measurement.

::: finding
That is the observation this section is built on, and it is sharper than the audit that provoked it. **If the kill threshold is genuinely source-independent, then every difference between these two arms must live upstream of the cell.** Not in what the oxygen does on arrival -- in how much of it arrives. The engine already carried one such difference, in depth attenuation. Everything else was missing, and the identity in the panel was the symptom.

So the last two arms in the chapter are the only ones where the work was not "build a mechanism" but "find out where the difference had to be, and go there".
:::

**What was simulated, on the sonodynamic side.** Ultrasound does not deliver energy the way light does. Halve the photons and you halve the singlet oxygen; halve the acoustic pressure and, below a point, you get nothing at all, because the dominant sonochemical route is inertial cavitation and a bubble either collapses violently or it does not. The likelihood is governed by peak rarefactional pressure over the square root of frequency -- the mechanical index every clinical scanner displays.

That puts sonodynamic therapy in the small set of arms in this chapter whose limit is a threshold rather than a multiplier, alongside the CAR-T antigen density, the conjugate's receptor and the oncolytic entry receptor. It is why its depth limit is a hard edge where the photodynamic one is an exponential fade.

Three frequency dependences then act at once, and two of them oppose. A diffraction-limited focus shrinks with wavelength, so at fixed acoustic power a higher frequency concentrates the beam and raises focal pressure. Attenuation rises with frequency, so a higher one loses more on the way in, and the loss compounds with depth. And the index itself divides by the square root of frequency, penalising high frequency a third time. The product has an interior maximum, and it has a closed form: the best frequency falls as one over the attenuation coefficient and one over the depth.

::: numbers
At the engine's own attenuation of 0.7 dB/cm/MHz, the model puts the optimum at 1,241 kHz for a five-centimetre focus, 620 kHz at ten, and 414 kHz at fifteen.
:::

The threshold then buys a statement a coverage fraction cannot make, and it is the sonodynamic counterpart of the ablation section's perivascular sleeve: **a depth beyond which no setting helps.** Not a fading efficacy -- a hard edge, past which raising the exposure time, the pressure or the frequency changes nothing, because the bubble either collapses or it does not.

::: numbers
Deepest focus still clearing the cavitation threshold, at each of three applicator strengths and three device frequency caps:

| applicator | 2 MHz cap | 5 MHz | 20 MHz |
|---|---|---|---|
| weakest | fails everywhere | fails everywhere | 0.40 cm |
| weak | 1.15 cm | 1.65 cm | 1.65 cm |
| moderate | 10.45 cm | 10.45 cm | 10.45 cm |
| strongest | beyond the 30 cm scan | beyond the 30 cm scan | beyond the 30 cm scan |

Two of those cells are not measurements and are marked rather than printed as numbers. An applicator that cannot cavitate at the surface fails *everywhere*, and rendering its zero as a shallow limit is the degenerate row the oncolytic and ablation sections each shipped once. A limit equal to the scan's own bound never found a limit at all, and thirty centimetres would read as an extraordinarily deep applicator when it measures how far the scan went. The first version of this table shipped the second one.
:::

::: refusal
Neither the threshold height nor the applicator strengths are measured. Published in-vivo inertial-cavitation thresholds move by more than an order of magnitude with nucleation, and this layer represents no nucleation at all, so the shape of the table is the result and no row is a recommendation.

The frequency cap is different, and it is the one input here that is a real property rather than a placeholder. Without it this model is *unbounded* at shallow depth: nothing has attenuated yet, and the focusing term rewards frequency without limit. An early version of the figure hid that divergence by starting its axis at half a centimetre, which is hiding a model's artifact in a picture rather than fixing it in the model.
:::

**And here the chapter gets its first refutation.** Ellens and Hynynen asked the same question by a route with nothing in common with this one -- full-wave acoustic propagation into a bioheat solve, over simulated transducer arrays of up to seventy thousand elements -- and named the same three mechanisms in almost the same words. Two of the model's claims survive the comparison. An interior optimum exists; their whole study presumes one and finds one. It moves down as attenuation rises; they report 750 kHz falling to 500 when their attenuation coefficient doubles, and the model predicts an exact halving.

The third does not. This model says the optimum should fall roughly threefold between fifty and a hundred and fifty millimetres. They report the same 750 kHz at all three depths.

::: refusal
That is a disagreement, not a tuning gap: no value of the attenuation coefficient makes a one-over-depth law flat. And the missing term is one they name themselves -- near-field heat accumulation, which their conclusion calls the rate-limiting factor for large-volume ablation. This model evaluates a single point and has no representation of the tissue in front of the focus at all. Lowering the frequency to chase the focal optimum also lengthens the heated path, and a model that cannot see that path is free to slide its optimum in a way a real applicator is not.

Two further mismatches are why no numerical agreement is claimed in either direction. Their observable is thermal ablation efficiency and this model's is cavitation likelihood, and absorption enters those two with opposite sign -- it helps heating and hurts cavitation -- so the two optima should not coincide even for a perfect model of both. And their attenuation is quoted on the pressure amplitude in nepers per metre while this engine's is a decibel figure applied to intensity, a conversion carrying a factor of two that is the easiest unit error in acoustics to make. A match obtained after picking one of two defensible conversions would be a statement about the choice.
:::

**What was simulated, on the photodynamic side.** Photodynamic therapy is prescribed as a total light dose in joules per square centimetre, and for most of this engine's life the *rate* at which that light arrives did not exist as a quantity: the photosensitizer was sampled once, at the moment illumination began. That treats a ten-minute and a two-hour delivery of the same joules as the same treatment.

They are not, and this is the one arm where the field says so loudly. A Type II photosensitizer works by handing energy to ground-state oxygen, so the illumination consumes its own substrate at the site it is treating; Henderson and Busch report depletion within *seconds* at seventy-five milliwatts per square centimetre in murine tumours. The practical consequence -- the same total light delivered more slowly producing a greater response -- is the fluence-rate effect, and an oxygen-blind model has no way to express it.

::: numbers
The layer added here is two terms and no new machinery. Tissue oxygen at the illuminated site falls as one over one plus the rate over a critical rate, the point at which photochemical consumption matches perfusive resupply. The per-photon yield then follows the Alper-Howard-Flanders hyperbola -- the same oxygen curve the radiation arm uses, deliberately, so the engine's two oxygen-dependent modalities cannot drift apart for no better reason than which module was written first.

Against that sits the module's own pharmacokinetics, integrated across the illumination window instead of sampled at its start. Sampling once is exactly the approximation that makes a long treatment look free.
:::

::: finding
Two monotonic effects in opposition give another interior optimum of exactly the shape this chapter keeps finding -- the same one as fractionation, dose density, oncolytic spread and conjugate loading, and as the sonodynamic frequency two pages back. Faster depletes the oxygen; slower runs further into the drug's own clearance.

**The result is a statement about drugs rather than doses.** The optimal fluence rate falls monotonically as the sensitizer clears more slowly: about 315 milliwatts per square centimetre for a fifteen-minute half-life, 66 at four hours, 18 at forty-eight. A fast-clearing sensitizer should be illuminated fast and a slow-clearing one slowly, and the two differ by more than an order of magnitude. Nothing in this repository tests that, and it is registered as a prediction rather than reported as a result.
:::

::: refusal
The *position* of that optimum is not a finding. It rides on the critical fluence rate, which nothing here measures, and the section says how hard rather than merely conceding it: a twentyfold span in the parameter moves the optimum 3.66-fold, an exponent of 0.43. The optimum sits on roughly the square root of an unmeasured quantity, so the milliwatt figures above are a restatement of an assumption and the *ordering* is not.

The oxygen model is also quasi-steady and site-local, where real photochemical depletion is transient and coupled to a vasculature this layer does not see. And the clinic bounds the slow end while the model does not: a two-hour illumination is a scheduling fact as much as a physical one, so wherever the model's optimum falls below what a treatment room tolerates, the binding constraint is not in the model at all.
:::

**What it means for the thesis.** The two founding modalities now differ in the engine, and they differ in a way that says something. Photodynamic therapy's limitation is a *rate* -- something a clinician can lower, at the cost of time. Sonodynamic therapy's is a *threshold* -- something no exposure time crosses. That distinction is the honest version of the depth argument the project was founded on: sound really does reach further than light, and reaching further is not the same as delivering enough once you are there.

It also leaves this chapter with something none of the other eight sections produced: a prediction an independent study contradicts, with a named missing term. That is a better outcome than another confirmation. Eight arms agreeing with their comparators would say more about how the comparisons were chosen than about the engine.


[FIGURE 39: An optimum the model finds, and a scaling an independent study contradicts. (a) Three frequency dependences act at once and two oppose -- focusing favours going high, while attenuation and the mechanical index's own inverse square root both favour going low -- so the product peaks in between. (b) The disagreement: this model's optimum falls threefold across fifty to a hundred and fifty millimetres, crossing the near-flat 750 kHz an independent full-wave study reports at all three depths. No attenuation coefficient makes a one-over-depth law flat, so it is a refutation rather than a tuning gap, and the missing term is one that study names itself. (c) Why this arm is structurally unlike the dose-response arms: inertial cavitation is a threshold, so below the line no exposure time produces anything and the depth limit is a hard edge. The threshold height is a parameter, not a measurement.]

[FIGURE 40: Photodynamic therapy consumes the oxygen it needs, so the same light delivered faster does less. (a) The two opposing monotonic effects drawn separately: oxygen depletion punishes speed, sensitizer clearance punishes slowness. (b) Their product, an interior optimum whose position MOVES with the drug's half-life -- which is the registered prediction, since it is a statement about drugs rather than doses. (c) The refusal: that position scales as roughly the square root of the critical fluence rate, a quantity measured nowhere in this project, so the ordering is the result and the milliwatt figures are a restatement of an assumption.]

### 6.14 Radiation in the Spatial Engine, and an Effect the Formula Could Not Have

Chapter 6 has, until this point, been a chapter of closed-form functions. Every arm built for it takes a few numbers and returns a few numbers, evaluated at a point. The ferroptosis engine those arms are measured against is nothing of the sort: it is an agent-based simulation over a grid of eighty thousand cells, with an oxygen gradient, a vessel network, an immune layer, subclones and drug-tolerant persisters. That is the difference the parity table did not measure, and when it was finally asked, the answer was three of ten -- for seven arms the spatial engine returns a literal zero and the run is bit-identical to an untreated tumour.

This section is the first arm lifted across, and radiation went first for the reason it went first before: the oxygen machinery it needs was already built, already calibrated, and already waiting.

**What was known, and what the project had promised itself.** The oxygen effect is the oldest quantitative result in radiobiology. A cell starved of oxygen needs roughly two and a half to three times the dose to be killed as well as an oxygenated one, and the ratio is called the oxygen enhancement ratio. The engine has carried the measured shape of that effect since the layer that adopted it, and the calibration ledger has carried an unusual admission ever since:

> This layer ships with no independent failable prediction yet. The one it will have is the oxygen enhancement ratio a full run *exhibits*, which is emergent and needs the binary wiring.

That sentence was written before the work in this section and is the target it is scored against. It also names the trap. The engine already contains a function returning 2.86, and that function is documented and tested as a *restatement* of the published hyperbola -- reporting it against the published band would be a guard computing its own expectation, which is a mistake this project has made and had to retract elsewhere.

::: numbers
What was wired: radiation's DNA-damage channel, applied per cell, once, at its own step. Each living tumour cell's dose is attenuated, then scaled by the oxygen effect at *that cell's* local oxygen supply -- the same per-cell field that gates the sonodynamic yield, read rather than recomputed, so the two modalities cannot drift apart in their oxygen dependence. Survival follows the linear-quadratic model; a draw against it decides the cell.

What was deliberately **not** wired: the second, ferroptotic channel. Its gray-to-ROS conversion has no value in the literature, and switching it on would have made the measurement a function of an unanchored knob.
:::

**What was found, and it is not the formula.** The dose-modifying factor -- the dose ratio for equal kill between the oxygenated rim and the hypoxic core -- was read off the population across a sweep of oxygen gradients. It is not a number. It is a curve, and the curve has a peak.

::: numbers
| oxygen gradient | rim pO₂ | core pO₂ | dose-modifying factor |
|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| 30 µm | 11.2 mmHg | 0.0001 mmHg | 2.27 |
| 50 µm | 16.6 mmHg | 0.0149 mmHg | **2.55** |
| 80 µm | 22.0 mmHg | 0.2762 mmHg | 2.36 |
| 120 µm | 26.4 mmHg | 1.4260 mmHg | 1.67 |
| 200 µm | 30.9 mmHg | 5.3636 mmHg | 1.24 |
:::

::: finding
**The factor moves with a quantity the formula does not contain.** The oxygen enhancement ratio, as an equation, has no term for how steeply oxygen falls with depth; it takes a pressure and returns a ratio. The spatial measurement runs from 1.24 to 2.55 across the gradients swept, so whatever it is, it is not the equation restated -- and it varies by more than twice the margin that would make the difference arguable.

**And it peaks in the middle.** Not at the steepest gradient, where one would expect the sharpest contrast between a well-fed rim and a starved core, and not at the shallowest. The reason is structural and nobody put it there: *one gradient parameter sets both zones*. A gradient steep enough to drive the core to true anoxia has already left the rim at eleven millimetres of mercury, which is hypoxic enough that the rim has lost part of its own oxygen advantage -- so the contrast between them narrows from the wrong end.
:::

::: refusal
That structure is also why the comparison against the published band has to be stated carefully, and why the first version of this measurement nearly got it backwards.

Read at the engine's own zone reference of 120 µm, the factor is 1.67 -- well below the published 2.5 to 3.0 -- and the obvious reading is that the model fails. It is the wrong reading. The published band is measured between fully oxygenated and fully *anoxic* cells, and at that gradient this model's deep zone sits near one and a half millimetres of mercury. It is not anoxic. Comparing the two is a category error, and reporting it as a failure would have been an error about what was being measured rather than about the model.

What can honestly be said is narrower and it is a bound, not a match. This model cannot present a fully oxygenated rim beside an anoxic core, because one parameter sets both. **Every factor above is therefore a lower bound on the ratio the published band describes,** and the sweep's maximum of 2.55 is the model's best estimate rather than its answer. It reaches the band at one gradient of five, and only just.
:::

**What it means.** Two things, and the second matters more than the first.

The narrow one: the arm survives a test it could have failed, on a quantity it was not fitted to, and the test was named by this project before the work rather than chosen afterwards to be passable.

The broader one is about what a spatial engine is *for*. A closed-form arm cannot be asked what the oxygen effect looks like in a tumour, because it has no tumour -- it has a cell, and the answer is whatever the formula says. The interior optimum in gradient steepness is not a deeper truth about radiobiology; it is a consequence of how this particular model represents a tumour's oxygen field with a single length. But it is exactly the kind of thing that only appears once an arm is asked to act on a population instead of a point, and it is the reason the remaining six arms are worth lifting across rather than leaving as functions that return numbers.

[FIGURE 41: Radiation in the spatial engine: an oxygen effect measured from a population rather than restated from a formula. (a) The same single-fraction dose against an oxygenated rim and a hypoxic core. (b) The dose-modifying factor against the steepness of the oxygen gradient -- a parameter the formula has no term for -- running from 1.24 to 2.55 and peaking in the interior of the swept range, against the 2.86 the formula restated returns regardless. (c) Why every value is a lower bound: one gradient length sets the rim and the core together, so a gradient steep enough to make the core anoxic leaves the rim hypoxic too.]

### 6.15 Oncolytic Virus, and a Threshold Borrowed From Another Field

Radiation went into the spatial engine first because its oxygen machinery was already built. This arm went second for the opposite reason: it is where the mismatch between a closed-form function and a simulation is at its sharpest, because the thing being modelled is *a front*, and a front in zero dimensions is not a front at all.

**What the engine already had.** A speed. `front_speed` returns `2√(D r)` -- the classical Fisher-KPP result for a wave of advance in a uniform medium, which is a genuinely useful formula and the standard one for this problem. It takes a diffusion constant and a net replication rate, and it returns how fast the front travels.

What it does not take is any account of *where the front is allowed to go*. It has no term for the fraction of the tumour a virus can actually enter, so it returns a positive speed whenever replication beats clearance. Read literally, it says that a virus which can replicate will eventually spread through any tumour, given time.

::: numbers
What was wired: an infection that spreads cell to cell across the grid. A cell is permissive or it is not, decided once at setup -- receptor status is a property of the cell, not a dice roll it gets to repeat. An infected cell lyses, and each permissive living neighbour in its twenty-six-cell neighbourhood is infected with the transmission probability. That is a contact process rather than a differential equation, deliberately: the question here is whether the front *crosses*, not how fast a continuum approximation says it should travel.
:::

**What was found.** Not a slower front. A wall.

::: finding
Below a critical permissive fraction the infection does not cross the tumour at all -- it makes a small local cluster, tens of microns across, and stops. Above it the front spans the whole tumour, five hundred and forty microns. There is almost nothing in between: the largest non-crossing run reaches about a hundred and seventeen microns and the smallest crossing one three hundred and seventy-six.

**And the place where the transition happens is a number from a completely different field.** Site percolation on a three-dimensional cubic lattice is a solved problem in computational physics, and its threshold depends on which neighbours count as connected: 0.0976 for the twenty-six-neighbour rule, 0.3116 for the six-neighbour one. This grid uses twenty-six.

At certain transmission, the measured front crosses between a permissive fraction of 0.08 and 0.1. The twenty-six-neighbour constant, 0.0976, lies inside that bracket. The six-neighbour constant does not.
:::

Nothing in this project was fitted to that number, and percolation theory has no interest in oncolytic viruses. The agreement is a check that the spread rule is doing what a spread rule on a lattice should do -- and the closed-form speed, on those same conditions, is positive throughout. It is wrong about 19 of the swept conditions, and wrong in the direction that matters clinically: it says the virus gets there.

**The threshold moves, and it should.** Lowering the transmission probability raises the permissive fraction needed to cross -- 0.1 at certain transmission, 0.12 at 0.6, 0.3 at 0.3. That is the signature of *site-bond* percolation, where a cell must both be enterable and be successfully infected, and pure site percolation is the limit of certain transmission. A threshold that did not move with transmission would mean the model was not percolating at all, and the agreement above would be a coincidence.

::: refusal
This is not a claim about real oncolytic viruses, and the distinction is easy to lose. The permissive fraction here is a parameter, not a measurement -- nothing in this project measures what share of a real tumour a given virus can enter. What the section shows is conditional: *if* that share is low enough, no dose helps, and here is where "low enough" sits for this lattice.

Nor is the agreement evidence that tumours percolate. It says the spread rule behaves like site percolation on the neighbourhood it runs on, which is a check on the implementation and on the framing rather than a result about biology. A real tumour's permissive cells are not independently scattered -- receptor expression is clustered, and clustering moves a percolation threshold -- so the number would not transfer even if the fraction were known.

One row of the sweep is marked rather than plotted, for a reason worth stating: at the lowest permissive fraction there was no enterable cell anywhere near the tumour centre, so the infection never started. A front radius of zero cannot distinguish that from an infection that started and went nowhere, and those are different claims.
:::

**What it means.** The arm joins the small set in this chapter limited by a threshold rather than a multiplier -- alongside the antigen density a CAR-T cell needs, the receptor a conjugate needs, and the pressure a cavitation event needs. The pattern is consistent enough by now to be worth naming: *the things that stop a therapy are more often gates than dials.* A dial can be turned harder. A gate that is shut is shut.

And it is the clearest case yet for why these arms belong in the spatial engine rather than beside it. The closed-form speed is not wrong as mathematics; it is the correct answer to a question about a uniform medium. A tumour is not one, and the difference is not a correction factor -- it is the difference between a front that arrives late and a front that never arrives.

[FIGURE 42: Oncolytic spread is a threshold, and a closed-form front speed cannot express one. (a) The front either makes a local cluster or spans the tumour, with almost nothing in between; crosses mark runs where no enterable cell existed near the seed at all, a failure to start rather than to cross. (b) The permissive fraction needed to cross, against transmission probability, converging on the twenty-six-neighbour site-percolation constant as transmission approaches certainty -- the signature of site-bond percolation. (c) The closed form predicts spread on every one of these conditions.]

### 6.16 Antibody-Drug Conjugates, and the Distance a Scalar Does Not Have

The bystander effect is the reason a large part of this modality exists. An antibody-drug conjugate carries a poison to a cell bearing the right antigen; a *bystander* payload is one that, when the cell dies, escapes and kills its neighbours too. Conjugates are deliberately engineered with cleavable linkers to get that behaviour, and the clinical argument for it is entirely spatial: the antibody is a large molecule that penetrates a tumour badly, the payload it releases is small and does not, so the payload's job is to reach the cells the conjugate never got to.

**What the engine had.** A number. `bystander_kill_fraction` takes a parameter called `neighbours_in_reach` -- a fraction of the tumour, supplied as an input, which the module's own documentation is careful to flag as an input rather than something derived. There is no distance in it, and no direction. The payload has nowhere to go.

That is not a small simplification. It means the arm cannot state its own rationale: with no geometry, there is no such thing as "cells the conjugate did not reach", and therefore no way to ask the question the effect exists to answer.

::: numbers
What was wired: the conjugate's own penetration field, `exp(-depth/λ)` over the same radial geometry the oxygen field uses but with its own, much shorter length. A cell's chance of direct kill scales with the conjugate that reached it. Each directly killed cell then releases payload into a sphere of a given radius in cells.

Every condition is run twice, identical in every respect including the random stream, differing only in the linker -- cleavable, so the payload escapes, or not, so it does not. The difference between those two runs *is* the bystander's contribution. It is measured rather than supplied.
:::

::: finding
**The payload multiplies the conjugate's reach most where the conjugate reaches least.** At a payload reach of 2 cells, the total kill with a cleavable linker is 4.92 times the kill without it when the antibody penetrates 20 microns -- and only 3.24 times when it penetrates 320. The advantage falls monotonically as penetration improves, which is what the clinical argument says should happen and what the point model has no way to express.
:::

**And then the sweep found the condition, which is the better half of the result.**

::: refusal
At a payload reach of one cell the advantage does not fall at all. It sits at about 2.1 times across a sixteenfold range of antibody penetration -- a span of 1.06 times, which is flat.

The reason is the prediction's own logic running out. A payload that reaches only its immediate neighbours lands almost entirely on cells the conjugate had already reached, so there is no penetration-dependent gap for it to fill. The effect needs the payload to travel *past* the conjugate's own front to be worth anything extra, and at one cell of reach it does not.

That condition was not chosen. It came out of a sweep that included it, and reporting only the reaches that agreed would have been picking the arm that agreed -- which is a thing this project has done before and had to withdraw.

There is a second column pointing the other way, too. The *absolute* number of bystander kills mostly rises as penetration improves, because a conjugate that reaches more cells kills more of them and so leaves more payload sources behind. Both statements are true of the same runs. The ratio is the clinically meaningful one -- it is what adding a cleavable linker buys you -- but publishing it alone would be publishing the more flattering of two answers.
:::

**What is not claimed.** This arm's fitted verdict is NO TARGET and stays there: no published bystander dose-response exists to fit, so the kill magnitudes above are illustrative. What is measured is how the contribution *moves*. The payload's reach is a parameter and nothing here measures how far a released payload actually travels before it is cleared or bound. The conjugate's distribution is the radial-depth proxy rather than a real antibody distribution, which is shaped by binding-site consumption as much as by diffusion. And a bystander-killed cell releases nothing of its own -- the payload came from the conjugate, and a cell that never took up conjugate has none to give, so letting it release would model a chain reaction rather than a bystander effect.

[FIGURE 43: A bystander payload multiplies a conjugate's reach most where the conjugate reaches least. Every point is a matched pair of runs differing only in the linker, so the bystander's contribution is measured rather than supplied. (a) is the prediction and the condition on it together: the advantage falls with penetration at a payload reach of two or three cells and is flat at one, because a payload reaching only its immediate neighbours lands on cells the conjugate reached anyway. (b) is the column that points the other way -- the absolute count mostly rises -- reported beside the ratio rather than instead of it. (c) is why the arm needed a distance at all: the scalar the point model returns has no term for penetration and cannot move with it.]

### 6.17 Chemotherapy, and Two Reasons the Core Survives

Every arm so far in this part of the chapter has been about a formula that was correct mathematics about the wrong domain. This one is about a question the point model cannot even ask.

**What is known.** A tumour's core resists chemotherapy. That has been known for as long as chemotherapy has existed, and there are two standard explanations. The first is delivery: a drug travels from a vessel by diffusion, is consumed on the way, and the cells furthest from a vessel see the least of it. The second is the cell cycle: most cytotoxics kill dividing cells preferentially, and the cells deepest in a tumour -- starved of oxygen and nutrients -- are the least likely to be dividing.

Both are true. Both predict that the core survives. And they fail *in the same place*, which is what makes them so hard to separate: any experiment that finds a rim-biased kill is consistent with either.

::: finding
The distinction is not academic, because the two have **opposite remedies**. If delivery is the binding constraint, the answer is to get more drug in -- better vasculature, smaller molecules, higher dose. If the cell cycle is, then getting more drug in buys nothing at all, and the answer is to recruit the quiescent cells into cycle before treating.

A model with no geometry cannot distinguish them, because it has no "deep" for either effect to happen in. The point model has neither term.
:::

**What was simulated.** The two terms, and the four combinations of them. Drug availability follows the conjugate's own penetration length from the vessel-distance geometry; the cell cycle is read from the phenotype the spheroid layer already assigns by radial position -- proliferating at the rim, quiescent-rich in the core. Turning each off in turn isolates it. Turning both off is the control.

::: numbers
Kill fraction in the hypoxic core, per agent class:

| agent | neither term | cell cycle only | delivery only | both |
|---|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| alkylator (phase-nonspecific) | 0.840 | 0.659 | 0.0023 | 0.0013 |
| antimetabolite (S-phase) | 0.401 | 0.173 | 0.0003 | 0.0000 |
| taxane (M-phase) | 0.226 | 0.089 | 0.0010 | 0.0003 |
:::

**The control comes first, because without it the rest is arithmetic about the zoning.** With neither term active the kill is flat with depth in all three classes -- rim and core within a tenth of each other. That is what makes the other columns terms rather than a property of where the zone boundaries happen to sit.

::: finding
**Delivery dominates.** In every class it takes the core kill to a fraction of a percent of what it was, far harder than the cell cycle does. On these parameters, the depth gradient a real experiment would see is overwhelmingly a delivery gradient.

**But only the cell-cycle term depends on the agent, and that is the half better delivery cannot fix.** With delivery held uniform, the alkylator keeps 78% of its core kill against a quiescent core; the antimetabolite keeps 43% and the taxane 39% -- a factor of 1.8 between them. That is exactly what the classes are supposed to do: an alkylator damages DNA whatever the cell is doing, while the others need it dividing.

That class-dependence is also the check that the cycle arm is measuring the cell cycle at all. If all three classes had lost the same share, the second term would have been the drug field wearing a different label.
:::

::: refusal
Which term dominates is a statement about these parameters, not about chemotherapy. The penetration length and the potency were both chosen rather than measured, and a longer penetration length would shift the balance toward the cycle term. What is robust is the *separability* -- that the two terms can be isolated at all, that the null case is null, and that only one of them varies by agent class.

The cell cycle here is read, not simulated. Cells inherit a phase distribution from their position; nothing in this engine actually divides, so there is no redistribution between phases over time and no recruitment. A regimen designed around recruiting quiescent cells is exactly the thing this cannot yet evaluate, which is worth saying because it is the intervention the finding points at.

And the fitted verdict stays NO TARGET. This is the arm whose dose-response target was already recorded as *unreachable* rather than merely unfitted, and a decomposition does not repair that. The phase-sensitivity numbers behind it are conventional rather than measured, as the module says of itself: the ordering and the zeros are the assertion, not the sizes.
:::

[FIGURE 44: Two effects push a chemotherapy kill to the rim, and they have opposite remedies. (a) is the control: with neither term active the kill is flat with depth, which is what makes the other columns terms rather than artifacts of the zoning. (b) delivery dominates on these parameters, taking the core kill to a fraction of a percent in every class -- the penetration length was chosen, not measured, and a longer one shifts the balance. (c) is the half better delivery cannot fix, and the check that the cycle arm is really the cell cycle: an alkylator damages DNA whatever the cell is doing and keeps most of its core kill, while the phase-specific agents lose most of theirs.]

### 6.18 CAR-T, and What Multiplying Fractions Assumes

Every arm in this chapter that models a sequence of barriers multiplies them. The CAR-T arm multiplies four: the fraction of infused cells that reach the tumour, of those the fraction that penetrate past the rim, of those the fraction not suppressed on arrival, and finally the fraction of tumour cells carrying the target antigen. It is the natural thing to write and it produces a number that matches the clinical picture -- a therapy that cures a leukaemia doing almost nothing to a solid tumour.

**Multiplying fractions asserts that they are independent.** That is a real assumption, and in a point model it is invisible, because there is nowhere for two fractions to fail to be independent. Two of these four are not independent in a tumour: infiltration is a property of *where a cell sits*, and antigen expression is a property of *the cell*. Whether they correlate decides whether the product is the answer.

::: numbers
What was wired: the infiltration **scalar** replaced by a depth field with the same mean, so the two models differ in *distribution* and in nothing else. Antigen positivity is then given a tunable correlation with depth -- from negative, where the antigen sits deep in the tumour that no effector reaches, through zero, where it is independent of position, to positive, where it sits at the rim where the effectors already are.
:::

**The control comes first, and it is the whole design.** At zero correlation the two terms genuinely are independent, so the product must be right. The spatial run agrees with it to 0.6%. Without that agreement, every disagreement elsewhere would be indistinguishable from a coding error -- and the first version of this measurement had one. It multiplied the depth field *on top of* the infiltration fraction rather than in place of it, double-counting the same barrier, and its control failed at every correlation including zero. The control caught it, which is what a control is for.

::: finding
Away from zero the product is wrong in both directions, and not by a little.

| antigen–depth correlation | product ÷ measured |
|--:|--:|
| −0.6 (antigen deep) | **1.20** |
| 0.0 (independent) | 1.006 |
| +0.4 (antigen at the rim) | **0.89** |

When the antigen is concentrated where the effectors cannot reach, the product **over-states** the kill by a fifth. When it is concentrated where they already are, it **under-states** it by a ninth. Neither direction is available to a model with no geometry.
:::

There is a second thing the product cannot represent at all. In the spatial run each cell fails in a nameable way: no effector reached it, or it was below the antigen threshold, or **both**. Between 10,970 and 23,599 cells fall into that last category depending on the correlation, and a product of fractions has no slot for them -- it cannot distinguish a cell lost to one barrier from a cell lost to two. The distinction matters for the same reason the chemotherapy decomposition mattered: improving trafficking rescues the first group and does nothing for the second.

::: refusal
This is not a measurement of any real antigen-depth correlation. Whether antigen expression tracks depth in real tumours, and in which sign, is not something this project measures. What is shown is that the answer *depends* on it, and by how much -- which is a statement about the model's structure rather than about biology.

Every barrier value here remains a placeholder, as this arm's calibration row has said since it was built, and the fitted verdict is unchanged: it was earned on a different quantity and nothing here touches it. The effector field is the same radial-depth proxy the other arms use, while real T-cell infiltration is shaped by chemokine gradients, stromal exclusion and vessel adhesion that this does not represent.

One row of the sweep is marked rather than read as a reversal. At the strongest positive correlation the effect stops growing, because the per-cell antigen probability saturates at one: once the rim cells are certainly positive, a stronger correlation cannot add more of them. That is a ceiling in the parameterisation, not a turning point in the biology.
:::

[FIGURE 45: Multiplying the barrier fractions is right only when the barriers are independent. The spatial run replaces the infiltration scalar with a depth field of the same mean, so the two models differ in distribution and nothing else. (b) is the control and the finding at once: at zero correlation they agree to under one per cent, which is what makes the rest a finding rather than a bug -- the first version of this measurement double-counted the infiltration barrier and its control failed everywhere. (c) is a bucket the product has no slot for: cells that failed both ways, which improving trafficking would not rescue.]

### 6.19 Checkpoint Blockade, and a Number With No Denominator

This arm is unlike the six before it, and the difference is the section.

Every other arm in this part of the chapter needed wiring: a formula existed, the spatial engine could not run it, and the work was to connect them. Checkpoint blockade needed none. Its mechanism has been in the engine for a long time -- a multi-checkpoint panel over PD-1, CTLA-4, LAG-3 and TIM-3, read by the spatial immune layer, with a combined brake that a drug term relaxes. Nothing had to be built.

What was missing is any way to ask the question the arm exists for.

::: finding
`residual_brake` returns a **multiplier**. A multiplier has no denominator.

"Blockade raises immune killing two and a half fold" is a true sentence and an empty one, because it does not say two and a half times *what*. In a point model there is no denominator to be had -- the immune term is a scalar and its size relative to everything else is not represented. On a grid, it is.
:::

**What was simulated.** A cold-to-hot ladder: an untreated tumour, then a pharmacologic inducer, then a physical one, spanning four orders of magnitude of cell death. Each against four strengths of blockade. The immune kill is reported twice -- as the fold-change the point model predicts, and as a share of the killing that is actually happening.

::: numbers
| treatment | direct kills | danger signal | immune kills, unblocked → blocked | fold | immune share |
|---|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| untreated | 2 | 22 | 0 → 0 | — | 0.00% |
| pharmacologic | 483 | 3,013 | 20 → 50 | 2.5× | 3.98% → 9.42% |
| physical | 23,125 | 12,226 | 30 → 75 | 2.5× | 0.13% → 0.32% |
:::

**Three things, and the third is the one to carry.**

The first is the cleanest. **An untreated tumour gets nothing.** Not a little -- nothing, at every blockade strength tested. Two cells die, almost no danger signal is released, and there is no response to amplify. That is the cold-tumour problem, and it is stated here where it can be checked rather than asserted.

The second is what makes the point model's output useless rather than merely incomplete. **The fold-change is the same for both active treatments** -- 2.5 against 2.5. Of course it is: a multiplier does not know what it multiplies. So the single number the point model reports is precisely the quantity that cannot distinguish these two cases, and the quantity that can -- the share, which differs by 31 times -- is the one it does not have.

::: finding
The third runs against the obvious inference, which is why it is worth the space.

**The treatment producing 4.1 times more danger signal has the *smaller* immune share.** The physical arm releases far more of the signal that primes an immune response, and blockade is worth far less against it -- because it has already killed almost everything directly, and left the immune system nothing to add.

The natural way to choose a partner for checkpoint blockade is to rank treatments by how much immunogenic signal they generate and take the top one. On this model that ranking is exactly backwards. What matters is the immune contribution as a share of the kill that *remains*, and the biggest signal producer is the worst on that measure precisely because it is the most effective on its own.
:::

::: refusal
The absolute immune contribution in this engine is small -- a fraction of a percent of the kill under the physical arm -- and that is a known property of the model rather than a discovery about biology. Section 8.4 already records the immune ratio collapsing from roughly a hundred to one down to four to one when the model moved into three dimensions. These are ratios this engine produces, not ratios a patient produces.

The counts are small in absolute terms too, tens of kills rather than thousands, and no interval is quoted. What was checked is convergence: the numbers at a hundred and fifty steps and at two hundred and fifty agree to within the per-cell randomness, so they are not an artifact of how long the run was left going. That check was made before the numbers were reported, because counts this small are exactly where that goes wrong.

The brake parameters are placeholders, and this arm's fitted verdict remains INADMISSIBLE for a reason that still stands: its published response band constrains a product of two factors, neither of which is identifiable from it. Nothing here repairs that.

And the ladder is three points, not a dose-response. It separates cold from warm from hot; it does not locate a threshold between them.
:::

**One last thing, about the arm's status rather than its biology.** Selecting this treatment in the engine still does nothing. The brake acts through the immune layer whatever treatment is chosen, so checkpoint blockade is a *modifier* rather than an applicable arm -- the same distinction `analysis/modality-coverage.md` draws between a mechanism the engine contains and one a run can select. The parity table records it that way rather than rounding it up, because the honest count of what this engine can be asked to *do* is the point of that table.

[FIGURE 46: Checkpoint blockade cannot start a response, and a fold-change cannot say whether one matters. (a) and (b) are the same runs. The fold-change is identical for both active treatments, because a multiplier does not know what it multiplies -- so the quantity the point model reports is the one that cannot tell the two cases apart; the share differs thirty-one fold. The untreated arm gets nothing at any blockade strength. (c) is the warning: the treatment producing four times the danger signal has the smaller immune share, because it already killed almost everything directly, so ranking partners by immunogenic signal gets this backwards.]

### 6.20 Ablation, and Heat Sinks That Do Not Take Turns

The last arm is the one that already had a spatial prediction. Section 6.12 asked where a thermal ablation fails rather than trying to fit it, because its target is a temperature threshold and a threshold cannot identify a threshold parameter. The answer it gave was a *radius*: the width of the surviving sleeve of tissue around a vessel, where flowing blood carries heat away faster than the applicator delivers it. That radius is registered as P20 and it is the arm's only falsifiable claim.

It is also a claim about one vessel.

::: finding
`perivascular_failure_radius_mm` asks how far from *a* vessel tissue survives. A real vessel network is not one vessel, and heat sinks do not take turns. Tissue lying between two vessels is cooled by both, and stays cooler than either sleeve alone describes -- so the surviving heat rise is the *product* of each vessel's own survival factor rather than the nearest one's.

The analytic model is not wrong about this. It simply never had the vessels to ask.
:::

**What was simulated.** The same applicator, on the same cells, over the same vessel network, under two rules: cooling from every vessel, and cooling from the nearest only. The second is the analytic model applied pointwise. They differ in nothing else, so any divergence is the physics rather than a change of parameters.

::: numbers
| vessel spacing | survivors, every vessel cools | survivors, nearest only | under-statement |
|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| 1600 µm | 584 | 584 | 1.00× |
| 800 µm | 584 | 584 | 1.00× |
| 600 µm | 584 | 584 | 1.00× |
| 400 µm | 6,121 | 1,863 | 3.29× |
| 300 µm | 20,784 | 4,565 | **4.55×** |
| 250 µm | 24,158 | 6,814 | 3.55× |
| ≤ 200 µm | *ablation fails everywhere* | — | — |
:::

**The control sits where it should.** At the widest spacings the two rules agree exactly, and not by accident: there the second-nearest vessel is far enough that its cooling factor is essentially one, so the product reduces to the nearest term and the analytic model is simply correct. Its being correct there is what licenses reading the divergence elsewhere as physics rather than as a coding error.

::: finding
Where vessels sit within a cooling length of each other, **the one-vessel model under-states the surviving volume by up to four and a half times.** The sleeves do not merely overlap -- they compound.

**This qualifies P20 rather than refuting it.** The direction that prediction registers survives untouched: thermal ablation fails near vessels and electroporation does not. What changes is that the registered radius is a *lower bound* on where recurrence can sit, and the shortfall grows as the vasculature gets denser -- which is exactly the setting in which perivascular recurrence is reported.
:::

::: refusal
Two rows of that table are not measurements and are marked rather than averaged in. At the tightest spacings the ablation fails *everywhere*: the all-vessel model leaves the tumour entirely intact, and the ratio drifts back toward one for a reason that has nothing to do with the models agreeing. A ratio computed across a saturated arm is not a comparison.

The arm also could not be run at all on the engine's usual geometry, and the reason is worth stating because it is a property of the modality rather than of the code. The default cell is twenty microns, so a tumour on the standard grid is under a millimetre across, while a perfusion cooling length is about two. At that scale every cell is deeply cooled, nothing reaches a lethal thermal dose, and the arm reports zero kills at every vessel density -- which is what the first version of this sweep did. The tumour was smaller than the cooling length. Ablation is a tissue-scale modality and cannot be asked a question on a spheroid-scale lattice, so it declares its own scale, exactly as the slab geometry does for the same reason.

The compounding rule itself is a modelling choice. Independent heat sinks multiplying is a reasonable first form and is not solved from a bioheat equation; a full Pennes solve over the same network would be the check and is not done here. The vessels are points, where real vessels are tubes that cool a line rather than a sphere. And the cooling length remains the placeholder Section 6.12 already flagged -- which is why the *ratio* between two models sharing that placeholder is the result, and the millimetres are not.

The fitted verdict stays UNCONSTRAINED. A second structural claim does not repair a failed fit, and the ledger carries both.
:::

**What it means, and what it closes.** Every arm in this chapter now runs in the spatial engine, and the pattern across the seven that were lifted is consistent enough to state plainly.

In every case the closed form was correct mathematics about the wrong domain -- a wave speed for a uniform medium, an oxygen ratio for a single cell, a bystander fraction with no distance, a product of barriers assumed independent, a multiplier with no denominator, a sleeve around one vessel. In every case the spatial answer differs by a term the formula has no slot for: the gradient's steepness, the lattice's connectivity, the payload's reach, a correlation, a share, a neighbouring vessel. And in most cases the difference is not a correction factor but a change in kind -- a front that never arrives rather than one that arrives late, a therapy that cannot start rather than one that starts weakly.

That is the answer to the question this part of the chapter was written to ask. The arms were not smaller versions of the ferroptosis engine. They were a different sort of object, and the difference was not visible until they were asked to act on a population instead of a point.

[FIGURE 47: Heat sinks do not take turns, and a one-vessel radius cannot say so. Both models in (a) run on the same cells and the same vessels, differing only in whether the non-nearest vessels cool. The agreement at wide spacing in (b) is a control: there the extra cooling factors are negligible and the analytic model is simply right, which is what licenses reading the divergence elsewhere as physics. The shaded tail is excluded rather than read as renewed agreement -- there the ablation fails everywhere, and a ratio across a saturated arm is not a comparison. (c) is the mechanism: solid curves count both vessels, dotted count only the nearer.]

### 6.21 What This Chapter Does Not Claim

**Breadth is not depth, and this project's own guidance says so.** The engine can now express every mechanism its taxonomy can count, and 8 of the sixteen can be applied as a treatment. Neither of those is a claim that any of them is modelled well. `simulations/calibration/CALIBRATION_STATUS.md` carries a row per layer with its named target and its used-in-any-reported-number status, and for every arm in this chapter that status is N: none of them feeds a number this manuscript reports.

**The arms are smaller than the engine they sit beside, and the gap is a range rather than a number.** `analysis/modality-module-depth.md` counts it: 9 modules a modality owns outright, 112 public functions and 1562 lines of production code. What it is counted AGAINST is a judgement, because five modules are machinery several arms reach through and subtracting them from the engine as well as from the arms would shrink the comparator in the direction that flatters this chapter. Counting them as neither side gives 26 modules, 182 functions and 3,805 lines; counting them as engine gives 31, 240 and 4,623. So the arms are between 2.4 and 3.0 times smaller by line count and 1.6 to 2.1 times smaller by public function, and the honest figure is the interval. Those figures were 10.9 to 13.1 and 6.0 to 7.9 before Section 6.6 -- nine arms built rather than reworded have cut the gap by roughly three quarters.  **Part of that last move was an error in the instrument, and separating the two matters more than the number.** Immediately before Section 6.13 the interval read 3.2 to 3.8. Adding the two modules that section describes made it read 3.3 to 3.9 -- *wider* -- because `photosensitizer_pk` had never been listed as a modality's own file and `sonodynamic` was new, so both were counted on the COMPARATOR'S side of a ratio measuring distance from the comparator. Photodynamic therapy's module has existed since long before any arm in this chapter, and its lines were being credited to ferroptosis and to photodynamic therapy at the same time, in two different documents. Correcting the classification is what takes the interval to 2.4 to 3.0; the two new modules on their own would have widened it. The error ran in the direction that made this chapter's case look worse, which is the direction an error of this kind is least likely to be caught in, and it went unnoticed through eight sections that all quoted the ratio it produced.  What none of that buys is stated in the same breath: radiation is at a fourteenth of the ferroptosis engine and chemotherapy at a thirteenth, sonodynamic therapy owns 63 lines against the comparator's 3,805, and the arms with no section of their own have not moved at all. The disparity in calibration is larger still and needs no interval: the ferroptosis work carries legs fitted against independent published data and supplies numbers this manuscript reports, while every arm in this chapter is recorded as feeding no reported number at all. That clause said "years of fitting" until a reviewer checked it against the repository's first commit, which is months rather than years ago — an unmeasured superlative bolted onto a paragraph whose entire design is to replace assertions with a measured interval, and sitting in the one clause the paragraph had declared exempt. An earlier version of this paragraph said the arms were "one function and a configuration struct each", which was true when written and stopped being true without anyone noticing; a later version replaced it with a single ratio, which hid the judgement that produced it. The measurement exists so the sentence cannot flatter itself as modules land, and equally cannot claim a parity it has not reached. What the arms change is which questions can be asked at all: before this work a reader could not ask this engine what radiation does, and the honest reason was that the engine could not answer.

**The taxonomy bounds everything above.** Chemotherapy, radiotherapy and surgery are not among the sixteen mechanisms because none carries a discriminative MeSH descriptor in this project's map, and Section 3.13 shows what happens to a modality the taxonomy cannot name — its literature is absorbed into whichever neighbouring mechanism does have a tag. The coverage column emptied against the taxonomy, not against oncology.


[FIGURE 25: What the engine can be asked, against what the field publishes. Panel (a) is per-mechanism census volume against engine representation; panel (b) changes subject once nothing is absent, because a column emptied to zero invites a reader to take it as a result. The informative split is then APPLICABILITY -- how many mechanisms a run can actually select -- which is a harder number and a smaller one.]

[FIGURE 26: Every applicable arm against the identical tumour from the identical seed, so a difference between two rows is the arm and nothing else. Bars are coloured by ROUTE rather than ranked, because a chart of kill fractions alone invites exactly the reading the analysis refuses. All but radiation's DNA channel are uncalibrated placeholders.]

[FIGURE 27: What the microenvironment does to every arm, split by cell state and drawn on a SIGNED scale. Red is a loss and blue a gain, and the gains are real: clonal heterogeneity supplies a low-glutathione tail that dies while the average cell resists. Which axis bites depends on the cell state, which is why one phenotype alone reported two axes inert -- a statement about the run, not the biology.]

[FIGURE 28: The same CAR-T construct against a leukaemia and a solid tumour, on a log axis. No single step looks catastrophic: three barriers leave six per cent, exhaustion removes most of the remainder, and the antigen ceiling is drawn although it contributes nothing here, because it is a cap rather than a coefficient. Every barrier value is an uncalibrated placeholder.]


[FIGURE 30: How far each modality reaches into tissue. (a) Kill at the surface and at 9.4 mm: photodynamic therapy falls from 92.5% to 0.6% while megavoltage radiation goes 45.4% to 43.7%, and the pharmacologic arm does not attenuate at all. (b) Retention as a share of surface kill. Both panels share one order, sorted by kill at depth, and the UNTREATED arm is the TALLEST bar in (b) at 400% -- a ratio of two near-zero numbers is not robustness, and the two panels must be read together or neither. Attenuation is fixed physics; every kill magnitude rests on uncalibrated biochemistry.]

[FIGURE 31: What a published target could settle, per arm. Drawn because the informative rows are the THREE that failed: a thermal-ablation arm whose target admits almost the whole scanned range, checkpoint blockade, whose response band constrains a product of two factors neither of which is identifiable from it, and the antibody-drug-conjugate bystander effect, which has no published target at all. ADMISSIBLE means a parameter reproduces a band, not that the arm is validated -- none of these feeds a number the quantitative chapters of this manuscript report.]

## Chapter 7: Drug Combinations and Penetration

> Two questions a single-cell model cannot answer on its own: what happens when treatments are combined, and what happens to a drug that has to travel through tissue before it can act.

### 7.1 Spatial Tumor Simulation: Depth-Dependent Energy Deposition

A drug that works in a petri dish may fail in a patient for reasons that have nothing to do with biology. Tumors are three-dimensional structures with blood vessels at the periphery and drug-starved interiors. Light, ultrasound, and drug molecules each attenuate differently as they travel through tissue. If a treatment can't physically reach cancer cells deep inside a tumor, its biochemical potency is irrelevant.

To address the single-cell model's lack of spatial context, we extended the simulation to a 2D heterogeneous tumor grid (500 x 500 cells, 20 μm/cell = 1 cm x 1 cm tissue cross-section) with physics-based energy attenuation. The tumor architecture consists of a glycolytic core (80% glycolytic, 15% OXPHOS, 5% persister), an OXPHOS-rich periphery (70% OXPHOS, 20% glycolytic, 8% persister, 2% persister+NRF2), persister cluster pockets, and a stromal border — totaling 159,033 tumor cells per simulation.

Energy is applied from the tissue surface (row 0) with physically grounded attenuation models:

- **PDT**: Modified Beer-Lambert exponential decay with effective attenuation coefficient 0.31 per mm, yielding a penetration depth of approximately 3.2 mm at 630 nm (Jacques, Phys Med Biol 2013)
- **SDT**: Acoustic intensity attenuation at 0.7 dB/cm/MHz and 1 MHz operating frequency (Cobbold, Foundations of Biomedical Ultrasound, 2007)
- **RSL3**: Uniform concentration (systemic drug, no depth dependence)

Dead cells release labile iron to their Moore neighborhood (8-neighbors), modeling the bystander ferroptosis effect. Iron is consumed by Fenton chemistry each timestep rather than accumulating permanently.

**Results** (Figure 8):

| Depth (mm) | Control | RSL3 | SDT | PDT |
|------------|---------|------|-----|-----|
| 1 | 0.0% | 2.9% | 98.1% | 90.8% |
| 3 | 0.0% | 4.0% | 93.3% | 43.2% |
| 5 | 0.4% | 4.9% | 88.9% | 15.7% |
| 7 | 0.0% | 1.2% | 88.8% | 1.7% |
| 9 | 0.0% | 4.3% | 96.6% | 0.5% |

**Key findings from the spatial simulation**:

1. **PDT kill rate collapses beyond 3 mm.** At 1 mm depth, PDT kills 90.8% of tumor cells; at 5 mm, only 15.7%; at 9 mm, 0.5%. This directly reflects the exponential light attenuation in tissue and quantifies why PDT is limited to superficial tumors.

2. **SDT maintains >88% kill rate through the full 1 cm depth.** Ultrasound attenuation at 1 MHz is mild (~0.7 dB/cm), preserving sufficient acoustic intensity to activate sonosensitizers at centimeter depths. This validates the paper's central claim: SDT extends ROS-ferroptosis to deep-seated tumors where PDT cannot reach.
