3. **RSL3 kills uniformly but weakly (~3-6%).** As a systemic drug, RSL3 shows no depth dependence — it reaches all cells equally. However, it kills selectively: 42.2% of persisters (4,815/11,391) versus 0% of glycolytic cells, consistent with the single-cell model.

4. **Model-predicted overall tumor kill: SDT 91.9%, PDT 26.1%, RSL3 3.7%, Control 0.1%.** PDT killed only 26.1% of the heterogeneous tumor because 74% of cells lie beyond its effective penetration depth. SDT killed 91.9%, roughly 3.5-fold higher modeled kill than PDT in this 1 cm tissue model (a light-versus-ultrasound penetration contrast between two physical modalities, not a drug-versus-physical claim).

[FIGURE 8: Depth-kill curves: tissue penetration sets modality reach (2D model). (a) Observed tumor kill versus depth across 1 cm of tissue: SDT (ultrasound) stays near 95 to 100% throughout, PDT (light) collapses from about 93% at the surface to near zero by 10 mm, and RSL3 (systemic drug) is a flat, near-zero baseline at every depth. (b) The driving physics computed from the model's own equations: PDT light decays by Beer-Lambert (effective attenuation 0.31 per mm, penetration depth about 3.2 mm), SDT acoustic attenuation is mild (0.7 dB/cm/MHz at 1 MHz), and RSL3 is depth-independent. RSL3 reaches every depth yet kills little, a biochemical limit rather than a penetration one. The depth profiles follow well-measured physics (high confidence); absolute kill percentages rest on uncalibrated biochemistry, so the profile shape is the result, not the magnitudes. SDT is modeled as O2-independent, an optimistic upper bound (Section 10.1).]

The spatial model treats RSL3 as uniformly distributed, but real drug concentration drops with distance from the nearest blood vessel. A tissue-specific drug penetration module using the exponential Krogh cylinder approximation (C(r) = C₀ × exp(-r/λ), where λ = √(D/k) is the penetration length) shows that even at the vessel wall (the highest-concentration position), tissue-specific vascular permeability reduces effective drug exposure — yielding model-predicted vessel-wall persister kill rates of 12.1% in well-vascularized epithelial tissue, 2.6% in poorly-vascularized tissue (pancreatic), and 1.8% behind the blood-brain barrier (glioblastoma), with overall tumor kill rates even lower (6.6%, 2.1%, 1.5% respectively). These are substantially lower than the 40% kill rate observed in 2D culture, reinforcing the in-vitro-to-in-vivo translation gap. All transport parameters are estimated, not calibrated — the comparison across tissue types is directionally informative, not quantitatively precise. Since then, the exponential penetration FORM and the reference-drug penetration LENGTH have been validated against published measured data (issue #335; `analysis/calibration/penetration-validation.md`). The model carries a doxorubicin transport reference, a well-characterized small molecule used here for its penetration physics only, not its DNA-damage pharmacology (the cell-level kill still runs through the RSL3 / GPX4-inhibition pathway). Primeau 2005 (PMID 16361566) and Tannock 2002 (PMID 11895922) both report drug concentration declining exponentially with distance from the vessel in vivo, and that doxorubicin reference (half-distance 34.7 micrometers) falls within Tannock 2002's measured 25 to 75 micrometer range and about 13 percent below the Primeau 2005 and Minchinton 2006 ranges (40 to 80 micrometers), so the functional form and order of magnitude are anchored at the conservative end. Two pieces remain unvalidated and are flagged as such. First, the RSL3-specific penetration length stays an extrapolation, since no ferroptosis inducer has published spatial penetration data. Second, a dose-dependent binding-site barrier is not a feature of this passive-diffusion model at all: its uptake is linear, so penetration depth is dose-independent, whereas a true binding-site barrier saturates and deepens with dose. A data-availability review concluded that this mechanism is not warranted for the small molecules the model targets, so it is deliberately not added rather than introduced as unvalidatable complexity. The strong dose-dependent binding-site barrier is fundamentally an antibody phenomenon: it was discovered with antibodies (Fujimori and Weinstein 1990, PMID 2362198), the canonical dose-titration showing penetration deepening with dose is an antibody study (Saga 1995, PMID 7568060), and the closed-form signature, penetration distance scaling as the square root of dose, is derived for antibodies (Thurber and Wittrup 2008, PMID 18541331). For small molecules the effect is physically weak, since a deep and slowly saturating binding sink barely saturates at achievable doses (the El-Kareh and Secomb tumor-cord model predicts little dose-through deepening for doxorubicin), and there is no extractable small-molecule dose-resolved penetration-depth dataset to validate such a term. Were the model ever extended to antibodies or antibody-drug conjugates, a saturable Michaelis-Menten uptake term validated against that antibody dose-titration data would be the appropriate addition.

**What this means.** For deep-seated tumors (pancreatic, hepatic, pelvic), any drug that cannot penetrate centimeters into tissue will underperform its in-vitro promise regardless of its biochemical potency. Part of the in-vitro-to-in-vivo gap is physics, not biology, and it applies to RSL3 and to any systemically delivered drug with similar tissue distribution limitations. Ultrasound penetrates centimeters with modest attenuation, so SDT's energy delivery is far less depth-limited than light (PDT), which is absorbed within millimeters; whether that delivered energy actually yields ferroptosis at depth depends on the modeled biochemistry (the established physics is the penetration, not the downstream kill).

**How to test this prediction.** Grow persister-enriched cancer cells in 3D tumor spheroids (500 μm diameter or larger) and apply PDT, SDT, and RSL3. Measure cell viability at multiple depths within the spheroid using confocal sectioning or optical clearing with viability staining.

*Expected if prediction holds*: PDT kill exceeds 80% at the spheroid surface but drops sharply toward the core (within 250 μm for a 500 μm spheroid). SDT kill remains high at all measured depths. RSL3 kill drops with distance from the medium supply surface. For larger tissue constructs or in vivo models, the differential should scale with the larger dimensions predicted by the 1 cm spatial simulation.

*Expected if prediction fails*: All three modalities show similar depth-dependent kill profiles. This would indicate that the model's differential attenuation parameters are wrong, or that drug penetration in 3D culture is better than the Krogh cylinder approximation predicts. Either result would be informative for refining the model.

### 7.2 Vulnerability Window: How Long Does the Ferroptosis-Sensitive Window Stay Open?

If residual cancer cells are temporarily more vulnerable to ferroptosis after initial treatment, the critical practical question becomes: how long does the window stay open? A vulnerability that exists but closes within hours is biologically real but clinically useless — there is no time to administer a follow-up treatment. A window that lasts weeks gives clinicians a realistic intervention opportunity.

A critical question for clinical translation is timing: how long after chemotherapy withdrawal do persister cells remain ferroptosis-sensitive? We modeled the recovery of defense pathways (FSP1, GPX4, NRF2, GSH) using exponential kinetics with literature-estimated half-times (FSP1: 7 days, GPX4: 3 days, NRF2: 5 days, GSH: 1 day) and simulated 100,000 cells per condition at each timepoint.

| Time post-chemo | RSL3 kill | SDT kill |
|-----------------|-----------|----------|
| 0 hours | 42.4% | 100.0% |
| 6 hours | 34.3% | 100.0% |
| 1 day | 16.1% | 100.0% |
| 3 days | 1.4% | 100.0% |
| 1 week | <0.01% | 99.95% |
| 2 weeks | 0.00% | 99.8% |
| 4 weeks | 0.00% | 99.5% |

**The RSL3 vulnerability window closes within 3 days** as GPX4 is re-expressed. By one week, RSL3 is completely ineffective (<0.01%). **The SDT window remains open for at least 4 weeks** (99.5% kill at day 28), because the exogenous ROS burst overwhelms even partially recovered antioxidant defenses. If these estimates hold, the scheduling implication is that pharmacologic ferroptosis inducers would require intervention within days of chemotherapy, while physical modalities are predicted to allow a wider window. These window estimates depend on literature-derived half-life parameters and should be treated as approximate until experimentally validated; whether either window is actionable in human tumors is untested.

[FIGURE 23: The ferroptosis-sensitive treatment window. (a) Post-chemotherapy kill rate over time: RSL3 collapses from 42.4% to 1.4% by day 3 and to near-zero by day 7 as GPX4 is re-expressed, while SDT (and PDT, which tracks it) holds ~100%→99.5% through day 28; the shaded region marks the open RSL3 window. (b) The RSL3 collapse tracks mean GPX4 recovery (twin axis) — the re-expression of the primary defense is what closes the window. Recovery half-times are literature-estimated, so durations are approximate (medium confidence).]

**What this means.** If the model is correct, the logistics of treatment timing become a critical variable — not just for efficacy, but for determining which modality class is even feasible. A GPX4 inhibitor administered one week after chemotherapy would arrive to find a population of cells that has already re-expressed its primary defense. A physical ROS modality administered at the same timepoint would still find cells whose antioxidant reserves, while partially recovered, remain overwhelmed by the exogenous ROS dose. This converts a biological question (how long are cells vulnerable?) into a practical scheduling question (how quickly can the follow-up treatment be delivered?).

**How to test this prediction.** After drug withdrawal from a persister-inducing therapy (e.g., osimertinib withdrawal from EGFR-mutant lung cancer cells), measure GPX4, FSP1, NRF2, and GSH levels at serial timepoints (0h, 6h, 1d, 3d, 1wk, 2wk, 4wk). At each timepoint, assess ferroptosis sensitivity by treating matched samples with RSL3 and measuring cell death.

*Expected if prediction holds*: GPX4 protein levels recover to baseline within 3-5 days (half-life ~3 days). FSP1 recovers more slowly (half-life ~7 days). RSL3 sensitivity drops in parallel with GPX4 recovery. Sensitivity to exogenous oxidative stress (e.g., tert-butyl hydroperoxide as a proxy for exogenous ROS, though it does not replicate SDT's spatial or mechanistic features) persists longer because the ROS dose overwhelms partially recovered defenses.

*Expected if prediction fails*: All defense pathways recover simultaneously rather than sequentially. This would mean the differential half-life estimates in the model are wrong, and there is no pharmacologic-vs-physical window difference — both modality classes face the same timing constraint. Alternatively, recovery may be much faster than modeled (hours, not days), in which case the window is too narrow for any follow-up treatment to exploit.

### 7.3 Mechanistic Combination Modeling: Why Drug Pairs Synergize

The question behind drug combination design is straightforward: if the cell has two parallel defense mechanisms, what happens when you attack both at once? The ferroptosis pathway has two parallel repair arms: GPX4 (GSH-dependent) and FSP1 (CoQ10-dependent). These feed into a shared antioxidant quench term that suppresses autocatalytic lipid peroxidation propagation. Depleting both arms simultaneously should produce mechanistic synergy — the simulation lets us test this prediction quantitatively.

Running pairwise combinations of four interventions (RSL3/GPX4 inhibitor, SDT/exogenous ROS, FSP1 inhibitor, HDAC inhibitor/basal ROS upregulation) on 1,000 persister cells per condition and comparing against Bliss-independent predictions reveals three synergistic pairs. RSL3 + FSP1i is the strongest (Bliss synergy score 1.99): single-agent RSL3 kills 40% and FSP1i kills 3.7%, but the combination kills 84.1% — nearly double the 42.2% Bliss prediction. The pathway traces show why: with both GPX4 and FSP1 depleted, mean lipid peroxidation reaches 8.7 (near the 10.0 death threshold), whereas RSL3 alone only drives LP to 4.5 because intact FSP1 partially compensates. RSL3 + HDACi (1.25x) and FSP1i + HDACi (1.21x) are also synergistic. SDT combinations are merely additive because SDT alone already kills 100% of persisters (ceiling effect). Drug potency parameters (FSP1i 85% inhibition, HDACi 2x ROS) are estimates — exact synergy scores depend on these assumptions, but the directional finding (dual-pathway depletion > single-pathway) is robust to parameter variation.

[FIGURE 22: Dual-pathway depletion synergy. (a) Single-agent RSL3 (40%) and FSP1i (3.7%) versus the observed combination (84.1%) and the Bliss-independent prediction (42.2%) — the combination nearly doubles the expected kill (1.99× synergy). (b) Pairwise Bliss synergy scores across the four interventions; SDT pairs are excluded because SDT alone kills 100% of persisters (ceiling). Drug potencies are estimates; the directional result (dual-pathway depletion outperforms single-pathway) held across the ±50% sensitivity sweep.]

**What this means.** The ~1.99× Bliss synergy — whose 95% prior-predictive interval runs ~1.0× to 5.2×, so the supra-additive direction is the result and the magnitude is not — is not about the specific drug pair RSL3 + FSP1i. It is about the principle: ferroptosis has two parallel repair pathways, and disabling both is mechanistically more effective than disabling either alone. Any drug combination that simultaneously depletes GPX4-dependent and GPX4-independent repair should produce synergy, regardless of the specific molecules used. This generalizes the prediction beyond the compounds in the simulation — it identifies a strategy (dual-pathway depletion) rather than endorsing a specific regimen. The practical implication for drug development: screen combination strategies by their pathway coverage, not just by their individual potency.

**How to test this prediction.** Treat persister-enriched cancer cells with a GPX4 inhibitor (RSL3 or ML162) and an FSP1 inhibitor (iFSP1 or DHODH inhibitor brequinar) across a dose matrix. Compute the combination index using Chou-Talalay analysis or compare against Bliss independence predictions. Measure lipid peroxidation (C11-BODIPY) to confirm the mechanistic pathway.

**The two arms are not equally well precedented, and the metric choice is not free.** Both cautions come from the census rather than from judgement. Across the 13,346 census articles carrying the `Ferroptosis` descriptor, the GPX4 arm's reagents appear in 621 articles (RSL3 609, ML162 12) and the FSP1 arm's in 35 (iFSP1 23, brequinar 12), a factor of 17.7. So dose-finding is asymmetric: there is abundant comparative work behind an RSL3 concentration and almost none behind an iFSP1 one, the offered surrogates are thinner than the primaries, and a negative result on the FSP1 arm is correspondingly harder to attribute. This is not an argument against the experiment -- a compound is rare in the literature when it is new for the same reason it is rare when it is poor, and iFSP1 comes from the 2019 work this prediction rests on -- but it is worth knowing before the bench rather than after (`analysis/census-protocol-precedent.md`). On the metric, the *or* above should be an *and*. The model's output is a Bliss excess, while the literature a result would be compared against names Chou-Talalay: over a strided census control of 108,712 records, Chou-Talalay appears in 46 and Bliss in 1. Reporting only the model's metric yields a number with nothing published beside it; reporting only the field's does not connect to the model being tested. Report both, and publish the raw dose-response matrix, because the two scales are not interconvertible without it (`analysis/census-synergy-metrics.md`).

*Expected if prediction holds*: Combination index < 0.5 (strong synergy) at concentrations where single agents produce 20-50% kill. Lipid peroxidation in the combination group exceeds single-agent levels by at least 50%, consistent with dual-pathway depletion removing the compensation that limits single-agent LP accumulation.

*Expected if prediction fails*: Combination index > 0.8 (additive or antagonistic). This would indicate that FSP1 does not function as a truly independent parallel repair pathway — perhaps it shares upstream regulators with GPX4, or perhaps the model's independence assumption overstates their biochemical separation. An antagonistic result (CI > 1.0) would be particularly informative, suggesting the two pathways interact in ways the model does not capture.

## Chapter 8: Resistance Mechanisms

> Four properties of a real tumour — too little oxygen, an immune response, a protective stroma, an acidic interior — applied to the same treatments, one at a time, to see which of them a drug survives and which it does not.

The simulations in Chapter 5 showed that persister cells are selectively vulnerable to ferroptosis. The spatial and combination models in Chapter 7 showed that this vulnerability is modulated by depth, timing, and pathway targeting. But all of those models assumed a uniform, well-controlled environment — the biological equivalent of a petri dish. Real tumors are nothing like petri dishes.

This chapter examines four features of the tumor microenvironment (TME) that the model predicts will alter treatment outcomes. Three are resistance mechanisms that selectively penalize pharmacologic ferroptosis inducers while leaving physical modalities relatively unaffected: oxygen gradients (Section 10.1), stromal metabolic support (Section 10.3), and acidic pH (Section 10.4). One is an amplification mechanism that favors treatments producing dense spatial kill patterns: immune coupling through DAMP signaling (Section 10.2). Together, they share a structural asymmetry: pharmacologic approaches depend on endogenous cellular processes — basal ROS generation, GSH depletion, drug bioavailability — that the TME can disrupt. Physical modalities deliver exogenous energy that bypasses these dependencies. Whether this asymmetry holds in real tumors is an open question; the experiments proposed in each section below are designed to test it.

### 8.1 Tumor Microenvironment: Oxygen Gradients and Hypoxia

The center of a solid tumor has almost no oxygen. Blood vessels penetrate only the outer 100-150 micrometers of tissue, and cells deeper than that rely on whatever oxygen diffuses inward — which, for many tumors, is close to zero. This matters for ferroptosis because the earliest step in the cascade — generation of basal reactive oxygen species by the mitochondrial electron transport chain — requires oxygen as the terminal electron acceptor. If there is no oxygen, there is no basal ROS, and a drug like RSL3 that works by disabling the cell's defense against ROS-driven lipid damage has nothing to defend against. It is like hiring a firefighter in a town where no fires ever start.

The preceding simulations assumed uniform oxygenation — every cell generates the same basal ROS regardless of position. Real tumors have oxygen gradients: O2 penetrates approximately 100-150 μm from the vasculature[^vaupel1989], modeled via the Krogh cylinder approximation. This creates hypoxic cores where mitochondrial electron transport chain activity drops and basal ROS production falls — oxygen is the terminal electron acceptor that drives the chain[^bio2e_74].

[^vaupel1989]: Vaupel P et al. Blood flow, oxygen and nutrient supply, and metabolic microenvironment of human tumors. Cancer Research (1989). O2 transport physiology consistent with OpenStax Anatomy & Physiology 2e, Ch.22.4.
[^bio2e_74]: OpenStax Biology 2e, Chapter 8, Section 10.4: Oxidative Phosphorylation.

To quantify this, we applied an exponential O2 gradient from the tumor edge inward (O2(d) = exp(-d/λ), same Krogh approximation used for drug penetration) and compared treatment efficacy under uniform vs gradient conditions. Because the model only modulates basal ROS (not Fenton chemistry or SDT/PDT dose), this is a conservative estimate of hypoxia's protective effect.

The model produces a clear differential. Under the O2 gradient (λ = 120μm), RSL3 overall kill collapses from 3.7% to 0.1%, and in the hypoxic zone (deeper than 360μm from the tumor edge) RSL3 kills zero cells. SDT overall kill drops only modestly, from 91.9% to 87.8%, and maintains 86.6% kill even in the hypoxic zone, though as the caveat below details this depends entirely on treating SDT as O2-independent (re-running the identical comparison under the opposite, fully O2-dependent assumption collapses the SDT overall gradient kill to 4.0% at this λ, 87.8% to 4.0%, and the hypoxic-zone kill to 0%, so 87.8% is an upper bound, not a central estimate; both bounds are reported in the caveat below). This differential is robust across all tested penetration lengths (λ = 80, 100, 120, 150μm) and emerges directly from the biochemistry: RSL3 inhibits GPX4 but depends on the cell's own mitochondrial ROS to initiate lipid peroxidation. In hypoxia, there is no basal ROS to drive the cascade, regardless of how thoroughly GPX4 is inhibited. SDT delivers exogenous ROS via sonosensitizer activation, which the model treats as a primarily Type I, electron-transfer mechanism (less O2-dependent than the Type II singlet-oxygen pathway) and therefore able to bypass the mitochondrial bottleneck. That "primarily Type I" assumption is itself contested: by the same Dong 2023 reference cited below, the SDT ROS mechanism is "not fully elucidated" and may include O2-dependent routes, so this paragraph states the model's premise, with the caveat that follows correcting the record.

This finding has two implications. First, it quantifies a specific mechanism by which the tumor microenvironment protects cells from pharmacologic ferroptosis inducers — not through upregulation of defense pathways (the NRF2/MUFA mechanisms modeled in Chapter 5), but through the absence of the upstream trigger (basal ROS). Second, it provides a mechanistic rationale for why physical ROS modalities might maintain efficacy in contexts where pharmacologic approaches fail: they supply what hypoxic cells lack. Independent in vivo evidence is consistent with the pharmacologic-inducer side of this mechanism: in pancreatic cancer the HIF-2 transcription factor coordinates ferroptosis resistance through two arms — upregulating cysteine/glutathione antioxidant defense (system Xc- and the transsulfuration enzymes), and inducing Parkin-mediated mitophagy that suppresses mitochondrial function and ROS generation[^pmid41932308]. The second arm matches the loss-of-upstream-trigger mechanism the model encodes (no basal ROS, no cascade); the first is precisely the defense-pathway upregulation this section distinguishes from it. So the paper supports the broader claim — hypoxia/HIF-2 confers ferroptosis resistance in vivo — while showing the real biology combines both routes rather than the single mechanism the model isolates. The relationship is not universal, though. In clear-cell renal carcinoma the HIF-2/HILPDA axis instead enriches polyunsaturated lipids and *sensitizes* cells to GPX4 inhibitors[^pmid30962421], so "hypoxia disables RSL3" is tumor-type-specific rather than a general law, and any tissue where hypoxia signaling enriches peroxidation-prone lipids would erase the modeled SDT advantage. This is the same depth-vs-penetration argument made in Section 10.2, but now grounded in TME biology rather than tissue optics alone.

The literature's own balance on this question is now measured rather than characterised, and it supports calling the leg contested. Of 291 census articles carrying `Ferroptosis` together with a hypoxia-family descriptor, 18 state a direction unambiguously in their title: 11 report hypoxia protecting against ferroptosis and 7 report it sensitising, a 61% to 39% split whose 95% interval (39-80%) comfortably contains an even one. So both directions are reported in substantial numbers and their ratio is not established — which is what contested should mean.

Two things about that measurement are worth carrying, because the first nearly went the other way. A keyword classifier put the sensitising direction ahead 62% to 38%, the reverse of the adjudicated answer, and it failed for a reason no pattern-tuning fixes: *hypoxia-induced ferroptosis resistance* contains *hypoxia-induced ferroptosis*, so a phrase asserting protection contains the phrase asserting sensitisation as a substring. That construction concentrates in tumour biology, where ferroptosis resistance is the thing being studied, so the misclassification fell hardest on exactly the articles this book cares about. And the adjudication rests on 18 titles judged by one reader, which is weaker evidence than a working classifier at scale would be; every label is committed with its reason in `analysis/hypoxia-direction-adjudication.csv` so a reader can disagree individually (`analysis/census-hypoxia-direction.md`).

We have since made the contested hypoxia leg runnable from BOTH sides, not just caveated. On the SDT side, an O2-dependent (Type II) exo-ROS mode (Section 10.1 caveat, issue #358) collapses the SDT hypoxic kill from 87.8% to roughly 4% if SDT's ROS is fully oxygen-dependent. On the RSL3 side, wiring the NCOA4-ferritinophagy plus hypoxia-iron coupling (ferroptosis-core 0.38.0, issue #365) into the 3D spatial model lets hypoxia RAISE the Fenton iron substrate: enabling it lifts RSL3 ferroptosis kill several-fold (in one 3D configuration, grid 60, λ = 50 μm, 180 steps, with the ferritinophagy-release and hypoxia-iron-sensitivity knobs both at 2.0, overall kill rose from 0.5% to 2.9%). Crucially, the rise reaches the deep, near-anoxic core too: the hypoxic-zone kill goes from 0% to 1.3% at λ = 50, and at a more anoxic λ = 40 the deep-zone kill rises further still. That deep-core rescue is, however, a MODEL ARTIFACT worth flagging rather than a finding. The model's Fenton ROS term is oxygen-independent: it scales with iron and a constant rate and is added to total ROS independently of basal ROS, and the model modulates only basal ROS, not the Fenton rate (see the caveat below). So the iron boost, which is largest exactly where oxygen is lowest, raises Fenton ROS in the core regardless of how anoxic it is. A real anoxic core would also lose the superoxide/SOD-derived H2O2 substrate that drives Fenton, which this model does not couple to oxygen, so the model OVERSTATES the deep-core rescue. The robust, defensible conclusion is the direction, not the deep-core magnitude: hypoxia can raise the RSL3 Fenton-iron contribution, so "hypoxia uniformly protects RSL3" is too strong, and combined with the O2-dependent SDT leg the SDT-versus-RSL3 hypoxia asymmetry shrinks from both sides; whether a genuinely anoxic core is rescued turns on whether its Fenton H2O2 substrate survives hypoxia. The default model assumes that substrate away (an O2-independent Fenton term), but the question is now runnable rather than only flagged. The model adds an off-by-default O2-dependent Fenton option (ferroptosis-core 0.44.0, issue #383) that scales the effective Fenton flux down where oxygen is low, on the grounds that the Fenton substrate H2O2 is itself O2-derived (superoxide, then SOD, then H2O2, the Haber-Weiss chain[^pmid10963860]) and that ferroptotic lipid peroxidation is an oxygen-consuming lipid-radical chain (reviewed in the ferroptosis Primer[^pmid28985560]). Re-running the same configuration with the hypoxia-iron coupling on and the Fenton fully O2-coupled removes the deep-core rescue: the near-anoxic-zone kill falls back down (in the spatial A/B it drops by more than half, toward the un-rescued baseline), which confirms that the rescue was a substrate artifact rather than a biological prediction. The coherent picture once both effects are active is that hypoxia raises the labile iron but starves the Fenton reaction of H2O2, so the net deep-core effect is no rescue, while the iron rise can still raise kill in the oxygenated rim and mid-zone where some H2O2 remains. All three knobs (ferritinophagy release, hypoxia-iron sensitivity, Fenton O2-dependence) are off by default and uncalibrated; the numbers are directional sensitivities, not book results.

[FIGURE 15: Hypoxia cross-section showing O2 gradient from blood vessel into tumor core. RSL3 efficacy collapses in the hypoxic interior; SDT maintains efficacy via exogenous ROS delivery that bypasses the mitochondrial O2 requirement.]

[FIGURE 21: Quantitative hypoxia kill-collapse (2D model, immune off). (a) Normoxic versus hypoxic overall kill: RSL3 collapses from 3.7% to ~0.1% (a ~37-fold drop) while SDT holds at 91.9%→87.8%. (b) The hypoxic-zone result is robust across O2 penetration lengths λ = 80–150 µm (flat lines), with normoxic baselines shown as dashed references. Important caveat: SDT is modeled here as fully O2-independent — an optimistic upper bound. SDT's own ROS generation is widely held to be O2-dependent (the leading clinical SDT agent is a Type II, O2-dependent sensitizer; see the caveat above), so the *direction* of the asymmetry is more defensible than the *magnitude* of the gap. Re-running the same comparison under full O2-dependence (issue #358) collapses the SDT gradient kill to a mean of roughly 3.7% (range 2.7% to 4.9% across λ; 4.0% at λ = 120) and to 0% in the hypoxic zone: the deep-zone asymmetry versus RSL3 vanishes (both roughly 0%), while a small rim-driven overall gap remains, so the panel's 87.8% is the upper of two bounds bracketing the unresolved true value.]

**How to test this prediction.** Compare RSL3 and SDT efficacy in normoxic (21% O2) versus hypoxic (1% O2) conditions using a hypoxia chamber or 3D tumor spheroids with verified hypoxic cores (pimonidazole staining).

*Expected if prediction holds*: RSL3 kill drops more than 10-fold in 1% O2 conditions, paralleling the simulation's 3.7% → 0.1% collapse. SDT kill drops less than 2-fold (the simulation predicts 91.9% → 87.8%). Lipid peroxidation measurements (C11-BODIPY) should show that RSL3-treated cells in hypoxia accumulate minimal LP, confirming that the basal-ROS-dependent cascade cannot initiate without oxygen.

*Expected if prediction fails*: Both RSL3 and SDT show similar efficacy reductions in hypoxia. This would mean SDT's ROS generation is more O2-dependent than the model assumes — specifically, that the Type I electron-transfer mechanism (which we model as O2-independent) actually requires significant oxygen. Alternatively, RSL3 may maintain efficacy in hypoxia through a mechanism the model does not capture (e.g., direct GPX4 inactivation triggering LP accumulation through non-mitochondrial ROS sources). Either result would revise the model's central assumption about the ROS-source asymmetry.

Caveats — and this is the most important caveat in the chapter: the O2 model treats SDT/PDT as fully O2-independent, which is the single most optimistic assumption in this analysis and is contested by the broader literature. The Type II photodynamic mechanism requires O2 for singlet-oxygen generation, and the mainstream sonodynamic-therapy field treats hypoxia as a primary SDT bottleneck whose ROS-generation mechanism remains "not fully elucidated"[^pmid37621595]. Tellingly, the leading clinical SDT agent (SONALA-001, a 5-ALA-derived protoporphyrin IX activated by focused ultrasound) is a Type II, oxygen-dependent sensitizer[^sonala2026] — not the Type I, O2-independent mechanism we model. The modeled 87.8% hypoxic-zone SDT kill should therefore be read as an upper bound, and "physical ROS bypasses hypoxia" as a hypothesis to be tested (Section 10.2), not an established result. The Fenton reaction substrate (H2O2) is also O2-derived via the superoxide→SOD pathway, but the default model modulates only basal_ros, not the Fenton rate directly (an off-by-default O2-dependent Fenton option, ferroptosis-core 0.44.0, issue #383, now couples the Fenton substrate to local O2; enabling it removes the deep-core hypoxia-iron rescue described above, confirming that rescue was a substrate artifact). The O2 field is steady-state (no consumption dynamics) and uses distance from the tumor edge as the O2 source (no explicit vasculature in this 2D analysis; the 3D suite adds an explicit but uncalibrated `vasculature` module — see Section 10.4). These simplifications cut both ways: the RSL3 collapse is well-grounded (the basal-ROS dependence is independently supported, above), but if SDT's own ROS generation is O2-limited — as the Type II clinical agent implies — the SDT-versus-RSL3 asymmetry shrinks rather than grows. The honest position is that the *direction* (SDT less O2-dependent than RSL3) is plausible while the *magnitude* of the gap, and whether it survives at all in a given tumor, is unresolved. To make that contested assumption testable rather than only caveated, the model now includes an off-by-default oxygen-dependent SDT mode (a configurable Type II fraction that scales the exogenous ROS yield by local O2, so SDT loses efficacy in hypoxic zones like the clinical SONALA-001 agent; ferroptosis-core 0.33.0), exposed in the 2D `sim-tme` via the `--sdt-o2-dependence` flag and in the 3D suite (issue #358). Re-running the same 2D hypoxia comparison under full O2-dependence (the Type II extreme) collapses the SDT result: gradient SDT overall kill falls from 87.8% to roughly 3.7% (the mean across λ; 4.0% at λ = 120 μm, the λ matching the 87.8% upper bound, with a range of 2.7% to 4.9% across λ = 80 to 150 μm) and the hypoxic-zone SDT kill falls from 86.6% to 0%. In the deep hypoxic zone the SDT-versus-RSL3 asymmetry disappears entirely (both fall to roughly 0%, matching RSL3's 86.6%-zone collapse to 0%). In the overall metric a residual SDT advantage remains (roughly 3.7% to 4.0% versus RSL3's 0.1%), but it is rim-driven: it comes only from the still-oxygenated tumor edge that SDT's O2-dependent ROS can still reach and RSL3 cannot. The real hypoxic SDT efficacy lies between the two bounds, because the actual Type I versus Type II split is sonosensitizer-specific and uncalibrated here: the O2-independent upper bound (SDT 87.8% at λ = 120) and the fully O2-dependent lower bound (SDT 4.0% at λ = 120) bracket it. This converts the contested leg from "the model assumes O2-independence" into a quantified range, and it makes the honest reading unambiguous: the *direction* (SDT at most as O2-dependent as RSL3, plausibly less) may survive, but the *magnitude* of any hypoxia advantage is unresolved and could be zero.

[^pmid41932308]: Hubbi ME, Wang CL, Suhail Y, et al. (Dang CV, senior author). The HIF-2 transcription factor mediates resistance to ferroptosis in pancreatic cancer. Molecular Cell (2026). PMID: 41932308. HIF-2 coordinates two resistance arms of roughly equal weight: (i) upregulating system Xc- and transsulfuration (CBS/CTH) to raise cysteine/glutathione antioxidant defense, and (ii) inducing Parkin mitophagy that suppresses mitochondrial function and ROS generation. We cite it for arm (ii), which matches the model's basal-ROS-removal mechanism, while noting arm (i) is the defense-pathway route the model does not isolate.
[^pmid30962421]: Zou Y et al. A GPX4-dependent cancer cell state underlies the clear-cell morphology and confers sensitivity to ferroptosis. Nature Communications (2019). PMID: 30962421. In clear-cell renal carcinoma the HIF-2/HILPDA axis enriches PUFAs and *increases* GPX4-inhibitor sensitivity, the opposite direction, establishing that the hypoxia-ferroptosis relationship is tumor-type-specific.
[^sonala2026]: An early clinical trial of 5-ALA sonodynamic therapy in recurrent high-grade glioma (SONALA-001 / SonALAsense; 5-ALA-derived protoporphyrin IX, a Type II sonosensitizer, activated by MR-guided focused ultrasound). Sanai N et al. Science Translational Medicine (2025; published online 2025-11-26). PMID: 41296829. DOI: 10.1126/scitranslmed.ads5813; trial NCT04559685. The leading clinical SDT agent uses the oxygen-dependent Type II mechanism.
[^pmid37621595]: Dong HQ, Fu XF, Wang MY, Zhu J. Research progress on reactive oxygen species production mechanisms in tumor sonodynamic therapy. World Journal of Clinical Cases (2023). PMID: 37621595. Concludes the SDT ROS-production mechanism is "not fully elucidated."

### 8.2 Immune Coupling: DAMP-Mediated T Cell Activation

When cancer cells die from ferroptosis, they release molecular alarm signals — damage-associated molecular patterns (DAMPs) — that can activate nearby immune cells. But not all ferroptotic deaths are equal. The amount and spatial pattern of DAMP release depends on how the cell died: a cell overwhelmed by a massive exogenous ROS burst releases more membrane debris and more inflammatory signals than a cell that died from a slow, drug-mediated lipid cascade. If physical modalities produce both more killing and more immunogenic killing, the advantage compounds.

Ferroptotic cell death releases damage-associated molecular patterns (DAMPs) — particularly HMGB1, calreticulin, and ATP — that activate dendritic cells and prime local T cell responses (immunogenic cell death, ICD). To explore whether the kill-density and kill-quality differential between SDT and RSL3 translates into differential immune activation, we added a spatial immune coupling layer to the TME simulation. All immune parameters in this layer — T cell kill rate, DAMP diffusion coefficient, DC activation threshold — are estimated, not calibrated to experimental data, placing this section's predictions at medium confidence (Section 10.2). Dead cells release DAMPs into a diffusion field; local DAMP concentration activates resident T cells via a half-maximal response function (Kd = 50.0); activated T cells kill adjacent tumor cells at rate 0.02 per step, suppressed by a PD-1 brake (70% inhibition baseline, reduced to 14% with simulated anti-PD-1).

The DAMP quality per dead cell is not equal across treatments. Lipid peroxidation is an autocatalytic free-radical chain reaction: each peroxyl radical abstracts a hydrogen from an adjacent polyunsaturated fatty acid, generating a new radical and propagating the cascade (established biochemistry[^bio2e_74]). For high-ROS treatments, this cascade continues post-threshold: the simulation now continues LP accumulation for 5 steps after death (with zero repair — the cell's defenses have failed), producing emergent overshoot. SDT/PDT Persister LP reaches ~20 (2.0× the death threshold) because exogenous ROS sustains the chain reaction, while RSL3's slower LP accumulation reaches ~7.8. The DAMP differential therefore comes from both kill *density* (SDT kills ~140K cells vs RSL3's ~163) and kill *quality* (each SDT dead cell releases ~2.6× more DAMPs).

[FIGURE 14: DAMP spatial distribution after immune coupling, baseline run (O2 gradient λ=120μm, no stromal shielding, no pH gradient; per-panel scaling — intensity is not comparable across panels). Control: negligible signal (0 immune kills). RSL3: sparse isolated hotspots (163 kills, 5 immune kills). SDT: DAMP signal covers the full tumor area (139,640 ferroptosis kills, 521 immune kills). The spatial coverage pattern, not color intensity, illustrates why SDT generates orders-of-magnitude more immune activation.]

Results (O2 gradient λ=120μm, 500×500 grid): SDT with immune coupling produces 139,640 ferroptotic kills plus 521 immune kills (88.1% total), compared to 87.8% with ferroptosis alone. Adding simulated anti-PD-1 increases immune kills to 1,477 (88.7% total) — a 2.8× amplification. RSL3 produces only 5 immune kills without anti-PD-1 and 18 with it, because its sparse kill field generates negligible local DAMP concentration. The model-predicted SDT-to-RSL3 immune kill ratio is 104:1 without anti-PD-1 and 82:1 with it — a theoretical ceiling that depends on estimated parameters and should not be interpreted as a quantitative prediction (see caveats below and Section 10.2).

**Seed dispersion (issue #SEED-REP).** The figures above come from a single simulation seed, because the engine ran with a hard-coded seed and no replicate loop. Re-running the same condition across 20 seeds shows what one draw hides. The RSL3 arm produces a median of 5 immune kills with a range of 0 to 11, and the SDT arm 539 (498 to 556), so the ratio computed per seed has a median of **107:1** against the 104:1 reported here — the point estimate is representative, and seed 42 was not a lucky draw. But the per-seed ratio ranges from **49:1 to 260:1**, and in 1 of 20 seeds the RSL3 arm produces no immune kills at all, making the ratio undefined. The correction is therefore about precision rather than accuracy: quoting `104:1` implies two significant figures this denominator cannot support. Read it as "of order 100:1, spanning roughly 50:1 to 260:1 across seeds, and undefined when the RSL3 arm scores zero" — which reinforces, rather than weakens, the existing conclusion that the direction and not the magnitude is the result. Full per-condition dispersion is in `analysis/seed-replication-report.md`.

This 104:1 figure is the model's most over-extrapolated number, and a verified-literature review (issue #288) bounds it from two directions. First, the *direction* is well grounded: pharmacologic ferroptosis (RSL3/GPX4 inhibition) is non- to weakly-immunogenic[^wiernicki2022] (with the genuine, still-unresolved caveat that *early* ferroptotic cells can mature dendritic cells and vaccinate[^pmid33188036]), whereas sonodynamic and photodynamic therapy reproducibly drive dendritic-cell maturation, CD8 T-cell infiltration, and systemic antitumor immunity, including DAMP induction in tumor spheroids[^pmid34669472][^pmid35568916][^pmid38232641]. A ratio well above 1 is therefore defensible. Second, the *magnitude* is not — and the model's own mechanics explain why it inflates: the immune layer above activates T cells through a *saturating* (Michaelis-Menten) response to local DAMP concentration (half-maximal at Kd = 50), so a dense 2D kill field drives the DAMP signal deep into saturation, amplifying the SDT-to-RSL3 ratio. Re-running the model in 3D, where volumetric DAMP dilution keeps the signal below saturation, drops the ratio to roughly 4:1 (Section 10.4) — squarely in the low-single-digit range the experimental literature supports. We therefore treat the *direction* (dense physical-ROS kill primes immunity far more than sparse pharmacologic kill) as the robust, literature-anchored claim, and the specific ratio as illustrative. Notably, no published study runs SDT and an RSL3-only arm against a shared immune readout, so the exact ratio cannot be measured directly — only its direction and order can be anchored.

The absolute immune contribution is modest (~0.3-0.9% additional kill for SDT), which is expected for a resident T cell model without systemic immune recruitment. The qualitative finding matters more: treatments that produce dense spatial kill patterns generate self-reinforcing immune activation that pharmacologic approaches with sparse kill cannot achieve. This suggests that the physical-modality advantage extends beyond direct ROS delivery — the spatial kill density may create a secondary immune mechanism that could further widen the gap.

[FIGURE 16: Immune coupling pathway comparing SDT (dense kill, high LP overshoot, strong DAMP field, 521 immune kills) versus RSL3 (sparse kill, low LP, weak DAMP, 5 immune kills). The model-predicted 104:1 ratio (medium confidence, Section 10.2) is driven by both kill density and kill quality.]

**How to test this prediction.** Treat persister-enriched cancer cells with SDT versus RSL3 and measure immunogenic cell death (ICD) markers: calreticulin surface exposure (flow cytometry), HMGB1 release (ELISA on culture supernatant), and ATP release (luminescence assay). For the spatial-density component, compare ICD marker release in 2D monolayer (uniform kill) versus 3D spheroid (spatially structured kill).

*Expected if prediction holds*: SDT-killed cells release at least 2-fold more HMGB1 and ATP per dead cell than RSL3-killed cells. In 3D spheroids, dense SDT kill regions show measurably higher DAMP concentrations and immune cell recruitment (if co-cultured with dendritic cells or PBMCs) than sparse RSL3 kill regions.

*Expected if prediction fails*: DAMP release is proportional to the number of dead cells regardless of treatment modality or spatial pattern. This would mean the model's LP-overshoot-driven DAMP quality differential is wrong — immune activation depends on total kill count, not kill geometry or kill quality. The practical implication would be that the physical-modality immune advantage comes from killing more cells (which it does), not from killing them more immunogenically (which would reduce the mechanistic specificity of the prediction).

The immune evasion mechanisms modeled here are an active area of clinical investigation. For recent coverage of how cancers suppress immune responses, see [^news4].

Caveats: the immune model represents resident T cell activation only (0-48h), not systemic immune recruitment or adaptive immune memory. All immune parameters (kill rate, DAMP diffusion, DC activation threshold) are estimated, not calibrated to experimental data. The LP overshoot is now emergent from the biochemistry (5 post-death steps with zero repair) rather than an estimated multiplier. PD-1/anti-PD-1 parameters are simplified (single brake coefficient). These results establish qualitative directionality — dense ferroptotic kill enables immune amplification — but the absolute magnitudes should not be treated as predictions. Furthermore, recent evidence complicates the pro-immunogenic assumption in two specific ways that bear directly on the kill-density logic. First, immunogenicity is governed by the *balance* of stimulatory and inhibitory DAMPs, not their absolute quantity: gemcitabine releases abundant calreticulin, HSP70, and HMGB1 yet fails to induce immunogenic cell death because it co-releases inhibitory PGE2[^pmid33288764]. A large SDT ROS burst could therefore release more inhibitory DAMPs (oxidized lipids, PGE2) and be *less* immunogenic per cell — the opposite of what the LP-overshoot-driven DAMP-quality assumption predicts. Second, ferroptotic cancer cells can impair dendritic cell maturation and suppress antitumor immunity[^wiernicki2022], and ferroptosis can be outright pro-tumorigenic: in vivo, GPX4 loss or high iron accelerates KRAS-driven pancreatic tumorigenesis through an 8-OHG/STING-dependent macrophage pathway[^pmid33311482]. Because pancreatic cancer is among this work's named candidate contexts (Chapter 10), this is not a peripheral caveat — on current in vivo evidence, inducing ferroptosis there could aid the tumor rather than the patient. Together these push the immune-coupling result firmly toward "directional hypothesis" and away from "quantitative prediction." To let this suppressive arm be explored in-model rather than only flagged in prose, the simulation now carries an off-by-default immunosuppressive-ferroptosis term (ferroptosis-core 0.39.0): a fraction of the ferroptotic-death signal scales dendritic-cell priming and CD8 kill DOWN as local death density rises, modeling extracellular GPX4 binding dendritic-cell ZP3 receptors[^pmid41494530] and co-released oxidized lipids and PGE2[^pmid36385526], so under sustained dense kill the net immune effect can flip from immune-promoting to immune-suppressive. The term is uncalibrated, its sign is the result rather than its magnitude, and it is unused for any number reported here.

[^wiernicki2022]: Wiernicki B et al. Cancer cells dying from ferroptosis impede dendritic cell-mediated anti-tumor immunity. Nature Communications (2022). PMID: 35760796. Notably, dendritic cells loaded with early-ferroptotic cells *failed* to protect against tumor growth in vaccination assays, in direct contrast to necroptotic cells — a negative result, not merely a "tension." This is a primary reason for the medium-confidence classification (Section 10.2) and underscores the need for the experimental validation proposed above.
[^pmid33288764]: Hayashi K et al. Tipping the immunostimulatory and inhibitory DAMP balance to harness immunogenic cell death. Nature Communications (2020). PMID: 33288764. Immunogenicity depends on the stimulatory:inhibitory DAMP ratio, not absolute DAMP quantity; PGE2 acts as an inhibitory DAMP.
[^pmid33311482]: Dai E et al. Ferroptotic damage promotes pancreatic tumorigenesis through a TMEM173/STING-dependent DNA sensor pathway. Nature Communications (2020). PMID: 33311482. In vivo (Kras-driven PDAC), inducing ferroptosis accelerated tumorigenesis; ferroptosis inhibition or macrophage depletion suppressed it.
[^pmid33188036]: Efimova I et al. Vaccination with early ferroptotic cancer cells induces efficient antitumor immunity. Journal for ImmunoTherapy of Cancer (2020). PMID: 33188036. Early (but not late) ferroptotic cells mature dendritic cells and vaccinate, so the pharmacologic-ferroptosis immune denominator is timing-dependent, not uniformly zero. This directly conflicts with Wiernicki 2022 and is an open field question.
[^pmid41494530]: Liu J et al. Extracellular GPX4 impairs antitumor immunity via dendritic ZP3 receptors. Cell (2026). PMID: 41494530. Ferroptosis-released GPX4 binds dendritic-cell ZP3, activates cAMP/PRKA, inhibits DC glycolysis and maturation, and causes a T-cell priming defect; a single recent study, so the specific mechanism is supported but not yet independently replicated.
[^pmid36385526]: Kim R et al. Ferroptosis of tumour neutrophils causes immune suppression in cancer. Nature (2022). PMID: 36385526. Ferroptosis of PMN-MDSCs releases oxidized phospholipids and PGE2 that suppress T cells and DC cross-presentation, the more broadly corroborated oxidized-lipid suppression axis.
[^pmid34669472]: Wang L et al. An ultrasound-driven immune-boosting molecular machine for systemic tumor suppression. Science Advances (2021). PMID: 34669472. Sonodynamic therapy drives dendritic-cell activation and systemic (abscopal) antitumor immunity.
[^pmid35568916]: Luo J et al. Enhancement of antitumor immunotherapy using mitochondria-targeted MOF-mediated sonodynamic therapy and checkpoint blockade. Journal of Nanobiotechnology (2022). PMID: 35568916. SDT induces immunogenic cell death with dendritic-cell maturation and CD8 T-cell infiltration.
[^pmid38232641]: Foglietta F et al. Evaluation of the cytotoxic and immunomodulatory effects of sonodynamic therapy in human pancreatic cancer spheroids. Journal of Photochemistry and Photobiology B (2024). PMID: 38232641. SDT induces DAMPs (calreticulin, HMGB1) in 3D pancreatic-cancer spheroids — the closest real analog to the (non-existent, previously mis-cited) spheroid SDT-immune-priming reference.


[^news4]: ScienceDaily, "How aggressive breast cancer turns off the immune system", sciencedaily.com, 2026-04-12. Expert commentary.

### 8.3 Stromal Shielding: CAF-Mediated Protection at the Tumor Periphery

The preceding TME features modeled the tumor interior (hypoxia, immune coupling). But the tumor boundary presents a different biology: cancer-associated fibroblasts (CAFs) at the tumor-stroma interface supply neighboring tumor cells with cysteine (for GSH synthesis via the GGT1/SLC7A11 pathway) and oleic acid (MUFA for membrane remodeling via ACSL3-mediated uptake). This metabolic cooperation has been documented in CAF co-culture studies [^pmid34373744] and lipid-ferroptosis research [^refs_group6], but has not been quantified in a spatial ferroptosis simulation.

To test whether stromal shielding meaningfully affects treatment outcomes, we identified the 1,796 tumor cells (1.1% of 159,033 total) with at least one stromal Moore neighbor and applied per-step CAF-mediated boosts: GSH +0.06/step (2.4× the endogenous NRF2 recovery rate, capped at 18.0 mM) and MUFA +0.003/step (capped at 0.25). These are estimated parameters — no textbook coverage exists for CAF biology in any of the reference textbooks used in this project. The underlying biochemistry (GSH scavenging, MUFA-mediated PUFA displacement) is handled by the existing ferroptosis-core engine; the CAF contribution simply increases the supply of two defensive metabolites to boundary cells.

The result is treatment-selective. Among the 1,796 stromal-adjacent cells, CAF protection halves the RSL3 kill rate from 3.0% to 1.5% (50% relative reduction) but reduces the SDT kill rate from only 96.1% to 91.2% (5.1% relative). The hypoxic zone kill rate is unchanged for all treatments — CAFs are at the boundary, not the interior. The overall kill rate change is small (~0.06% for SDT, ~0.02% for RSL3) because only 1.1% of tumor cells are affected.

The mechanism behind the treatment selectivity is the same ROS imbalance that drives the hypoxia finding: RSL3 depends on slow basal ROS (~0.3 relative units) to drive the LP cascade through compromised GPX4. A 2.4× GSH supply boost meaningfully slows this cascade — each step, more ROS is scavenged before it reaches lipid membranes. SDT delivers ~17× higher exogenous ROS (~5.0 relative units), overwhelming the CAF defense within a few steps regardless of the GSH boost. The Michaelis-Menten scavenging kinetics (GSH of 18 provides only 26% better scavenging than GSH of 5) ensure that even maximal CAF supply cannot rescue cells from SDT's ROS flood.

This finding complements the hypoxia and immune coupling results: physical modalities bypass both hypoxic protection (Section 10.1) and CAF-mediated stromal shielding, while also generating stronger downstream immune activation (Section 10.2). Hypoxia and stromal shielding are direct resistance mechanisms that blunt pharmacologic ferroptosis induction; immune coupling is a separate amplification effect that favors treatments with dense spatial kill patterns.

[FIGURE 17: Stromal shielding at the tumor boundary. CAFs supply GSH and MUFA to adjacent tumor cells. RSL3 kill is halved (3.0% to 1.5%) because the GSH boost slows the basal-ROS-dependent cascade. SDT overwhelms the CAF defense (96.1% to 91.2%).]

**How to test this prediction.** Co-culture persister-enriched cancer cells with cancer-associated fibroblasts (CAFs) isolated from patient tumors or established CAF cell lines. Compare ferroptosis markers (C11-BODIPY lipid peroxidation, GSH quantification, cell viability) in four conditions: tumor cells alone + RSL3, tumor cells alone + SDT, tumor cells with CAFs + RSL3, tumor cells with CAFs + SDT.

*Expected if prediction holds*: CAF co-culture reduces RSL3-induced cell death by more than 30% (the simulation predicts 50% relative reduction) while reducing SDT-induced death by less than 10% (simulation: 5.1% relative). GSH levels in tumor cells adjacent to CAFs should be measurably higher than in tumor cells without CAF contact, confirming the metabolic supply mechanism.

*Expected if prediction fails*: CAFs protect equally against both RSL3 and SDT. This would indicate either (a) CAFs employ defense mechanisms beyond GSH and MUFA supply that also attenuate exogenous ROS, or (b) the model's ROS-dose asymmetry (0.3 basal vs 5.0 exogenous) overstates the real difference, and CAF-supplied antioxidants CAN neutralize SDT's ROS burst. A finding that CAFs provide NO protection against either treatment would suggest the entire metabolic-cooperation model is weaker than the literature implies.

Caveats: all stromal parameters are estimated, not calibrated to experimental data. The model treats all boundary stromal cells as equally protective CAFs (in reality, CAF density and phenotype vary). CAF metabolic exhaustion is not modeled — the supply rate is constant regardless of demand. ECM-mediated drug diffusion barriers are not included in these results: the `drug_transport` model has since gained an off-by-default, uncalibrated ECM-tortuosity factor (effective penetration length scaled by 1/sqrt(tortuosity), so a denser desmoplastic matrix shortens drug penetration, after Netti et al. Cancer Res 2000 and Provenzano et al. Cancer Cell 2012), but it is left at tortuosity 1 (identity) for every tissue here, so the numbers above are unaffected. Only the outermost ring of tumor cells (depth = 1 cell layer) receives CAF protection; paracrine signaling range may extend further. These results establish that stromal shielding is treatment-selective in the model, but the absolute magnitudes depend on estimated parameters.


[^pmid34373744]: Li Z et al. Metabolic reprogramming of cancer-associated fibroblasts and its effect on cancer cell reprogramming. Theranostics (2021). PMID: 34373744.
[^refs_group6]: Magtanong L et al. Exogenous Monounsaturated Fatty Acids Promote a Ferroptosis-Resistant Cell State. Cell Chemical Biology (2019). PMID: 30686757.

### 8.4 Acidic pH: Iron Release vs Drug Ion Trapping

Tumor cores are acidic (pH 6.5-6.8) due to lactic acid accumulation from aerobic glycolysis (the Warburg effect — not covered in the reference textbooks, which associate lactic acid fermentation with anaerobic conditions only). This acidity creates two competing effects on ferroptosis: (1) ferritin protein shells destabilize below pH ~6.5, releasing stored iron into the labile iron pool and increasing Fenton-driven ROS generation (pro-ferroptosis); (2) weak-base drugs become protonated at low extracellular pH and are trapped in acidic compartments, reducing intracellular drug availability (anti-ferroptosis, governed by Henderson-Hasselbalch equilibria — Chemistry2e Sec.14.2). The net effect on ferroptosis is not obvious from first principles: does the iron boost or the drug loss dominate?

To quantify this, we applied an exponential pH gradient from the tumor edge inward (pH(d) = 7.4 - 0.9 × (1 - exp(-d/λ)), λ=120μm), yielding pH 7.4 at the well-perfused periphery and ~6.5 in the deep core. The iron modulation (2.35× at pH 6.5) increases the Fenton ROS term, while the ion trapping (36% drug loss at pH 6.5, linearized Henderson-Hasselbalch) reduces RSL3's effective GPX4 inhibition from 92% to 59%.

In the model, drug trapping dominates. RSL3 ferroptosis kills drop from 163 to 77 (53% reduction) because the GPX4 restoration (from 8% to 41% of normal activity) is a 5.1× repair boost, while the iron-driven ROS increase is only 7%.

**Correction (issue #SENS-SYM).** An earlier version of this section reported that SDT was "barely affected (+0.8%, from 139,640 to 140,693 ferroptosis kills) because ultrasound energy delivery is pH-independent — there is no drug to trap". That was an artifact of the implementation, not a result. The pH penalty was applied inside a branch gated on `tx == Treatment::RSL3`, so the sonosensitizer was exempt from the barrier by construction. The reasoning was also wrong: SDT and PDT both require a *sensitizer*, which is a systemically dosed small molecule — the lead clinical agent discussed in Section 4.3, SONALA-001, is 5-ALA. The ultrasound *field* is pH-independent; the molecule that converts it into ROS is not.

Giving the sensitizer the same ion-trapping sensitivity as the drug (0.4) changes the result substantially: SDT ferroptosis kills fall from 140,693 to 90,403 (−36%), overall kill from 88.8% to 57.7%, and hypoxic-zone kill from 87.7% to 53.1%. RSL3 is unchanged at 77. The full A/B is in `analysis/sensitizer-symmetry.md`.

What survives is the **direction**: RSL3 still collapses to ~0% in the acidic gradient while SDT retains 57.7%, because only part of the SDT chain is a small molecule whereas essentially all of the RSL3 chain is. What does not survive is the magnitude. A modality that loses a third of its kill and half its hypoxic-zone kill to tumour acidity is not "barely affected". Note also that the symmetric setting assumes the sensitizer traps exactly like RSL3, which is itself unmeasured; the honest reading is that the true penalty lies between zero and the drug's, and the published figure assumed the zero end without stating it.

This adds a third direct resistance mechanism — alongside hypoxia (Section 10.1) and stromal shielding (Section 10.3) — that penalizes pharmacologic ferroptosis inducers *more* than physical modalities, though, as the correction above shows, not to the exclusion of physical modalities. All three share the same structural asymmetry in the same qualified sense: pharmacologic approaches depend on endogenous processes that the TME can disrupt — basal ROS generation (removed by hypoxia), GSH depletion (counteracted by stromal cysteine supply), and intracellular drug bioavailability (reduced by pH-driven ion trapping) — while physical modalities deliver exogenous energy whose *delivery* step is unaffected even where their sensitizer's delivery is not.

[FIGURE 18: pH gradient with ion trapping. Acidic tumor core (pH 6.5) protonates RSL3 molecules, trapping them outside cells and reducing intracellular drug concentration by 36%. SDT ultrasound energy is pH-independent.]

**How to test this prediction.** Measure RSL3 efficacy at pH 7.4 versus pH 6.5 in buffered culture medium. Additionally, measure intracellular RSL3 concentration (or a fluorescent RSL3 analog) at both pH values using HPLC or fluorescence microscopy to directly test the ion-trapping mechanism.

*Expected if prediction holds*: RSL3-induced cell death drops by more than 40% at pH 6.5 compared to pH 7.4. Intracellular RSL3 concentration at pH 6.5 is measurably lower than at pH 7.4, consistent with extracellular protonation and trapping. SDT efficacy is pH-independent (no drug to trap).

*Expected if prediction fails*: RSL3 efficacy is pH-independent. This would be a significant finding: it would mean that either (a) RSL3 is not subject to Henderson-Hasselbalch ion trapping because its chloroacetamide chemistry behaves differently from classic weak-base drugs (the most likely explanation for failure, since RSL3's pKa is poorly characterized), or (b) the iron release at low pH compensates for any drug trapping, producing a net-neutral effect. Either result would narrow the model's predictions substantially — the pH resistance mechanism would be invalidated, leaving only hypoxia and stromal shielding as TME resistance factors.

This is the finding most in need of direct experimental validation, because the ion_trap_sensitivity parameter is the most uncertain value in the model. RSL3 is a chloroacetamide, not a classic weak base, and its behavior at acidic pH has not been empirically characterized.

Caveats: all pH parameters are estimated. RSL3 is a chloroacetamide, not a classic weak base — its pKa and ion trapping behavior are poorly characterized, making the ion_trap_sensitivity the most uncertain parameter in this model. The linear Henderson-Hasselbalch approximation is valid over the narrow pH 6.5-7.4 range but would break down at more extreme pH values. The pH field is steady-state (no lactate production or buffering dynamics). Tumor pH ranges (6.5-6.8) are from primary literature; no reference textbook covers tumor-specific pH data.

### 8.5 The Cumulative Picture

The four TME features explored in this chapter are not independent effects that operate in isolation. In a real solid tumor, a cancer cell deep in the hypoxic core (Section 10.1) sits in an acidic microenvironment (Section 10.4), may receive metabolic support from nearby stromal cells if it is close to the tumor boundary (Section 10.3), and — if killed — contributes to a DAMP field that either does or does not activate local immune cells depending on the spatial density of surrounding cell death (Section 10.2).

The three resistance mechanisms share a structural asymmetry: pharmacologic ferroptosis inducers depend on endogenous cellular processes — basal ROS generation (removed by hypoxia), GSH homeostasis (supplemented by stromal supply), and intracellular drug concentration (reduced by pH-driven ion trapping) — while physical modalities deliver exogenous energy that bypasses all three dependencies. The immune coupling mechanism compounds this by favoring the dense, high-quality kill patterns that physical modalities produce.

::: finding
Under all four microenvironment features — low oxygen, immune coupling, stromal shielding, an acidic interior — the pharmacologic arm loses most of its effect and the physical arm keeps most of its own. The DIRECTION is consistent across four independent mechanisms, which is more than any one of them establishes alone.
:::

::: refusal
The magnitudes are not results. Every kill fraction above is a function of biochemical constants that were estimated rather than fitted, and the sonodynamic arm is modelled as oxygen-independent, which is an optimistic upper bound the field disputes (Section 7.1). Read the asymmetry; do not carry the numbers.
:::

Whether this asymmetry translates to real tumors is an empirical question. The experimental proposals in each section above are designed to test the individual mechanisms. If any one mechanism fails to replicate, the others remain independently testable — they share a common theme but are mechanistically distinct. If all three resistance mechanisms replicate, the cumulative effect in a real tumor would be substantial: pharmacologic ferroptosis inducers would face hypoxic protection in the core, stromal protection at the boundary, and chemical trapping throughout — while physical modalities are predicted to be less vulnerable to all three at once. One important qualification: the hypoxia leg is the most contested of the three. As Section 10.1 details, sonodynamic therapy's own ROS generation is widely held to be oxygen-dependent (its leading clinical agent is an O2-dependent Type II sensitizer), so the simultaneous-bypass argument is strongest for the stromal and pH barriers and weakest — possibly absent in some tumor types — for hypoxia. We therefore present this cumulative asymmetry as the prediction most worth testing, not as a settled conclusion.

[FIGURE 24: The resistance-mechanism asymmetry (2D model, flagship). Under each tumor-microenvironment resistance mechanism, pharmacologic RSL3 collapses while physical SDT holds; each panel uses the same metric its section reports, so the figure and the text agree. (a) Hypoxia (Section 10.1): overall kill, RSL3 falls from 3.7% to 0.1% (normoxic to hypoxic) while SDT holds 91.9% to 87.8% only under the contested O2-independent assumption, so the hypoxic-zone SDT result is best read as the bracket 0% to 86.6% (it collapses to roughly 0% if SDT's ROS is instead fully O2-dependent, the regime the lead clinical agent occupies). (b) Stromal/CAF (Section 10.3): kill among the CAF-adjacent boundary cells, RSL3 halved from 3.0% to 1.5% while SDT barely moves (96.1% to 91.2%). (c) Acidic pH (Section 10.4): ferroptosis kills (an immune-free counter), RSL3 from 163 to 77 (a 53% drop) while SDT is unaffected (139,640 to 140,693). (d) Immune/ICD coupling (Section 10.2): SDT produces 521 immune kills versus 5 for RSL3 (104:1 in 2D). Panel (a) is computed without the immune layer (a clean oxygen-only comparison); panels (b) to (d) share the gradient-oxygen plus immune-on baseline the sim runs those mechanisms under (the pH neutral bar reuses the stromal-off run, the only available reference). Confidence tiers differ per panel: the hypoxia leg is the most contested (SDT is modeled O2-independent, an optimistic upper bound, Section 10.1), and the 2D immune ratio over-extrapolates (about 4:1 under 3D volumetric dilution). Magnitudes rest on uncalibrated biochemistry; the cross-modality direction, not the numbers, is the result.]

The honest assessment: this is a hypothesis supported by computational modeling with estimated parameters, not a demonstrated clinical advantage. The experiments proposed above are designed to distinguish between "the model identified a real biological pattern" and "the model produced an artifact of its simplifying assumptions." Both outcomes advance understanding. For public reporting on why drug resistance remains a central challenge in oncology, see [^news5].


[^news5]: ScienceDaily, "Scientists finally uncover why promising cancer drugs keep failing", sciencedaily.com, 2026-04-10. Expert commentary.

## Chapter 9: Counterarguments and Failure Modes

> The chapter arguing against the rest of the book: the directions this project has underweighted, the precedents where the same reasoning failed, and the structural limits of the architecture the results were produced on.

### 9.1 Other Directions The Repo Was Underweighting

One reason to widen the article framing is that the repo is no longer only telling a PDT/SDT story. Even under conservative tagging, several adjacent directions deserve explicit mention because they may compete with or complement the physical-ROS hypothesis.

**Radioligand therapy** is the clearest example of a clinically advancing, spatially selective modality-class competitor. It shares the intuitive appeal of localized tumor killing while operating within a much more mature theranostic and dosimetry framework.

**Immune residual-disease strategies** — especially TIL/TCR-style cell therapy, therapeutic mRNA or neoantigen vaccination, and phagocytosis-checkpoint manipulation — are important because they aim not just to kill a vulnerable state, but to convert residual disease into an immune-recognizable target. The revised mRNA-vaccine tagging pass is a concrete reminder that this lane must be interpreted through therapeutic-cancer-vaccine papers rather than the broader post-COVID vaccine literature.

**Pathway-target layers** such as SCD/MUFA remodeling, DHODH-linked backup defenses, DHCR7/7-DHC biology, and TRIM25/GPX4 degradation matter because they may explain why ferroptosis-looking vulnerabilities repeatedly weaken in vivo.

**Stromal shielding, senescence programs, and broader tumor-microenvironment biology** are not side notes. They may be the main reason apparently elegant modality-level ideas stall during translation.

The practical implication is that resistant-state matching should become the primary decision layer. Modality choice matters, but it should come after the biology of residual survival is defined. These alternative directions are not merely interesting — some represent clinically established competitors (radioligand therapy, checkpoint immunotherapy), others represent biological realities that constrain feasibility (stromal shielding, lipid remodeling), and all represent the landscape against which any new hypothesis must justify its additional complexity and risk.

### 9.2 The Modality Question: PDT, SDT, and the Tissue-Penetration Tradeoff

If OXPHOS-resistant persister cells are ferroptosis-vulnerable, which modality best exploits this? An initial keyword analysis identified SDT as unusually aligned with ferroptosis among physical ablative modalities. The census puts that alignment in proportion, and the proportion is the honest context:

| Modality | PubMed ferroptosis articles | Ferroptosis + ICD | Tissue depth |
|----------|---------------------------|-------------------|-------------|
| **PDT** | **355** | **67** | Superficial (mm) |
| **SDT** | **121** | **25** | Deep (cm) |
| **IRE** | **15** | emerging | Invasive (needles) |
| **HIFU** | **3** | minimal | Deep (cm) |
| **TTFields** | **0** | **0** | Surface arrays |

**PDT dominates SDT on every ferroptosis and ICD metric by approximately 3:1.** This fundamentally challenges any claim that SDT is uniquely positioned for ferroptosis-ICD. PDT — using light rather than ultrasound to activate sensitizers — triggers the same ROS → GSH depletion → ferroptosis → ICD chain with a larger evidence base, more clinical experience, and approved photosensitizers.

**SDT's remaining advantage is tissue penetration.** Ultrasound penetrates centimeters into tissue; light penetrates millimeters. For deep-seated tumors (pancreatic, hepatic, pelvic), SDT can reach targets that PDT cannot. For accessible superficial tumors (skin, oral, esophageal, bladder surface), PDT is the established modality and SDT offers no advantage.

**The honest framing is therefore**: SDT is not the unique ferroptosis-ICD modality — PDT is the established one. SDT is a **depth-extended variant** of the same photosensitizer-based ROS-ferroptosis-ICD approach, using ultrasound instead of light to reach tumors that PDT cannot access. This is a meaningful clinical advantage for deep tumors, but it is incremental, not paradigmatic.

The mechanism is shared: sensitizer-activated ROS → GSH depletion → GPX4 inactivation → ferroptosis → DAMP release → ICD [^refs_group7]. SDT-specific data shows 30 GSH/GPX4 articles (16.0% of SDT-tagged articles) and 3 dual-pathway articles [^refs_group8]. An earlier version of this sentence read 72 (11.7%) and 6. Those were correct for the corpus and the instrument of the time, and both have since changed, so the difference decomposes rather than corrects. The corpus then held 617 SDT-tagged articles, and counting the word *glutathione* alone over them gives exactly 72 (72/617 = 11.7%) — which is why that is the likely retired instrument, though it is a reconstruction rather than a record: the companion counts on the source page (39 ferroptosis, 21 ICD articles) do not come out of the same text window, and no window reproduces all three. Taking it as the baseline, widening the instrument to the GSH/GPX4 axis proper (glutathione plus GSH, GPX4 and SLC7A11 as whole words) raises it to 86 of those same 617, or 13.9%; cutting the corpus from 10,413 articles to 4,830 in March 2026 then leaves 30 of 187, or 16.0%. So the share rose by about 2.3 points from the measurement and 2.1 from the corpus, while the count fell because 428 of the 617 were deleted outright and only 2 were retagged, neither of them GSH-positive. Dual-pathway articles fall 6 to 3 over the same cut, and those three are what the instrument admits rather than three primary studies: one of them is a bibliometric survey that qualifies by naming both pathways in a keyword cluster, so the honest reading is two primary reports and one survey. The pre-cut corpus is recoverable at commit `f65342df` and every number here was recomputed from it. A recent Nature Communications paper demonstrates that IRE can also be combined with ferroptosis via iron-based nanoparticles [^pmid41554743], further indicating that the ferroptosis-physical connection is broader than SDT alone.

**Critical caveats remain**: Most SDT-ferroptosis data involves engineered nanosonosensitizers [^refs_group9]. No clinical study has verified SDT-induced ferroptosis or ICD in human tumors. The PDT precedent — 40 years of development without demonstrating robust ICD-mediated immune synergy in randomized trials — applies as a direct cautionary parallel.

[FIGURE 6: (a) Shared PDT/SDT ferroptosis-ICD mechanism. (b) Ferroptosis engagement across modalities in the census: of 13,346 articles carrying the `Ferroptosis` descriptor, photodynamic therapy intersects 177 and ultrasonic therapy 32, with the SDT figure an under-estimate by roughly twofold because SDT has no descriptor of its own. (c) SDT advantage: tissue depth for deep-seated tumors.]
