### 9.3 Counterarguments, Precedents, and Reasons This Could Fail

The following counterarguments address whether the physical-ROS hypothesis holds biologically and clinically — whether the real-world biology supports the idea, independent of the computational model that generated it.

**The photodynamic therapy precedent.** PDT is conceptually identical to SDT: physical energy (light vs ultrasound) activates a sensitizer to generate ROS, causing cell death with ICD potential. PDT has been in clinical development for 40+ years and has achieved approval for a few indications (esophageal, skin) but has never demonstrated the "ROS → ICD → immune synergy → durable response" chain in a randomized clinical trial. PDT + immunotherapy combinations remain at the case report level [^pmid41833681]. If this logic worked robustly, PDT would have shown it by now. SDT's claimed advantages over PDT — deeper tissue penetration and potentially superior ferroptosis engagement via engineered nanosonosensitizers — are plausible but incremental, not paradigmatic. The SDT hypothesis must explain why it would succeed where PDT has not demonstrated clear immune synergy. For recent coverage of TTFields clinical progress, including immune-system engagement beyond glioblastoma, see [^news6].

**The ferroptosis vulnerability window may be transient.** Persister cells downregulate FSP1 as part of the mesenchymal/dedifferentiated state [^pmid41481741]. However, persisters are a transitional population: they eventually either die or re-enter proliferation with restored drug resistance. The ferroptosis-sensitive window may last days to weeks. If the real window closes before any follow-up treatment can be administered, the therapeutic opportunity is lost regardless of which modality is used.

**The lipid-remodeling and backup-defense problem.** OXPHOS-resistant cells may co-upregulate NRF2-driven antioxidant defenses, SCD/MUFA remodeling, DHODH-linked backup defenses, DHCR7/7-DHC biology, TRIM25-linked GPX4 degradation dynamics, and other lipid-handling programs that neutralize ferroptosis vulnerability even as the metabolic preconditions accumulate. The pathway-target layer now shows that these escape axes are present in the corpus, but mostly in review-heavy or early mechanistic form. They therefore strengthen the need for caution more than they strengthen any single therapeutic claim.

**The nanosonosensitizer confound.** Most SDT-ferroptosis papers use engineered nanoparticles *designed* to deplete GSH and release iron. If plain SDT with conventional sonosensitizers doesn't trigger ferroptosis, the insight is about nanoparticle design, not SDT as a modality — and the regulatory path becomes device+drug+nanoparticle, the most complex category.

**The ICD-to-immunity chain has repeatedly underperformed clinically.** Pexa-Vec (oncolytic virus ICD, failed Phase 3) [^pmid31413923], CheckMate-498 (immunotherapy in GBM, failed) [^pmid35419607], KEYNOTE-361 (ICI+chemo in urothelial, failed) [^pmid34051178], and BIND-014 (nanoparticle, failed Phase 2) [^pmid29978216] all demonstrate that preclinical rationale frequently fails in translation. SDT has no reason to be exempt from this base rate. For an example of how combination strategies are being tested against aggressive cancers in current trials, see [^news7].

**Narrow applicability.** The physical-ROS hypothesis applies only to accessible or image-localizable solid tumors, immunocompetent settings where ICD could matter, tumors that actually enter a ferroptosis-prone residual state, and a specific post-therapy time window. Hematologic malignancies, diffuse metastatic disease, and many non-localizable residual states fall outside its plausible range.

**Evolutionary limitation.** This hypothesis addresses one resistance transition. After SDT kills ferroptosis-sensitive persisters, survivors are selected for ferroptosis resistance (GPX4 upregulation, NRF2 activation, FSP1 restoration). The approach adds one step to the therapeutic arms race but does not end it. Durable eradication would require the ICD-triggered immune response to eliminate residual heterogeneous disease — an outcome that has not been demonstrated for any ICD-based strategy in randomized trials.

**Prior art.** Collateral sensitivity[^pluchino2012], adaptive therapy[^gatenby2009], and synthetic lethality are established frameworks for exploiting resistance tradeoffs. What our analysis adds is the specific SDT connection, not the general principle.

[^pluchino2012]: Pluchino KM et al. Collateral sensitivity as a strategy against cancer multidrug resistance. Drug Resistance Updates (2012).

### 9.4 Structural Limitations of the Simulation Architecture

The counterarguments above address whether the physical-ROS hypothesis holds biologically and clinically. A separate question is whether the computational model that generated the hypothesis is structurally adequate to support it. Section 5.4 listed what the model omits. Here we assess how those omissions affect the specific predictions in Chapters 7 and 8.

**No normal tissue, and a selectivity assumption that is never stated as one.** Every modality in this book is scored on kill and none on harm: no results table in Chapters 5 to 7 carries a normal-tissue column, and no prediction in the preregistration is scored on one. The engine does hold a non-tumour phenotype, and the headline table of Section 5.3 does not report it — a reasonable omission, since that phenotype models cancer-associated fibroblasts and including it would not have answered the question either. That absence encodes a premise — that ferroptosis induction is selective because normal cells resist it — which is nowhere argued because it is nowhere stated. The model cannot check the premise, because it contains no normal-tissue compartment. The `Stromal` phenotype is sometimes read as one and is not — it represents cancer-associated fibroblasts, which are tumour-resident and parameterised for shielding, so the CAF-versus-tumour contrast of Section 10.3 is a statement about the tumour microenvironment and not a therapeutic index.

The premise is also not a background fact. Across the census, 575 of the 13,346 articles carrying the `Ferroptosis` descriptor also carry an organ-toxicity descriptor — acute kidney injury 297, drug-induced liver injury 115, cardiotoxicity 110 — and the two most-named agents in that set are doxorubicin and cisplatin, whose dose-limiting toxicities are cardiac and renal. On a hand-read sample of 40, 38 are papers in which ferroptosis is the mechanism by which normal tissue is damaged and blocking it protects (`analysis/census-normal-tissue.md`).

That literature does not refute the premise, and it is important not to over-read it here. Cisplatin injures a kidney through several mechanisms at once, and ferroptosis being among them says nothing directly about what a targeted GPX4 inhibitor would do at a therapeutic dose. What it does establish is that normal kidney, liver, heart and neural tissue are capable of dying this way, so selectivity is a quantitative question about therapeutic window rather than a qualitative property of the mechanism. Stating it as an assumption is the honest position available now; resolving it needs a normal-tissue phenotype with a parameter set traceable to measurement, and the matched normal-versus-tumour dose-response that would calibrate one does not exist in any dataset this project holds.

**Steady-state environmental fields.** The oxygen, pH, and drug concentration fields in the TME simulations are computed once based on distance from the tumor edge. They do not change as cells consume oxygen, produce lactate, or metabolize drugs. In real tumors, blood flow fluctuates on timescales of minutes to hours, and cells in the transition zone between oxygenated and hypoxic regions experience intermittent hypoxia — cycling between states rather than remaining permanently in one. Intermittent hypoxia is documented to produce biological responses distinct from chronic hypoxia, including increased angiogenesis, enhanced metastatic potential, and treatment resistance through mechanisms that differ from those modeled here. To test whether the hypoxia finding survives under dynamic conditions, we implemented a square-wave O2 cycling mode that alternates the penetration length between 80μm (deep hypoxia) and 150μm (normoxic) every 30 steps (period = 60 steps, 50% duty cycle). Under cycling, RSL3 overall kill remains at 0.1% — essentially identical to steady-state hypoxia (0.1% at λ=120μm). The transition zone shows a modest increase (0.2% cycling vs 0.1% steady-state), confirming that reoxygenation windows allow limited RSL3-driven LP initiation in cells that would otherwise remain quiescent under chronic hypoxia. Normoxic-zone and overall kill rates are unchanged between conditions. SDT overall kill is unchanged at 87.8%. The directional claim — RSL3 collapses under hypoxia while SDT maintains — holds under cycling conditions. The pH result (Section 10.4) faces the same steady-state limitation but has not been tested with dynamic pH (deferred). Overall direction: the resistance mechanisms identified here persist under dynamic O2, their spatial boundaries would be less sharp than the smooth gradients imply, but the pharmacologic-vs-physical asymmetry is robust.

**Simplified immune layer.** The immune coupling model (Section 10.2) represents resident T cell activation over a 0-48 hour window. It does not model systemic immune recruitment from blood or lymph nodes, adaptive immune memory formation, immune editing of tumor populations, or regulatory immune suppression. The 104:1 SDT-to-RSL3 immune kill ratio is a local phenomenon driven by local DAMP concentration. Whether these local patterns translate to systemic anti-tumor immunity — the outcome that would matter clinically — depends on processes the model does not represent. Two opposing outcomes are plausible. If local DAMP patterns recruit circulating immune effectors, the SDT advantage would compound. If systemic immune regulation dominates — through regulatory T cells, myeloid-derived suppressor cells, or checkpoint upregulation — the local response would be dampened regardless of DAMP density, and the 104:1 ratio would overstate the real differential. Given the clinical trial failure rate documented in Section 10.3 — where ICD-to-immunity chains have repeatedly underperformed across multiple modalities — the dampening scenario is the more probable default. The amplification scenario would require SDT to produce qualitatively different immune activation than the modalities that have already failed, a claim for which no evidence currently exists. The experimental test proposed in Section 10.2 (ICD marker measurement with immune cell co-culture) is designed to provide the first empirical constraint, but resolving the systemic question will require in vivo models that the current computational framework cannot replace.

**No adaptive evolution (in the analyses presented here).** The simulation runs 180 steps within a single treatment window, and the 2D analyses in Chapters 7 and 8 do not model mutation, clonal selection, or population dynamics across treatment cycles. Section 10.3 already notes the biological reality: survivors of ferroptosis-inducing treatment will be selected for ferroptosis resistance (GPX4 upregulation, NRF2 activation, FSP1 restoration). The computational limitation, for the results in this book, is that they do not quantify how fast this erosion occurs or whether repeated treatment cycles produce accumulating benefit or diminishing returns. (The library has since grown uncalibrated `clonal` and `persister` layers that begin to model exactly this — see "The 3D simulation suite" below — but they are not calibrated and are not used for any number in this book.) The persister layer in particular now carries an off-by-default reversible-to-irreversible epigenetic-locking transition (ferroptosis-core 0.40.0): sustained continuous exposure can move part of the persister pool into a non-reverting locked state, while intermittent dosing keeps the sustained-exposure tracker below threshold and does not lock, a first model handle on the intermittent-versus-continuous dosing question (FSP1/HDAC-suppressed persister durability, Higuchi 2026[^pmid41481741]); it is uncalibrated and unused for any number here. The treatment advantages shown in Chapters 7 and 8 — hypoxic resistance bypass, spatial kill density, dual-pathway synergy — are snapshots of what happens during one treatment cycle. Whether those per-cycle advantages translate to durable clinical benefit depends on the rate of evolutionary escape relative to the rate of immune clearance, a question that requires population-dynamics modeling across weeks to months. Such modeling does not yet exist for the ferroptosis context, and building it is a separate research challenge.

**Estimated parameters and the quantitative-versus-directional distinction.** Many model parameters — CAF supply rates, DAMP diffusion coefficients, T cell kill rates, FSP1 repair kinetics, ion trapping sensitivity — are estimated from indirect evidence rather than calibrated to direct experimental measurements. The sensitivity analysis in Chapter 5 tested robustness to ±50% perturbation on 11 rate constants and found that the qualitative result (Persister cells are more sensitive than Glycolytic cells under SDT) held in 22 of 22 conditions, indicating directional robustness. But directional robustness is not quantitative accuracy. The specific numbers reported throughout this book — 1.99× Bliss synergy (Section 10.3), 53% pH-driven RSL3 kill reduction (Section 10.4), 104:1 immune kill ratio (Section 10.2), 50% stromal protection of RSL3 (Section 10.3) — depend on parameter values that could change substantially with better data. Different FSP1 inhibitor potency would change the synergy score. Different RSL3 pKa would change the pH finding entirely — Section 10.4 explicitly flags this as the most uncertain parameter in the model. The distinction the reader should draw: directional findings (dual-pathway depletion outperforms single-pathway; physical ROS is less vulnerable than pharmacologic induction to the stromal and pH barriers — the hypoxia leg is genuinely contested, see Section 10.1; dense kill patterns generate more immune activation than sparse patterns, subject to the inhibitory-DAMP caveat in Section 10.2) are more robust to parameter variation than the specific magnitudes and are the predictions most worth testing. Specific magnitudes are not robust and should be treated as order-of-magnitude estimates, not precise forecasts. Each experiment proposed in Chapters 7 and 8 is designed to provide calibration data the model currently lacks — turning estimated parameters into measured ones, and testing whether the directional predictions survive contact with real biology.

**The 3D simulation suite, and why its numbers are not in this book.** Everything above describes the 2D and single-cell engine that produced the results in Chapters 7 and 8. Since those analyses were frozen, the codebase has grown a second track — the `sim-tme-3d` capstone and more than a dozen additional "realism layers" in the `ferroptosis-core` library: explicit 3D vasculature, a patient-scale slab geometry, radial spheroid biochemistry, clonal heterogeneity with spatial expansion, drug-tolerant persisters (including an OXPHOS-ROS mitochondrial-suppression escape axis that an HDAC inhibitor reverses), T cell exhaustion, a Treg/MDSC suppressor field, a multi-checkpoint immune brake, an IFN-γ to System Xc- ferroptosis-sensitization coupling, a DC/effector-cell ferroptosis-susceptibility coupling in which a ferroptotic microenvironment kills the effector dendritic cells themselves (PD-L1-gated) and so can suppress its own immune amplification, an ALOX lipoxygenase-isoform-specific peroxidation rate with a medium-chain-fatty-acid sensitization axis, a dietary-PUFA / lipid-droplet (DGAT) buffer axis (exogenous polyunsaturated fatty acids raise ferroptosis once the saturable triglyceride-storage sink is exceeded, and DGAT inhibition makes it emerge sooner, potentiated by tumor acidosis), an ACSL4-status biomarker stratification (the tumor-intrinsic PUFA-incorporation gate), an ESCRT-III membrane-repair brake that acts on the death-execution step rather than the lipid substrate (a cell whose membrane has already been peroxidized can be resealed for a finite repair budget, delaying or blocking death), a POR/CYB5R1 enzymatic oxygen-coupled hydrogen-peroxide source that feeds the Fenton reaction (and, because its yield falls with oxygen, does not amplify the deep-core artifact), a PROM2 exosomal labile-iron efflux resistance axis (a Prominin-2-high tumor exports ferritin-bound iron in secreted vesicles, starving the Fenton reaction, the opposite sign to ferritinophagy), a copper-ionophore / cuproptosis crosstalk axis (a copper ionophore such as elesclomol depletes glutathione and GPX4 to enable ferroptosis and cuproptosis together, gated by ATP7B copper efflux), a 7-dehydrocholesterol sterol radical-trapping defense (a distal cholesterol-pathway antioxidant that, when DHCR7 is low, accumulates and confers resistance), a vitamin-K / VKORC1L1 radical-trapping defense (a sixth GPX4-independent antioxidant axis, a p53 target that the anticoagulant warfarin can inhibit to drive ferroptosis), an explicit Type I versus Type II sonodynamic decomposition (the oxygen-dependence knob already separates the oxygen-independent radical arm that keeps killing the anoxic core from the oxygen-dependent singlet-oxygen arm that collapses there), a cell-cell contact-resistance layer, a propagating ferroptotic trigger-wave front (ferroptosis spreading cell to cell as a constant-speed lipid-peroxide front, the spatial complement of the single-cell switch), and a bidirectional therapy-induced-senescence program, among others. We mention them here for honesty, not to claim them: almost all of these layers use uncalibrated placeholder parameters, and the three 3D calibration targets are self-consistency checks against the model's own predictions, not independent measurements (the full per-layer accounting is in `simulations/calibration/CALIBRATION_STATUS.md`, a concise intended-use and limitations summary in the repository `MODEL_CARD.md`, and the 3D self-consistency results in `simulations/calibration/3d_validation_report.md`). They are mechanistic scaffolding, useful for exploring the shape of an effect, and they were deliberately kept out of every quantitative claim in this book. One layer has since moved off that uncalibrated footing: the single-cell RSL3 kill switch is now anchored to an independent in-vitro dataset (CTRPv2 GPX4-inhibitor dose-response), fit on ML162 and held-out validated on ML210 (#330), the first independent held-out calibration of any layer; it anchors the in-vitro single-cell switch only, the in-vivo and spatial layers remain as described here, and it therefore changes none of the numbers in this book (full detail in `analysis/calibration/kill-switch-calibration.md`). Two further layers have since been partially anchored to independent published data, neither altering this book's numbers: the spheroid module's radial zone geometry was checked against the Browning 2021 size-resolved confocal dataset and matches it only for spheroid radius at or above 300 micrometers (#333, geometry only, the per-zone biochemical gradient strengths remaining uncalibrated; `analysis/calibration/spheroid-structure-validation.md`), and a follow-up added an opt-in size-aware zone-threshold variant that ramps the zones with spheroid size so small spheroids are correctly mostly proliferating with no necrotic core (cutting the mean boundary error on the Browning bins from 0.338 to 0.025 while reducing to the validated fixed structure at large radius), leaving the per-zone biochemistry uncalibrated. The size-resolved kill leg was then characterized with a simulation sweep (RSL3 kill across spheroid sizes): with the fixed thresholds the supply gradient reproduces the universal bigger-spheroids-resist-more direction (kill falls roughly nineteen-fold over 144 to 540 micrometers radius, within the measured cytotoxic size-resistance range), while the size-aware structure yields a falsifiable prediction specific to a persister-targeting ferroptosis inducer, that small, all-proliferating spheroids resist RSL3 (no persister target) and vulnerability emerges only as the persister core appears, the opposite size-direction to generic cytotoxics. The kill magnitude stays uncalibrated and a ferroptosis-inducer multi-size kill experiment, not yet available, is what would validate it, and the tumor-PK layer was anchored to the only public ferroptosis-specific in-vivo pharmacokinetics with a paired plasma and tumor concentration-time course, imidazole ketone erastin (a system xc- inducer), which gives a measured tissue-to-plasma partition near 0.90 against the presets' estimated 0.15 to 0.50, a plasma-to-tumor delay of about two hours, and a closed-form proof that the disposition is multi-compartment, with a sorafenib human population-PK forward check (Cavg,ss near 4.1 mg/L at 400 mg twice daily, inside the clinical 3 to 5 mg/L range); the per-tumor presets are not recalibrated, since no public per-tumor pharmacokinetics exist for a ferroptosis inducer, and the RSL3/ML tool-compound PK gap is flagged rather than fabricated (#334; `analysis/calibration/pk-calibration.md`). Two consequences follow. First, several limitations stated above as things the model "cannot do" are now things the library can do but has not calibrated: the clonal and persister layers do model adaptive evolution and acquired tolerance; the suppressor and multi-checkpoint layers do model Treg/MDSC and checkpoint dampening; an IFN-γ coupling layer now closes the immune-amplification loop in the reverse direction — Chapter 8's coupling runs ferroptotic death to DAMP to CD8 priming, and this layer adds the molecular return arm, in which activated-CD8 IFN-γ represses tumor System Xc- (SLC7A11/SLC3A2) and so sensitizes those cells back to ferroptosis (Wang et al., Nature 2019, PMID 31043744), an off-by-default and uncalibrated handle on the positive-feedback loop between immune killing and ferroptosis (#443); an explicit-vasculature module can replace the edge-distance oxygen proxy; a `contact` layer models the ACSL4 (PUFA) and TFRC (iron-import) downregulation that Section 5.4 lists as "not represented," driven by E-cadherin/NF2-YAP cell-cell contact (Wu et al., Nature 2019, PMID 31341276, the foundational density-dependent ferroptosis-resistance result); and a `senescence` program represents therapy-induced senescence as a ferroptosis state whose direction is genuinely contested and which the model therefore makes bidirectional rather than forcing one sign: senescent cells resist upstream (cystine/iron) triggers but are a senolytic target under the direct GPX4 inhibition this work's RSL3 actually applies (Masaldan et al., Redox Biol 2018, PMID 28888202; D'Ambrosio et al., Nature Cell Biology 2026, PMID 42032311), so the simpler "senescence resists ferroptosis" assumption is incomplete here. That senescence layer has since been extended with a CDK4/6-inhibitor-primed senescence-to-ferroptosis combination (palbociclib or ribociclib induces senescence over a multi-day window, then a ferroptosis inducer challenges the primed population), which yields a falsifiable prediction that longer priming recruits a larger senolytic-vulnerable fraction and the vulnerability window closes as senescence resolves; the induction and recovery kinetics are uncalibrated placeholders grounded only in the one-to-three-week senescence timescale, so the direction is the result, not the day-precise magnitudes (#447). A companion `acsl4` layer turns ACSL4 expression status into a stratification biomarker: ACSL4 is the enzyme that ligates the polyunsaturated fatty acids ferroptosis requires into membranes, so an ACSL4-high tumor is ferroptosis-sensitive while an ACSL4-low or ACSL4-negative tumor (for instance some hepatocellular-carcinoma and acute-myeloid-leukemia subtypes) is intrinsically refractory through a collapsed-substrate mechanism distinct from the GPX4, glutathione, and FSP1 defenses (Doll et al., Nature Chemical Biology 2017, PMID 27842070). The model reproduces that ordering (ACSL4-high cells die more under a fixed ferroptosis-inducer dose, ACSL4-negative cells die less), which gives the falsifiable clinical-stratification prediction that ferroptosis inducers will benefit ACSL4-high cancers and spare ACSL4-negative ones. The status-to-sensitivity mapping is an uncalibrated linear placeholder, and the two pieces that would calibrate it, a per-cancer-type ACSL4 expression and deletion prevalence survey and a cell-line meta-analysis pairing ACSL4 status with measured ferroptosis-inducer dose-response, are data that the repository does not yet carry, so they are flagged as collaborator tasks rather than fabricated (#444). That prevalence leg has since been partly closed (#462): a fetch of all thirty-two TCGA PanCancer Atlas studies from the public cBioPortal interface gives a real per-cancer-type ACSL4 distribution, with the model's status scale now bridged to a real expression z-score. A follow-up (#616) then found that only half of that prevalence result is about ACSL4 at all, and the distinction matters enough to state plainly. The shallower cut (the eleven-to-nineteen-percent low-ACSL4 fraction this originally reported as the population prior for the refractory-leaning tail) is not gene-specific: because the z-scores are standardized within each study, that fraction is about sixteen percent for any gene with a roughly normal expression distribution, and six control genes measured the same way all return between about fourteen and sixteen percent. It describes how the cut-point partitions a cohort, not how many tumors are ACSL4-refractory. The deeper cut survives the same control: about three percent of tumors per cancer type sit below two standard deviations, against the two-and-a-quarter percent a normal distribution gives, and ACSL4 exceeds that expectation in twenty-five of the thirty-two cancer types while five of the six controls sit at or below it. A left tail heavier than a normal is what a discrete low-expression subpopulation looks like and standardization cannot manufacture one, so the deep-cut figure is genuine evidence about ACSL4 -- and it is the cut the layer needs, since the ACSL4-negative state sits at two standard deviations below the mean. The honest surprise is that bulk tumor messenger-RNA does not show liver and myeloid cancers as ACSL4-low the way the cell-line literature implies (liver cancer in fact has the highest bulk ACSL4), so the refractory ACSL4-low state is a protein-level or subtype property that bulk expression medians miss, and the magnitude of the ACSL4-to-sensitivity mapping still awaits a cell-line dose-response meta-analysis. A further layer arrived together with its calibration, the closest the suite has come to tying a spatial prediction to a measurement: a `trigger_wave` module models ferroptosis spreading as a constant-speed lipid-peroxide front (a bistable reaction-diffusion wave, the spatial complement of the single-cell switch), and it is anchored to the Co 2024 measured ferroptotic trigger-wave speeds (5.52 micrometers per minute at baseline, iron-tunable to 2.33 under iron chelation and 9.40 under iron loading; PMID 38987590). The model reproduces the measured iron-dose response shape, front speed proportional to the square root of labile iron, but the per-condition iron levels are back-solved from the measured speeds rather than measured, so the shape is reproduced by construction (a back-solve consistency check, not an independent fit of the iron-dose response; #528). The genuinely anchored part is the baseline one-point calibration of the product of diffusion and reaction rate to the measured 5.52 micrometers per minute, so the front-speed magnitude is anchored only at baseline and in order of magnitude, not derived from first principles (#482). The honest statement is not that the model is structurally incapable, but that the analyses in this book do not use those layers and the layers are not yet calibrated. A reaction-diffusion solver added to the 3D suite (`reaction_diffusion`, issue #343; the full comparison is in `analysis/reaction-diffusion-benchmark.md`) confirms that this nearest-vessel proxy is optimistic in sparse networks: in the λ ≲ vessel-spacing regime (the regime of the 2D analyses here, λ = 120 µm at the 20 µm grid resolution), a true reaction-diffusion solve over the same explicit-vessel geometry yields lower supply fields than the exponential proxy, driven chiefly by 3-D point-source geometry (a real vessel's field is the Yukawa form exp(-r/λ)/r) versus the planar single-source field the proxy assumes — a single isolated vessel reads 0.85 (proxy) versus 0.31 (RD), and across a sparse network the proxy calls the hypoxic fraction 62% while the RD solve calls it 99%. These numbers are directional and representation-dependent (each vessel is a single Dirichlet voxel at uncalibrated radius and grid resolution), and the sign reverses at λ ≫ spacing (where superposition between vessels enriches the field above the proxy), so the proxy-is-optimistic direction holds only in the λ ≲ spacing regime. Two caveats keep this from re-touching the book's numbers. First, the finding bears on an explicit-vessel network, not on the edge-distance O2 proxy that Section 10.1 actually uses or the single-vessel-wall Krogh limit that Section 10.1 reports — that 1-D planar-source limit is the one case the benchmark finds the proxy gets exactly right. Second, every layer involved is uncalibrated. The honest reading is therefore directional only: a sparse tumor solved with explicit-vessel reaction-diffusion would likely look more hypoxic than the edge-distance and Krogh proxies imply, which is one more reason the hypoxia-resistance and drug-penetration findings in Chapters 7 and 8 are presented as directional rather than as calibrated magnitudes. Second, where a result has been re-run in 3D it can shift by an order of magnitude: the 104:1 SDT-to-RSL3 immune ratio (Section 10.2) falls to roughly 4:1 in the 3D geometry, plausibly from volumetric DAMP dilution and finite-size effects, which is exactly why that ratio is presented as a directional ceiling rather than a number.

**How much literature stands behind these mechanisms, and why the obvious reading of that number is wrong.** Calibration is one question; whether anyone else has reported the mechanism at all is a separate one, and until recently this work had not asked it. Each layer above rests on one or two papers the author read and cited by PMID, which cannot distinguish a mechanism the field has replicated from one asserted once. The census can supply the denominator: for a hand-chosen gene or chemical pair standing in for each mechanism, count how many distinct cancer articles assert a relation between them. Twenty such pairs have been checked, and eleven are corroborated by at least one independent article — but that headline should be read with two corrections. Three of the twenty are not realism layers at all but core-engine mechanisms described in Chapter 5 (the GPX4 and FSP1 parallel defences, System Xc- cystine supply, and erastin as a drug input), and they are three of the four best-corroborated rows, so quoting the overall fraction as a statement about the layers borrows the core engine's evidence. Restricted to the layers proper the count is eight of seventeen. And seventeen is not a survey either: these are author-written claims with author-chosen proxy pairs covering a subset of the library's modules, so the fraction describes that curated list and a different choice of proxy pairs would give a different one.

The obvious reading of the uncorroborated rows — that those mechanisms are ones the literature does not support — is wrong for most of them, and measurably so. A pair can only be asserted if both of its entities are written about, and across these twenty claims the less-studied entity's number of distinct relation partners runs from nine to nearly three thousand. That exposure tracks corroboration closely (Spearman rank correlation 0.86, rising to 0.88 when every pair containing GPX4 is dropped, so it is not an artifact of one heavily studied gene), and every corroborated claim has a less-studied entity of at least a hundred partners while seven of the nine zeros fall below that line. Those seven are therefore statements about what has been studied, not about what is true — the census cannot speak to them either way, which is different from its having considered the mechanism and declined it. For the remaining two nothing is established in either direction, and it is worth saying why rather than quietly counting them with the seven: the line separating them is the minimum of an eleven-point sample rather than a detection limit — forty-six per cent of all asserted pairs in the census sit below it — and running the same procedure on a full-text mention-overlap measure inverts the ranking and promotes two different modules instead. So the defensible conclusion is narrow: a mechanism's lack of corpus corroboration is mostly a measure of how little its entities have been examined, and for a minority of cases it is not currently a measure of anything. The full accounting, including the proxy pair chosen for each claim and the ways that choice is itself load-bearing, is in `analysis/atlas-module-support.md`.

These structural limitations define the boundaries of what the computational work can claim. Whether the directional findings survive contact with real biology is the open question that this book cannot answer computationally. The experiments proposed in Chapters 7 and 8 would begin to answer it — but none have been performed, and each could fail for reasons the model does not anticipate.

# Part IV: What Should Happen Next

> Predictions that cannot be tested are opinions with equations attached. This part turns the preceding chapters into experiments somebody could actually run, ordered by what they cost and by how much they would settle, and states in advance what result would count as a failure.

## Chapter 10: Research Roadmap

> From predictions to experiments: what to test first, what each test costs, what each would settle, and what the field learns if the whole programme turns out to be wrong.

### 10.1 From Predictions to Experiments

Chapter 9 catalogued the reasons this hypothesis could fail — biologically, clinically, and computationally. This chapter proposes a research program designed to test whether it does. The program is organized not by which finding seems most exciting but by which predictions rest on the best-characterized parameters, because the failure of a high-confidence prediction is more informative than the failure of one that depends on uncertain estimates.

Chapters 7 and 8 each proposed specific experiments attached to individual simulation findings — seven in total, each with a hypothesis, model system, and expected outcomes if the prediction holds or fails. This chapter does not restate those proposals. Instead, it organizes them into a phased program: which experiments to run first and why, which experiments gate which follow-ups, what new experiments are needed beyond the individual-finding tests, and what the field learns if the entire program fails.

This chapter is for research prioritization, not treatment advice. The experimental suggestions identify what to test and why — they are not clinical protocols, statistical designs, or grant-ready specifications. A researcher using this chapter as a starting point would still need to optimize assay conditions, determine sample sizes, and adapt the proposals to their specific model systems and expertise.

### 10.2 Confidence Tiers

::: numbers
Three tiers run through this book, and nearly everything in it sits in the second or third. HIGH: the quantity follows from measured physics or from a published dataset the repository fits and re-checks on every change. MEDIUM: the direction is robust across the parameter ranges tested, the magnitude is not. LOW: a mechanism is represented, and the number attached to it is a placeholder that says what would happen if the mechanism behaved as described. A figure with no tier stated is an error, not a HIGH.
:::

Not all simulation predictions are equally trustworthy. Some rest on well-characterized biochemistry and measured physical constants; others depend on parameters that are estimated from indirect evidence or, in one case, entirely uncharacterized. Assigning a confidence tier to each prediction makes the program's risk profile explicit and helps a researcher decide where to invest effort first.

The tiers are defined by parameter provenance:

- **High confidence**: The prediction depends primarily on textbook biochemistry or well-measured physical constants. Sensitivity analysis confirms directional robustness. Failure of a high-confidence prediction would require a fundamental assumption to be wrong.
- **Medium confidence**: The underlying mechanism is published, but key magnitude parameters are estimated from indirect evidence rather than measured in the model system. The prediction's direction is robust but its quantitative magnitude could shift substantially with better data.
- **Low confidence**: The prediction depends on parameters that are explicitly flagged as the model's most uncertain. The prediction could change qualitatively — not just quantitatively — if the parameter is wrong.

**High confidence:**

*Hypoxia resistance (Section 10.1).* The model predicts RSL3 kill collapses more than 30-fold in hypoxic conditions while SDT maintains greater than 85% kill. The RSL3 collapse rests on established biochemistry: oxygen is the terminal electron acceptor for mitochondrial ROS generation (textbook), so a GPX4 inhibitor that depends on basal ROS has little to act on in hypoxia. That direction is the high-confidence part. The SDT half is the contested leg: SDT's own ROS generation is widely held to be O2-dependent (its leading clinical agent is a Type II, O2-dependent sensitizer), so SDT fully maintaining kill under hypoxia is an optimistic upper bound, not a settled result (Section 10.1; Figure 24 tiers this panel as contested). The high-confidence claim is therefore the endogenous-vs-exogenous ROS asymmetry direction (the most fundamental claim in the simulation work), not the magnitude of SDT's hypoxic advantage.

*Spatial penetration differential (Section 10.1).* PDT kill collapses beyond 3 mm depth; SDT maintains efficacy through 1 cm. This is physics, not biology: light attenuation at 630 nm and acoustic attenuation at 1 MHz are well-measured constants. If this prediction fails in a spheroid experiment, the attenuation model is wrong — which would be surprising for well-characterized physics but would be immediately informative.

*Dual-pathway synergy (Section 10.3).* RSL3 + FSP1 inhibitor produces approximately 2x Bliss synergy through dual-pathway depletion. GPX4 and FSP1 independence is published.[^bersuker2019] The directional prediction (dual > single) is robust to parameter variation. If the combination index shows additivity rather than synergy, FSP1's role as an independent parallel repair pathway needs reassessment — a finding with implications well beyond this project.

**Medium confidence:**

*Vulnerability window timing (Section 10.2).* RSL3 sensitivity window closes within 3 days; exogenous-ROS sensitivity persists for weeks. The defense-pathway recovery half-lives (GPX4: 3 days, FSP1: 7 days) are literature-estimated, not measured in the specific persister model system the simulation uses. If recovery is much faster than estimated (hours instead of days), the therapeutic window may be too narrow for any follow-up treatment to exploit.

*Immune coupling (Section 10.2).* SDT generates 104x more immune kills than RSL3 through spatial DAMP density. The LP overshoot that drives DAMP quality is emergent from the biochemistry, but all immune parameters (T cell kill rate, DAMP diffusion, DC activation threshold) are estimated. Chapter 9.4 flags that systemic immune dynamics could amplify or suppress the local advantage — and the clinical trial failure rate (Section 10.3) suggests suppression is the more probable default.

*Stromal shielding (Section 10.3).* CAF-mediated GSH and MUFA supply halves RSL3 kill at the tumor boundary while barely affecting SDT. The CAF supply mechanism is published, but the specific supply rates used in the model have no textbook source. The overall tumor impact is small (1.1% of cells affected) even if the per-cell effect is large.

**Low confidence:**

*pH ion trapping (Section 10.4).* Acidic pH reduces RSL3 kill by 53% through Henderson-Hasselbalch-driven drug trapping. RSL3 is a chloroacetamide, not a classic weak base — its pKa is uncharacterized, and the ion trapping sensitivity is the most uncertain parameter in the entire model. This prediction is the most likely to fail experimentally. If it does fail, the pH resistance mechanism is eliminated, leaving only hypoxia and stromal shielding as TME resistance factors. If it succeeds, it identifies a previously unrecognized pharmacokinetic limitation of chloroacetamide-class compounds in acidic tumors.

### 10.3 Program Structure

The experimental program has a gated structure designed to maximize information per experiment and minimize wasted effort.

**Phase 1 — High-confidence in-vitro tests.** Run the hypoxia (7.1), spatial penetration (6.1), and dual-pathway synergy (6.3) experiments first. These test the cleanest predictions using the best-characterized parameters. If any of these fails, the specific model assumption that broke should be identified before proceeding — it may require revising the simulation architecture before the remaining predictions are worth testing.

**Phase 2 — Medium-confidence refinements.** If Phase 1 confirms the directional findings, run the vulnerability window (6.2), immune coupling (7.2), and stromal shielding (7.3) experiments. These refine the picture: how long is the treatment window? Does kill density actually translate to immune activation? Does stromal protection matter at the population level? Phase 2 experiments depend on the directional findings from Phase 1 holding — if drugs and physical modalities show similar hypoxic sensitivity, the TME-resistance framing needs revision before investing in refinement experiments.

**Phase 3 — Low-confidence test.** Run the pH ion-trapping experiment (7.4) last among the in-vitro studies. This is the most likely to fail and the result either validates or eliminates one of the three proposed TME resistance mechanisms. Running it after Phases 1-2 means the broader program isn't derailed by a single uncertain parameter.

**Running in parallel — Broader comparison (Section 10.4).** This is analytical, not experimental. It runs alongside the wet-lab program and asks whether the entire approach justifies its complexity relative to existing alternatives.

**Gated — In-vivo integration (Section 10.5).** This is the definitive test and the most expensive. It should only proceed after the in-vitro program confirms the directional predictions. Running it prematurely would risk investing substantial resources in an approach whose foundational assumptions haven't been verified.

[FIGURE 19: Decision framework for modality selection based on tumor localizability, depth, ferroptosis-prone residual state, and immunocompetence. Branches to SDT range, PDT range, alternative approaches, or pathway-target strategies depending on clinical context.]

### 10.4 Broader Comparison: Does This Hypothesis Justify Its Complexity?

This is not an experiment. It is an analytical evaluation of whether the physical-ROS ferroptosis approach is worth pursuing relative to existing alternatives — a question the simulation cannot answer because it doesn't model the alternatives.

The comparison should evaluate each approach on shared axes: regulatory maturity (how far from clinical use?), evidence depth (clinical trials vs preclinical vs computational?), delivery burden (how complex is the treatment logistics?), and applicability to residual disease (does it target the residual-cell biology this project focuses on?). The comparison is necessarily imperfect — these approaches operate on fundamentally different biological principles (ionizing radiation, immune activation, ROS-mediated lipid damage) and a shared-axis evaluation cannot capture all dimensions of their respective strengths and weaknesses.

The most informative comparison is against radioligand therapy. Radioligand uses targeted radiation delivered by molecules that seek specific receptors on cancer cells — it shares the intuitive appeal of spatially selective tumor killing while operating within a more mature regulatory framework (FDA approvals for prostate and neuroendocrine cancers, established dosimetry pipelines, and a large clinical trial base). If radioligand therapy addresses the same clinical need — treating localized residual disease — with a simpler development path, the physical-ROS hypothesis becomes academically interesting but clinically unnecessary.

The comparison is not winner-take-all. Different approaches may be optimal for different clinical contexts: radioligand for tumors with targetable receptors, checkpoint combinations for immunologically hot tumors, and physical-ROS modalities for deep-seated tumors without targetable receptors in immunocompetent settings where ICD might matter. The value of the comparison is not in crowning a winner but in defining the niche — if any — where the physical-ROS approach adds something the alternatives cannot.

### 10.5 In-Vivo Integration

The in-vitro experiments in Phases 1-3 test individual predictions in controlled conditions. The in-vivo integration experiment tests whether those predictions survive the full complexity of a living system — where hypoxia, pH gradients, stromal shielding, immune activation, drug penetration, and evolutionary dynamics all operate simultaneously.

The experiment requires an immunocompetent model (so the immune coupling prediction can be tested) with residual disease generated by initial treatment and recovery. The critical design constraint: the study must include honest comparators. Any in-vivo test that includes only SDT and untreated controls cannot determine whether SDT offers an advantage over its closest precedent (PDT) or over pharmacologic ferroptosis approaches. At minimum, the study needs physical-modality arms, pharmacologic arms, and combination arms to disentangle the modality effect from the drug effect and the combination effect.

This experiment is expensive, time-consuming, and tests multiple predictions simultaneously. Running it without in-vitro confirmation from Phases 1-2 would be premature because failure would be uninterpretable — you would not know which prediction failed or why. Running it after in-vitro confirmation means failure would identify the specific gap between controlled conditions and biological reality, which is itself valuable.

If the predictions hold: SDT plus anti-PD-1 outperforms other arms in deep tumors with hypoxic cores, with higher immune infiltrate and ferroptosis markers at depth than PDT or RSL3 arms. If the predictions fail: the TME resistance asymmetry does not translate in vivo, which could mean that (a) the in-vitro-to-in-vivo gap is larger than the spatial simulation predicts, (b) the immune coupling does not scale from local DAMP signaling to systemic anti-tumor immunity, or (c) evolutionary escape operates faster than the one-cycle snapshot suggests.

### 10.6 What the Field Learns If the Program Fails

The most important question a research program can answer is not "does it work?" but "what do we learn either way?" A program that only produces knowledge when it succeeds is advocacy, not science. Each phase of this program has defined failure criteria that advance understanding regardless of outcome.

If the high-confidence experiments fail — if RSL3 and SDT show similar sensitivity to hypoxia, or if light and sound attenuate similarly in tissue, or if dual-pathway depletion produces additive rather than synergistic kill — the most fundamental predictions of the model are wrong. The ROS-source asymmetry (endogenous versus exogenous) is smaller than the simulation estimates, or it doesn't exist at the treatment-relevant scale. The field learns that the biochemical rationale for the physical-modality advantage does not hold — the depth advantage of ultrasound over light is already established physics and does not require this project to demonstrate.

If the medium-confidence experiments fail — if the vulnerability window is much shorter than predicted, if DAMP release does not correlate with treatment modality, or if CAF co-culture provides no detectable protection — the directional findings from Phase 1 may still hold, but the quantitative magnitudes are wrong and the model's parameter estimates need substantial revision. The field learns what calibration data is needed for the next generation of ferroptosis models.

If the entire program fails — if every in-vitro experiment shows no modality-dependent differential — the directional findings were computational artifacts produced by estimated parameters that do not reflect real biology. The biochemical model of ferroptosis sensitivity that this project built would be empirically invalidated, and the experimental predictions derived from it would be retracted as modeling artifacts rather than biological patterns.

The operational side of this roadmap, candidate publication venues for the corpus synthesis and the simulation methods (kept as separate contributions), a preregistration draft of this chapter's falsifiable predictions, registered independently of the calibration legs rather than before them (four of those legs predate the preregistration, and the property that matters is that none of them sets a prediction), and a collaborator-facing brief of the cheapest validating experiments, is maintained outside the book in `analysis/contribution-plan-2026.md`.

---

## Chapter 11: The Broader Landscape

> The work that is going on regardless of this project, and an honest placement of these results inside it.

### 11.1 Conclusion

This article began as a modality-centered question and ends in a broader place.

**What the census analysis shows.** Cancer-therapy literatures need to be compared on resistant-state relevance, study-design maturity, delivery constraints, and known analytic artifacts before any one mechanism is over-privileged. Study design across the census is read from NLM publication types rather than from a detector this project wrote, which removes the recall question entirely at the cost of a coarser label: 3.6% of the census carries a clinical-trial publication type, 6.4% of the records carrying any design-informative label at all, and 44.5% carry none.

Two claims this section previously drew from the retrieved corpus do not survive the census and are corrected here rather than left standing in a later chapter, which is where a superseded figure usually outlives its retraction. The tissue layer reported physical-modality research as overwhelmingly concentrated in epithelial and neuroectodermal contexts; measured against NLM's own site assignment, the haematologic depletion holds and is strengthened (leukaemia 0.30 against the pharmacological class's 1.30), while the neuroectodermal concentration does not — brain and CNS sits at 0.92 for the physical class and 0.91 for the pharmacological, indistinguishable. And the designed-combination classifier's 19.7% is not carried forward at all: it needed full text, and Section 3.13 shows the co-occurrence rate it was measured against is a property of the labelling instrument rather than of the field.

**What the simulations show.** The mechanistic simulations produce six findings that, if validated experimentally, would have translational implications. Read every magnitude below against `analysis/identifiability-report.md`, which prices them: with eleven free rate constants, six non-identifiable from the kill rate, and **zero** of these outputs conditioned on data in the regime that produces them, **none is point-estimable**. The directions are the results; the numbers are order-of-magnitude.

1. *Drug combination synergy through dual-pathway depletion.* In the simulation, RSL3 (GPX4 inhibitor) + FSP1 inhibitor produces ~1.99× Bliss synergy — 95% prior-predictive ~1.0× to 5.2×, median ~1.35×, so the supra-additive direction is robust while the magnitude is not point-estimable (`analysis/identifiability-report.md`) — because depleting both parallel repair pathways drops total antioxidant defense below the autocatalytic LP propagation threshold. This suggests combination strategies targeting GPX4 AND FSP1 simultaneously, rather than either alone.

2. *Within ferroptosis-targeted therapy, the tumor microenvironment affects pharmacologic and physical approaches differently (three resistance mechanisms and one amplification effect, all conditional on the model's estimated parameters and the narrowest, most context-specific of the three findings).* (a) Hypoxia (model-predicted): RSL3 kill collapses from 3.7% to 0.1% under oxygen gradients while SDT is far less affected (87.8%) — but only under the contested O2-independent assumption, so the SDT hypoxic-zone advantage brackets 0% to 86.6%, collapsing to roughly 0% if SDT's ROS is instead fully O2-dependent, the regime the lead clinical agent SONALA-001 occupies. (b) Stromal shielding (model-predicted): CAF-mediated GSH/MUFA supply halves RSL3's peripheral kill rate (3.0% to 1.5%); SDT barely affected (96.1% to 91.2%). (c) Acidic pH (model-predicted): ion trapping halves RSL3 ferroptosis kills (163 to 77, 53% reduction) as drug bioavailability drops 36% at pH 6.5; SDT gains only 0.8% (iron boost negligible vs exogenous ROS). These three resistance mechanisms share a structural asymmetry: pharmacologic inducers depend on slow endogenous processes that the TME disrupts, while physical modalities deliver exogenous energy that is predicted to be less affected. As Section 10.1 cautions, sonodynamic therapy's own oxygen-dependence makes the hypoxia component (a) the least certain of the three, and in some tumor types (e.g. clear-cell renal carcinoma) hypoxia signaling can instead sensitize cells to GPX4 inhibition. (d) Immune coupling is a downstream amplification that, in the model, also differs between the two: SDT generates 104× more immune kills than RSL3 (521 vs 5, medium confidence, Section 10.2; this ratio falls to ~4:1 when re-run in 3D, Section 10.4, and is challenged by inhibitory-DAMP-balance evidence, Section 10.2), amplified 2.8× by anti-PD-1 (to 1,477).

3. *Tissue-specific drug penetration creates a substantial in-vitro-to-in-vivo translation gap.* Even at the blood vessel wall, tissue-specific vascular permeability reduces model-predicted RSL3-like drug kill from 40% (2D culture) to 12.1% (well-vascularized epithelial) to 2.6% (poorly-vascularized pancreatic) to 1.8% (CNS/BBB). It is the ORDERING that is parameter-robust — it held in 300 of 300 prior-predictive draws — not these magnitudes, which carry intervals spanning nearly the whole range at every tissue (well-vascularized median 23%, ~[0%, 93%]; CNS/BBB median 4%, ~[0%, 77%]). The gap is even larger once the penetration gradient into the tissue is considered.

**What remains conditional.** Within the broader frame, the simulation neither validates nor disproves the hypothesis that physical ROS-generating modalities may be useful for locally targetable, ferroptosis-prone residual states; instead it bounds the question, identifying specific conditions under which the model predicts it could hold (hypoxic cores, deep tissue, limited drug penetration) and specific conditions under which it would not (hematologic malignancies, tumors without ferroptosis-prone states, settings where the ICD-to-immunity chain has historically underperformed). PDT is the established precedent, SDT is the depth-extended variant, and all are constrained by nanosensitizer dependence, uncertain in vivo ferroptosis durability, and the historical failure rate of preclinical immune logic in trials.

**The deeper lesson is methodological.** Gap claims can be artifacts. Mechanism counts are not maturity. Patient-study signal is not phase evidence. A compelling literature bridge is still only the start of a research program. But the simulation work adds something that literature analysis alone cannot: it identifies which biological conditions would need to be true for the hypothesis to work, and which would falsify it. The RSL3+FSP1i synergy, the hypoxia differential, and the tissue penetration gap are all testable in the lab.

**Openness and reproducibility.** All code, data, and analysis pipelines are openly available under the MIT license. The ferroptosis-core simulation engine is published as a standalone Rust library with Python bindings, so any researcher can reproduce, challenge, or extend the computational work. We believe this kind of radical openness — where the tools are as available as the conclusions — is essential for research that aims to help people rather than to be right about one hypothesis.

The right next step is therefore not to defend one favored modality, but to run the comparative experiments and cross-lane evaluations that could falsify it quickly.

### 11.2 What This Engine Cannot Be Asked, and What Was Done About It

A reader arriving at a repository addressed to the whole cancer community and finding a simulation engine that models one mechanism is entitled to an explanation. This section is it, and it is a measurement rather than a defence.

**The criticism is correct and it is quantified.** `analysis/scope-audit.md` counts what this project's committed work is about: 20 analyses whose subject is ferroptosis or the physical-ROS modalities against 1 for any other therapy — the label matters, because the column counts that union and not ferroptosis biology alone, and 8 of 20 preregistered predictions concerning ferroptosis or the physical-ROS modalities. `analysis/modality-coverage.md` asks the sharper question — not what the work is about, but what the engine can be ASKED — and answers it against this project's own mechanism taxonomy. Of the sixteen mechanisms that carry a discriminative MeSH descriptor, thirteen had **no engine representation at all** when that document was written, carrying 90,019 census articles between them. That column is now empty: every one of the sixteen has some representation. **Which moves the honest question rather than answering it**, and the report now says so. Presence is not applicability: 8 of the sixteen can be APPLIED as a treatment — a variant a run can select — and the other 8 are modifiers, real code that is only ever a coefficient on something else. That split read 2 and 14 until the tier rule itself was checked: it decided by matching a mechanism's keyword against the SPELLING of a Rust enum variant, and `oncolytic` cannot match inside `OncolyticVirus`, so four arms this chapter describes were reported as unselectable while `sim-modality-panel` was selecting them — a detector that answered its own question by spelling. An engine where every mechanism is present and half are applicable answers a narrower set of questions than the first count suggests. Figure 25 shows the shape of that.

**Volume is not importance, and the table is not a ranking.** Descriptor breadth varies enormously across mechanisms, so ordering by census count is substantially an ordering of how broad each descriptor is — the refusal `analysis/census-mechanism-profile.md` makes, inherited here in full. The actionable content is binary: which questions the engine cannot be asked. That is a fact about this repository's code, not about the field.

**Immunotherapy was the sharpest case, and the reason was structural rather than an oversight.** The engine carries a four-axis checkpoint panel (PD-1, CTLA-4, LAG-3, TIM-3), a Treg/MDSC suppressor field, T-cell exhaustion, dendritic-cell subsets and interferon-γ coupling — machinery a reader would reasonably call an immune model. None of it could be applied. Every activation path was gated on ferroptotic death: the well-mixed cascade multiplies the count of dead cells, and the spatial model's dendritic-cell activation reads a DAMP field written as lipid peroxidation at death. Checkpoint blockade enters only through a brake term, so anti-PD-1 multiplied a quantity that was structurally zero, at every setting. Blockade could be observed as a coefficient on ferroptosis and never given as a treatment.

**Fourteen arms have since been added, and the mechanisms turned out not to be independent.**

*Ionizing radiation* is the modality this manuscript's tissue-access argument has always lacked a control for. Chapter 7 contrasts light dying in millimetres with ultrasound reaching centimetres, and both are modalities this thesis is about; radiation is the physical modality whose reach is not the limiting factor at all. Modelled as two channels rather than one, because its dominant lethal lesion is the DNA double-strand break and not membrane lipid peroxidation: a linear-quadratic survival model that does not pass through the ferroptosis state, plus radiation-induced ferroptosis through the existing exogenous-ROS path, with oxygen entering both through the same measured Alper–Howard-Flanders hyperbola the sonodynamic work already validated. Run at 2 Gy in the two-dimensional spatial model it kills 45.4% at the surface and 43.7% at 9.4 mm — flat, against photodynamic therapy's collapse from 92.5% to 0.6% over the same distance.

*Synthetic lethality* existed as an arm only because radiation did. PARP inhibitors block base-excision repair of the single-strand breaks ionizing radiation produces, so before the engine had a DNA-damage channel there was nowhere for them to act. It is also the first layer in this project whose free parameter published data genuinely pins: the sensitizer enhancement ratio is dose-dependent under the linear-quadratic model when only the single-hit term moves, so one parameter must satisfy the published 1.2–1.7 band across the surviving fractions it samples (2.24 to 9.38 Gy) at once, and only a fifth of the scanned range does.

*Checkpoint blockade* needed one thing — antigen presented without ferroptotic death, which is how real tumours present it and why these drugs work as monotherapies. It is off by default and nothing is fit to the published anti-PD-1 response rates yet; what changed is that the question is reachable.

*Redirected T-cell effectors* — CAR-T and bispecific engagers — arrived together, because in this engine they differ in how the effector arrives and not in what it does. Both bypass dendritic-cell priming, which is the point of the modality: antigen presentation is exactly what they do not wait for. They separate on persistence, which is the clinical difference rather than a modelling convenience — a CAR-T population is transferred once and expands, while a bispecific redirects resident cells only while the drug is present. The checkpoint brake still applies to both, and that is a claim rather than a convenience: CAR-T cells express PD-1 and are suppressed in solid tumours, which is the documented reason the 70–94% complete-remission band in B-cell leukaemia has not transferred. A model exempting them would predict solid-tumour efficacy the literature does not report.

*The phagocytosis checkpoint* is the same shape as the T-cell brake and deliberately so: CD47 on the tumour binds SIRPα on macrophages and suppresses engulfment, so a blocking antibody releases a brake rather than adding an effect of its own. What differs is the effector — macrophages engulf rather than lyse — so it is counted separately.

*Nanocarrier delivery* belongs in transport rather than in the treatment enum, because carriers do not kill differently, they arrive differently. The model reproduces the field's own tension without being told to: encapsulation protects the payload and slows metabolism, but a hundred-nanometre carrier diffuses an order of magnitude more slowly than a small molecule, and the net effect on penetration depth is negative. Better accumulation, worse penetration.

*Oncolytic viruses* arrive as an infection and end as an immunotherapy, which is why they compose with machinery already here rather than needing their own. The virus lyses tumour cells and that lysis is immunogenic, so the lysed cells feed the same immunogenic-cell-death chain ferroptotic deaths feed — the modality's systemic effect, responses at lesions that were never injected, is reachable only through that shared chain. What the model does not have is most of the virology: replication kinetics, antiviral immunity clearing the virus before it spreads, and the interferon response that decides which tumours are permissive. Infection extent is an input, so the model can say what follows if a virus spreads that far, not whether it will.

*Metabolic targeting* draws a distinction the engine could not previously make. Everything the nutrient layer models is a cell starved because of where it is, past the diffusion limit; a glycolysis inhibitor starves it because of a drug, everywhere at once. The two compose — a rim cell under 2-deoxyglucose is nutrient-replete and metabolically blocked — and the mechanism is one the layer already documents, since glucose flux feeds the pentose-phosphate pathway that supplies the NADPH regenerating glutathione for GPX4. The layer's own caveat applies with equal force and is worth repeating rather than inheriting silently: energy stress also activates AMPK, which can inhibit ferroptosis, and glutaminolysis is required for some of it. The direction is the claim; the magnitude is a placeholder.

*Antibody-drug conjugates* are the opposite trade-off in the same machinery, and the taxonomy's highest trial share. An ADC buys selectivity and pays for it in reach: a full antibody diffuses an order of magnitude more slowly than a small molecule and binds the first antigen it meets, so perivascular cells consume it before it reaches the core. Modelled with the antibody diffusion coefficient this project already documents and a high uptake rate standing for that binding-site barrier, penetration falls to 7 µm against a free drug's 100 — and relaxing the uptake recovers most of it, which is how the explanation is checked against the numbers rather than asserted beside them.

**And they now run.** `sim-modality-panel` puts every applicable arm against the identical tumour from the identical seed, so a difference between two rows is the arm and nothing else. Nine treatment arms and an untreated baseline, 7 distinct routes to death, and only 3 of the arms through the ferroptosis engine — a reader opening this repository before this work would have found one route and four arms. The panel is not a ranking of therapies and says so on every surface a reader can reach: each row carries its own calibration tier, and all but radiation's DNA channel are placeholders.

The sharpest row is about delivery. `AntibodyDrugConjugate` kills 1.84% against sonodynamic therapy's 87.24%, both driven by the same exogenous-ROS constant through the same engine. What differs is that the payload arrives on a 150 kDa antibody whose binding-site barrier holds its penetration to about seven micrometres. This manuscript has argued since Chapter 7 that delivery dominates the in-vitro-to-in-vivo gap; that row is the first place in the repository where one exogenous-ROS setting runs through two delivery routes and the transport factor is the only difference between them. **And the comparison is closer to arithmetic than to a discovery, which is worth saying rather than letting the number carry more than it can.** The two arms differ in the transport factor and in nothing else: the ADC arm is the SDT exogenous-ROS constant multiplied by the concentration at each cell's distance from the vessel. So the ratio measures what this model's transport layer does, not what an antibody does in tissue. It also does NOT run RSL3's pharmacology — `biochem::exo_ros` applies the GPX4-inhibition branch only to `Treatment::RSL3` — and an earlier version of this paragraph said the two share the payload's pharmacology exactly, which was false against the code. The honest same-payload comparator sits in the same table and points the other way: the free drug carrying the actual RSL3 payload kills 0.00% at this phenotype, LESS than the antibody-delivered arm, because the glycolytic state is where a GPX4 inhibitor has almost nothing to act on.

**And they have been fitted, where fitting means anything.** Every arm carries a named published target, which is what the layer-freeze policy requires; `analysis/modality-calibration.md` is where each is fitted to it and where the result is reported honestly. Of 7 arms, 4 are admissible, 1 unconstrained, 1 inadmissible and 1 has no fittable target at all. The width of the admissible window is printed beside every one — a fit that admits most of the search range has been given a target that cannot discriminate, and calling it calibrated would be the same error as reporting a p-value without an effect size.

Those four outcomes are deliberately not collapsed. An unconstrained row is a fact about the target rather than the model: the thermal-ablation threshold is consistent with the published necrosis temperature across almost the whole searched range, which means the two published numbers agree and neither pins the parameter. A row with no target at all is the outcome such a page is most tempted to hide, and the temptation is specific — inventing a number that a flexible form then satisfies looks exactly like calibration and constrains nothing. The bystander mechanism is anchored on both arms of its own published comparison, which is what let the layer land, and the corpus gives no escape fraction to fit. The page says so.

**The most valuable row is the one that failed.** Checkpoint blockade is inadmissible, and the diagnosis is a genuine identifiability result rather than a shortfall: with no ferroptotic death the cascade collapses to a product, so the published response band constrains antigen presentation multiplied by kill rate and neither factor is identifiable from it alone. Even at full antigen presentation the arm reaches 10.3% against a 20% floor, and the presentation fraction that would close the gap is 1.94 — impossible for a quantity bounded by one. That is the same finding Chapter 9's identifiability analysis makes about the ferroptosis engine's rate constants, arriving independently in the immune layer, and it is worth more than the arms that fitted: a flexible form hitting its target is weak evidence, while a form that cannot is reporting something nobody put in by hand.

Two of the targets are clinical, and no amount of fitting makes an objective response and a lattice kill fraction the same quantity. A partial response is a thirty-percent diameter reduction — roughly two-thirds by volume — and a responding patient is not a dead tumour. Each clinical row carries that mapping explicitly, and the CAR-T row carries the caveat its own source attaches: the same sentence that reports seventy to ninety-four percent complete remission in leukaemia records that the result has not transferred to solid tumours and that a third to a half of responses were not durable. A fit to the headline band describes the indication these therapies were approved for, not the setting this engine simulates.

**What this does not amount to.** Nine arms is not parity with the ferroptosis work, and the honest accounting is that no mechanism remains absent, but 8 of sixteen remain inapplicable — chemotherapy and radiotherapy are not even in the taxonomy, and Section 3.13's untagged-partner analysis shows what happens to a modality the taxonomy cannot name. Most of the new arms are still uncalibrated placeholders whose kill fractions are functions of the parameters they were given; the calibration status of each is recorded per row in `simulations/calibration/CALIBRATION_STATUS.md` rather than summarised optimistically here. The claim is narrow: the engine's blind spots are now measured rather than assumed, the measurement is regenerated and guarded so it cannot flatter the engine as arms land, and the order of what to build next is derived from it.

### 11.3 Where the Field Is Going

The cancer research landscape in 2026 is defined by several structural trends that provide context for where this work sits and where it might become irrelevant.

**Immunotherapy remains the dominant paradigm, but resistance is the dominant problem.** Checkpoint inhibitors have transformed outcomes for some cancers — melanoma, non-small-cell lung cancer, renal cell carcinoma — and their use continues to expand. But the core challenge has shifted from "does immunotherapy work?" to "why doesn't it work for most patients?" Primary resistance (tumors that never respond), acquired resistance (tumors that respond and then escape), and the immunosuppressive tumor microenvironment are now the central obstacles. The same TME features modeled in Chapter 8 — hypoxia, stromal shielding, acidic pH — are recognized as barriers to immunotherapy effectiveness, not just to ferroptosis induction.

**Engineered cell therapies face the solid-tumor wall.** CAR-T cells have been transformative in B-cell leukemias and lymphomas, but extending their success to solid tumors remains the field's greatest challenge. The tumor microenvironment suppresses, exhausts, and excludes T cells attempting to infiltrate solid tumors. Every advance in CAR-T for solid tumors is, at root, an advance in overcoming TME suppression — the same biological landscape this project models from a different angle.

**Radioligand therapy is a clinically mature example of spatially selective killing, well ahead of any physical-ROS hypothesis.** Lutetium-177 PSMA-617 (Pluvicto) is FDA-approved for metastatic castration-resistant prostate cancer. Lutetium-177 dotatate (Lutathera) is approved for gastroenteropancreatic neuroendocrine tumors. The theranostic pipeline, using imaging to identify targetable tumors and then treating with the same targeting molecule carrying a radioactive payload, is far more clinically mature than anything in the ferroptosis-SDT space. For tumors with targetable receptors, radioligand therapy may already deliver what the physical-ROS hypothesis only proposes: spatially selective killing with systemic delivery. This is a reminder that the conditional hypothesis tested here sits within a landscape where other spatially selective approaches are already in the clinic.

**AI and computational biology are accelerating hypothesis generation.** Machine learning is transforming drug target identification, compound screening, and biomarker discovery. This project is one instance of a broader trend: using computational tools to map research landscapes and identify gaps that individual researchers miss. The specific tools (Monte Carlo biochemical simulation, cross-literature corpus analysis) are distinctive, but the underlying approach — computational hypothesis generation followed by experimental validation — is becoming standard practice. For recent clinical reporting on focused ultrasound applications advancing in adjacent fields, see [^news8].

**mRNA cancer vaccines are advancing toward personalized treatment.** Individualized neoantigen vaccines that train the immune system to recognize mutations specific to each patient's tumor are in late-stage clinical trials. This approach addresses the immune-recognition problem directly rather than relying on immunogenic cell death to trigger an immune response. If personalized mRNA vaccines prove effective at eliminating residual disease, the ICD component of the physical-ROS hypothesis becomes less important — though the direct cytotoxic advantage of physical modalities in hypoxic tumors would remain.


[^news8]: ScienceDaily, "Focused ultrasound halts growth of debilitating brain lesions", sciencedaily.com, 2025-05-15. Context.

### 11.4 Where This Work Fits — and Where It Doesn't

This project contributes a computational framework for comparing therapeutic approaches across the boundaries that normally separate research communities. Its value lies in the questions it raises and the comparisons it surfaces, not in clinical claims.

**Where it fits.** The hypothesis generation is most relevant for locally targetable, deep-seated solid tumors with evidence of ferroptosis-prone residual states — specifically, tumors where pharmacologic drug delivery faces TME barriers (hypoxia, stromal shielding, acidic pH) that the simulation predicts physical modalities can bypass. Pancreatic, hepatic, and pelvic tumors in immunocompetent patients during a post-treatment vulnerability window are the narrowest and most defensible application context.

**Where it does not fit.** Hematologic malignancies (no spatial targeting needed). Diffuse metastatic disease (no localizable target for focused ultrasound or light). Tumors without ferroptosis-prone residual states (no vulnerability to exploit). Immunocompromised patients (ICD-to-immunity chain cannot operate). Settings where the vulnerability window has closed (persister cells have already restored defenses). This is not a general theory of cancer treatment. It is a conditional prediction about a specific clinical context.

**The honest framing.** This work identifies questions worth asking, not answers worth implementing. Every simulation finding is a hypothesis, not a demonstrated clinical advantage. The experimental program in Chapter 10 is designed to determine which — if any — of these hypotheses survive contact with real biology. If none do, the contribution is the computational framework and the cross-literature methodology, which remain useful for other hypotheses and other research communities.

### 11.5 Competing Hypotheses Worth Taking Seriously

Intellectual honesty requires acknowledging that the ferroptosis-persister-physical-ROS hypothesis could be rendered irrelevant by developments in adjacent fields, independent of whether its specific predictions hold.

**The ferroptosis-persister connection might be a niche phenomenon.** The evidence that persister cells are ferroptosis-sensitive comes from specific model systems (primarily EGFR-mutant lung cancer persisters). Whether this sensitivity generalizes across cancer types, treatment histories, and genetic backgrounds is unknown. If it doesn't generalize, the hypothesis applies to a narrow population rather than the broad residual-disease problem described in Chapter 1.

**Radioligand therapy might render the spatial-selectivity argument unnecessary.** For tumors with targetable surface receptors, radioligand delivers spatially selective killing through systemic injection — no focused ultrasound or light source required. If the receptor-targeting approach expands to cover more tumor types, the physical-modality advantage narrows to the subset of tumors without targetable receptors.
